X^{A}[f]\ :=\ f^{\prime}(p\,\mathrm{exp}(tA))(t{=}0)\ .

(2)

Definition 3.3.1
For a given principal
G
-bundle
(P,M,\pi)
one defines the
vertical subspace
at
p\in P
, denoted by
V_{p}P
, as follows

V_{p}P\ :=\ \{X\in T_{p}P\penalty\ |\penalty\ \pi_{*}(X)\ =\ 0\}

(3)

The
horizontal subspace

H_{p}P
arises as the orthogonal complement of
V_{p}P
in the tangent space
T_{p}P
:

T_{p}P\ =\ V_{p}P\oplus H_{p}P\ .

(4)

Remark 3.3.2
It can be shown that

X^{A}_{p}\in V_{p}P\quad\forall\quad p\in P\ .

(5)

This means that the above map
i
induces an isomorphism
i_{p}
between
T_{e}G
and
V_{p}P
.

Definition 3.3.2
A
connection
on a principle
G
-bundle
(P,M,\pi)
is a smooth assignment of
H_{p}P
to each point
p\in P
such that

1.

T_{p}P\ =\ V_{p}P\oplus H_{p}P
,

2.

(r_{g})_{*}(H_{p}P)\ =\ H_{pg}P
(see Remark 2.3.7) and

3.

every
X_{p}\in T_{p}P
has a unique decomposition according to (a) as follows

X_{p}\ =\ ver(X_{p})+hor(X_{p})\ ,

(6)

where
ver(X_{p})\in V_{p}P
and
hor(X_{p})\in H_{p}P
.

Remark 3.3.3
A more technically convenient way to deal with connections is by associating them with a Lie-algebra valued one-form
\omega
in the following way

\omega_{p}(X)\ =\ i_{p}^{-1}(ver(X))\ ,

(7)

which, in turn, imposes following conditions on
\omega
:

1.

\omega_{p}(X^{A})=A
for all
p\in P
and
A\in L(G)
,

2.

(r_{g})^{*}\omega=\mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}\omega
, i.e.
(r_{g}^{*}\omega)_{p}(X)=\mathrm{Ad}_{g{-1}}(\omega_{p}(X))
for all
X\in T_{p}P
and

3.

X\in H_{p}P
iff
\omega_{p}(X)=0
.

Definition 3.3.3
For a local trivializing section
\sigma:U\subset M\to P
of a principal
G
-bundle
P
, its local
gauge
fields arise from the following local representative of a Lie-algebra valued one-form
\omega
:

\omega^{U}\ :=\ \sigma^{*}\omega\ .

(8)

We label the
1
-form components of such local gauge fields in Yang–Mills theory as

A_{\mu}\equiv(\omega^{U})_{\mu}\ ,

(9)

and in general relativity as

\Gamma_{\mu}\equiv(\omega^{U})_{\mu}\ .

(10)

Theorem 3.3.1
An explicit form of the local gauge field
\omega^{U}
for the local trivialization
\varphi:\pi^{-1}(U)\to U\times G
arising from
\sigma
with
(\alpha,\beta)\in T_{(x,g)}(U\times G)\cong T_{x}U\oplus T_{g}G
is given by

(\varphi^{*}\omega)_{(x,g)}\ =\ \mathrm{Ad}_{g^{-1}}(\omega^{U}_{x}(\alpha))+\langle\Omega_{l},\beta\rangle_{g}\ ,

(11)

where
\Omega_{l}
is the Maurer–Cartan one-form.

Example 3.3.1
An important example of such a local representative
\omega^{U}
is in the case of the frame bundle
LM
for an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
with a given chart
(U,\phi)
. For a given local section
\sigma:U\subset M\to LM
defined by

\sigma(m)\ :=\ ((\mbox{$\partial$}_{1})_{m},\ldots,(\mbox{$\partial$}_{n})_{m},)

(12)

the corresponding
\omega^{U}
has following components (the three indices below are divided into two indices
\alpha,\beta
for the Lie-algebra
L(GL(n,\mathds{R}))
and one
1
-form index
\mu
):

((\omega^{U})_{\mu})^{\alpha}_{\ \beta}\ =:\ \Gamma^{\alpha}_{\ \mu\beta}\quad\textrm{with}\quad\alpha,\beta,\mu=1,\dots,n

(13)

where
\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\ \mu\beta}
is the famous
Christoffel symbol
of this
Levi-Civita or affine connection
3
3

3

This relies on a choice of the natural metric on
M
, which is akin to choosing a basis
G^{\ a}_{b}
of the Lie-algebra
L(GL(n,\mathds{R}))
with components
(G^{\ a}_{b})^{c}_{\ d}:=\delta^{c}_{b}\delta^{a}_{d}
.
that is widely used in Riemannian geometry and general relativity.

Theorem 3.3.2
Let
(P,M,\pi)
be a principal
G
-bundle with
U_{i},U_{j}\subset M
such that
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq 0
. Further, let
A_{\mu}^{(i)}
and
A_{\mu}^{(j)}
be local gauge functions arising from given local trivializing sections
\sigma_{i}:U_{i}\to P
and
\sigma_{j}:U_{j}\to G
respectively. The transformation of these fields under the action of gauge functions
\Lambda_{ij}:U_{i}\cap U_{j}\to G
is, for any
m\in M
, given by

A_{\mu}^{(j)}(m)\ =\ \mathrm{Ad}_{\Lambda_{ij}(m)^{-1}}\left(A_{\mu}^{(i)}(m)\right)+(\Lambda_{ij}^{*}\Omega_{l})_{\mu}(m)\ .

(14)

Remark 3.3.4
We notice that for matrix Lie groups the above transformation rule takes the following simple form:

A_{\mu}^{(j)}(m)\ =\ \Lambda_{ij}(m)^{-1}\left(A_{\mu}^{(i)}(m)\right)\Lambda_{ij}(m)+\Lambda_{ij}(m)^{-1}\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\Lambda_{ij}(m)\ .

(15)

Remark 3.3.5
This gauge transformation behaviour is what prevents a gauge field
\omega^{U}
from being global, i.e.
A\penalty\ \mathrm{or}\penalty\ \Gamma\not\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. In particular, this explains why the Christoffel symbol is not a tensor! This is because of the following transformation rule between any two given charts
(U,\phi)
and
(U^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
on a
4
-dimensional manifold
M\ni m
with
\phi(m)=\{x^{0},\ldots,x^{4}\}
and
\phi^{\prime}(m)=\{x^{\prime 0},\ldots,x^{\prime 4}\}
where the indices, such as
\alpha
below, takes temporal
\alpha{=}0
as well as spatial
\alpha{=}1,2,3
values:

\Gamma^{{}^{\prime}\alpha}_{\ \mu\beta}\ =\ \frac{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\prime\alpha}}{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\tilde{\alpha}}}\,\frac{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\tilde{\mu}}}{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\prime\mu}}\,\frac{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\tilde{\beta}}}{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\prime\beta}}\,\Gamma^{\tilde{\alpha}}_{\ \tilde{\mu}\tilde{\beta}}+\frac{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\prime\alpha}}{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\lambda}}\,\frac{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\lambda}}{\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\prime\mu}\mbox{$\partial$}x^{\prime\beta}}\ .

(16)

An explicit expression for
\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\ \mu\beta}
for a given basis of vector fields
\{\mbox{$\partial$}_{0},\ldots,\mbox{$\partial$}_{4}\}
on
M
equipped with a pseudo-Riemannian metric
g
with components
g(\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu},\mbox{$\partial$}_{\nu})=g_{\mu\nu}
is given by

\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\ \mu\beta}\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\,g^{\alpha\lambda}\left(g_{\mu\lambda,\beta}+g_{\lambda\beta,\mu}-g_{\mu\beta,\lambda}\right)\ ,

(17)

where
g_{{\mu\beta,\lambda}}:=\mbox{$\partial$}_{\lambda}[g_{\mu\beta}]
.

4
Parallel transport

Definition 3.4.1
Owing to the fact that
\pi_{*}:H_{p}P\to T_{\pi(p)}M
is an isomorphism, there exists the notion of a unique vector field for a given
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
known as the
horizontal lift
of
X
and is denoted as
X^{\uparrow}
. This satisfies, for all
p\in P
, the following conditions

1.

ver(X_{p}^{\uparrow})=0
and

2.

\pi_{*}(X_{p}^{\uparrow})=X_{\pi(p)}
.

Remark 3.4.1
The act of horizontal lifting is
G
-equivariant i.e.
(r_{g})_{*}(X_{p}^{\uparrow})=X_{pg}^{\uparrow}
.

Definition 3.4.2
A
horizontal lift
of a smooth path
\gamma:[a,b]\subset\mathds{R}\to M
is another path
\gamma^{\uparrow}:[a,b]\to P
which is horizontal, i.e.
ver(\gamma^{\uparrow})=0
such that
\pi(\gamma^{\uparrow}(t))=\gamma(t)
for all
t\in[a,b]
.

Theorem 3.4.1
For each point
p\in\pi^{-1}(\{\gamma(a)\})
there exist a unique horizontal lift
\gamma^{\uparrow}:[a,b]\to P
of
\gamma
such that
\gamma^{\uparrow}(a)=p
.

Remark 3.4.2
Given a path
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
and another path
\beta:[a,b]\to P
which projects down to
\gamma
, i.e.
\pi(\beta(t))=\gamma(t)
for all
t\in[a,b]
, there exits some unique function
g:[a,b]\to G
such that

\gamma^{\uparrow}(t)\ =\ \beta(t)\,g(t)\quad\forall\quad t\in[a,b]\ .

(1)

Theorem 3.4.2
The unique path
g:[a,b]\to G
defined above satisfies the following first order ODE in terms of a Lie-algebra valued
1
-form
\omega
:

0\ =\ \mathrm{Ad}_{g(t)^{-1}*}\left(\omega_{\beta}(t)(X_{\beta,\beta(t)})\right)+\langle\Omega_{l},X_{g}\rangle_{g(t)}\ ,

(2)

where
X_{\beta,\beta(t)}\equiv\dot{\beta}(t)
is the tangent vector to the curve
\beta
at point
\beta(t)
(see Definition 2.1.6).

Remark 3.4.3
For a matrix Lie group
G
and a local gauge field
A_{\mu}
(
9
), the above ODE takes the following simple form

\dot{g}(t)\ =\ -A_{\mu}(\gamma(t))\,\dot{\gamma}^{\mu}(t)\,g(t)\ ,

(3)

where the components of the curve
\gamma
in a local chart has been denoted as
\gamma^{\mu}
. The solution to this ODE, for an initial condition
g(0)=g_{0}
, is obtained as a path-ordered exponential in the following way

\displaystyle g(t)

\displaystyle=\ \left({\bf P}\,\mathrm{exp}\left(-\int\limits_{a}^{t}A_{\mu}(\gamma(s))\,\dot{\gamma}^{\mu}(s)\mathrm{d}{s}\right)\right)g_{0}

(4)

\displaystyle=\ g_{0}-\left(\int\limits_{a}^{t}A_{\mu}(\gamma(s))\,\dot{\gamma}^{\mu}(s)\mathrm{d}{s}\right)g_{0}

\displaystyle+\left(\int\limits_{a}^{t}\mathrm{d}{s_{1}}\int_{a}^{s_{1}}\mathrm{d}{s_{2}}\,A_{\mu_{1}}(\gamma(s_{1}))\,A_{\mu_{2}}(\gamma(s_{2}))\,\dot{\gamma}^{\mu_{1}}(s_{1})\,\dot{\gamma}^{\mu_{2}}(s_{2})\right)g_{0}-\ldots\ .

Remark 3.4.4
From the above result we observe that the local expression for the horizontal lift
\gamma^{\uparrow}
, for the path
\gamma:[a,b]\to U\subset M
, is given by

\gamma^{\uparrow}(t)\ =\ \sigma(\gamma(t))\left({\bf P}\,\mathrm{exp}\left(-\int\limits_{a}^{t}A_{\mu}(\gamma(s))\,\dot{\gamma}^{\mu}(s)\mathrm{d}{s}\right)\right)g_{0}\ .

(5)

Definition 3.4.3
The
parallel transport
along a path
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
is defined by the following map

T_{\gamma}:\ \pi^{-1}(\gamma(a))\to\pi^{-1}(\gamma(b))\ ,\quad p\mapsto\gamma^{\uparrow}_{p}(b)\ ,

(6)

where
\gamma^{\uparrow}
is the unique horizontal lift of
\gamma
passing through
p\in\pi^{-1}(\gamma(a))
.

Remark 3.4.5
We observe that
T_{\gamma}
is a bijection on fibres and thus on
G
. An interesting thing happens when
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
is a loop i.e.
\gamma(a)=\gamma(b)
; one obtains a natural map from loops based at
\gamma(a)\in M
to elements of
G
. The subgroup of all elements of
G
that can be obtained in this way is called the
holonomy
group of the principle bundle
P
. This plays an important role in understanding the relation between certain topological properties of
M
with the connection
A_{\mu}
.

Definition 3.4.4
Let
(P,M,\pi)
be a principal
G
-bundle equipped with a connection
1
-form
\omega
. Furthermore, let
(P_{F},M,\pi_{F})
be associated with
P
via the left action of
G
on
F
. A
vertical subspace
of
T_{[p,v]}P_{F}
is defined, analogous to that of principal bundle, as

V_{[p,v]}P_{F}\ :=\ \{X\in T_{[p,v]}P_{F}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \pi_{F*}X=0\}\ .

(7)

Similarly, the
horizontal subspace

H_{[p,v]}P_{F}
can be defined as the orthogonal complement:

T_{[p,v]}P_{F}\ =\ V_{[p,v]}P_{F}\oplus H_{[p,v]}P_{F}\ .

(8)

Definition 3.4.5
The
horizontal lift
of a path
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
to the associated bundle
P_{F}
and passing through
[p,v]\in\pi_{F}^{-1}(\gamma(a))
is defined as

\gamma_{F}^{\uparrow}(t)\ :=\ [\gamma^{\uparrow}(t),v]

(9)

where
\gamma^{\uparrow}(a)=p
.

Remark 3.4.6
One can define
parallel transport

T_{\gamma}
along a path
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
on the associated bundle
P_{F}
analogous to the principal bundle case using the above definition of the horizontal lifting. For associated vector bundles
P_{V}
with the vector space
V
admitting a linear representation of
G
, this notion then facilitates the following definition of the covariant derivative.

1
Covariant derivative

Definition 3.4.6
The covariant derivative of a section
\psi:M\to P_{V}
of an associated vector bundle
P_{V}
along a path
\gamma:[0,\varepsilon]\to M
with
\varepsilon>0
at
m_{0}=:\gamma(0)
is defined by

D_{X_{\gamma,\gamma(0)}}\psi\ :=\ \lim_{t\to 0}\left(\frac{T_{\gamma}(\psi(\gamma(t)))-\psi(m_{0})}{t}\right)\in\pi_{V}^{-1}(m_{0})\ .

(10)

Remark 3.4.7
The covariant derivative
D_{X}
, for
X\in T_{m}M
, has the following algebraic properties for any section
\psi,\widetilde{\psi}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(P_{V})
:

1.

D_{fX+Y}\psi\ =\ f\,D_{X}\psi+D_{Y}\psi
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
X,Y\in T_{m}M
,

2.

D_{X}(\psi+\widetilde{\psi})\ =\ D_{X}\psi+D_{X}\widetilde{\psi}
for all
X\in T_{m}M
and

3.

D_{X}(f\psi)\ =\ X[f]\,\psi+f\,D_{X}\psi
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
X\in T_{m}M
.

This following more generic notion of a covariant derivative (
12
) is related to this one, as we will see below.

Definition 3.4.7
The
exterior covariant derivative
of a
k
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}(P)
on a principal bundle
P
is a horizontal
(k{+}1)
-form defined by

D\omega(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1})\ :=\ \mathrm{d}{\omega}(hor(X_{1}),\ldots,hor(X_{k+1}))

(11)

for a given set of vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k+1}\in\mathfrak{X}(P)
.

Remark 3.4.8
This notion of covariant derivative can be extended to an associated vector bundle
P_{V}
discussed before with the aid of a given
G
-equivariant map
\phi:P\to F
that has an associated section
\sigma_{\phi}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(P_{V})
(see Theorem 3.2.2) as follows

D\phi\ :=\ \mathrm{d}{\phi}\circ hor\ .

(12)

One can show, for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(P)
and a given connection
\omega
on
P
, that

F\ni D\phi(X)\equiv D_{X}\phi\ :=\ \mathrm{d}{\phi}(X)+\omega(X)\phi\ ,

(13)

where we notice that
\phi
are
F
-valued functions on
P
. Furthermore, one can pull this definition back to
M
using any local trivializing map
\sigma:U\subset M\to P
and noticing the fact that the pull-back operation is natural:

\sigma^{*}(D\phi)(X)\ :=\ \mathrm{d}{\sigma^{*}\phi}(X)+\sigma^{*}(\omega)(X)(\sigma^{*}\phi)\quad\forall\quad X\in\mathfrak{X}(P)\ .

(14)

Example 3.4.1
For a given local orthonormal coframe of vector fields
\{\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\}
with
\mu=0,1,2,3
on a
4
-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
M
, e.g. the Minkowski space
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
, the covariant derivative
D_{\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}}=:D_{\mu}
of the above-mentioned section
\phi
(or
\sigma_{\phi}
to be precise) is given in terms of local gauge fields
A_{\mu}
as

D_{\mu}\phi\ :=\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\phi+A_{\mu}\phi\ .

(15)

Example 3.4.2
The covariant derivative
D_{\mu}=:\nabla_{\mu}
of the tensor bundle
T^{p,q}(M)
associated with the frame bundle
LM
on
M
is given in terms of the Levi–Civita connection and can be expressed in terms of the Chritoffel symbols. For example, covariant derivative of
T\in T^{1,2}(M)
with components
T^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}:=T(\mathrm{d}{x}^{\mu},\mbox{$\partial$}_{\alpha},\mbox{$\partial$}_{\beta})
is given by

D_{\rho}\,T^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\ =\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\rho}T^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\rho\lambda}T^{\lambda}_{\alpha\beta}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\rho\alpha}T^{\mu}_{\lambda\beta}-\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\rho\beta}T^{\mu}_{\lambda\alpha}\ .

(16)

Remark 3.4.9
The Levi–Civita connection
\Gamma
is metric compatible:

\nabla g=0\nabla_{\mu}\quad\textrm{or}\quad g_{\alpha\beta}\ =\ 0\ .

(17)

Moreover this connection is torsion-free, i.e.

0\ =\ T(X,Y)\ :=\ \nabla_{X}Y-\nabla_{Y}X-[X,Y]

(18)

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. Choosing vector fields
X=\mbox{$\partial$}_{\alpha}
and
Y=\mbox{$\partial$}_{\beta}
, the torsion-free condition ensures that the Christoffel symbol is symmetric in the subscript indices:

\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\ =\ \Gamma^{\mu}_{\beta\alpha}\ .

(19)

2
Curvature

Definition 3.4.8
If
\omega
is a connection
1
-form on a principal
G
-bundle
P
then
D\omega=:\Omega
is the Lie-algebra valued
curvature

2
-form of
\omega
.

Remark 3.4.10
It can be shown that the curvature
2
-form
\Omega
satisfies the Bianchi identity:

D\Omega\ =\ 0\ .

(20)

Theorem 3.4.3
For arbitrary pair of vector fields
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(P)
the curvature
2
-form
D\omega
satisfies the following Cartan structure equation

D\omega(X,Y)\ :=\ \mathrm{d}{\omega}(X,Y)+[\omega(X),\omega(Y)]\ ,

(21)

where we have employed the Lie bracket on
L(G)
.

Example 3.4.3
For the Levi–Civita connection
\Gamma
the curvature
2
-form
D\Gamma=:R
—valued in
L(GL(n,\mathds{R}))
for an
n
-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
—is known as the Riemann curvature and is given by

R\ =\ \mathrm{d}{\Gamma}+\Gamma\wedge\Gamma\ .

(22)

This turns out to be a
(1,3)
-tensor, i.e.
R\in T^{1,3}(M)
and, in local coframe
\{\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\}
and frame
\{\mathrm{d}{x}^{\mu}\}
with
\mu=0,1,2,3,
of
M
, admits the following expression in terms of Christoffel symbol

R^{\rho}_{\ \sigma\mu\nu}\ =\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\nu\sigma}-\mbox{$\partial$}_{\nu}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\sigma}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\lambda}\,\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\nu\sigma}-\Gamma^{\rho}_{\nu\lambda}\,\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\sigma}\ .

(23)

Remark 4.3.11
Riemann curvature tensor gives rise to the so called Ricci tensor
R_{\mu\nu}:=R^{\rho}_{\ \mu\rho\nu}\in T^{0,2}(M)
through index contraction, which yields the scalar curvature
R:=g^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}
. With these tools and the insight of
equivalence principle
:

“For any point
m\in M
there always exists a local chart
(U,\phi)
with
m\in U
admitting a smooth local orthonormal frame
\sigma\in\Gamma^{\infty}(LU)
of the local frame bundle
LU
; in other words, every (spacetime) manifold is locally flat in a Minkowski sense"

Albert Einstein was able to construct the following field equation relating the curvature of spacetime with its matter content

G_{\mu\nu}\ :=\ R_{\mu\nu}-\ \frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R\ =\ \frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}\ ,

(24)

where
G
is the Newton constant,
c
is the speed of light and
T_{\mu\nu}
is the stress-energy tensor arising from the variation of the matter action
S_{m}
with respect to the matric:

T_{\mu\nu}\ =\ -\frac{2}{\sqrt{-\mathrm{det}\,g}}\frac{\delta S_{m}}{\delta g^{\mu\nu}}\ .

(25)

Example 3.4.4
A trivial solution of the vacuum Einstein equation is the Minkowski metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
which is globally flat. Another very important solution is the FLRW metric for homogeneous and isotropic universe, where the evolution of the
scale factor

a(t)
is governed by an appropriate stress-energy tensor
T_{\mu\nu}
, is given by

g\ =\ -c^{2}\mathrm{d}{t}^{2}+a(t)^{2}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{r}^{2}}{1-\kappa r^{2}}+r^{2}\mathrm{d}{\theta}^{2}+r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}{\phi}^{2}\right)\ ,

(26)

where
\{r,\theta,\phi\}
are coordinates on celestial spheres with respect to an observer (like us) and the parameter
\kappa=-1
,
0
or
1
denotes the topology of the
3
-dimensional Euclidean space as being open, flat or closed respectively.

Example 3.4.5
For local Yang–Mills field
A:=\sigma^{*}\omega
the curvature
2
-form
D\omega=:F
is given by

F\ =\ \mathrm{d}{A}+A\wedge A\ .

(27)

5
Yang-Mills equation

Remark 3.5.1
A local expression for the Yang–Mills curvature
F
on a Lorentzian manifold
M
with local orthonormal coframe
\{\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\}
is given by

F_{\mu\nu}\ =\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\mbox{$\partial$}_{\nu}A_{\mu}-[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}]\ .

(1)

Remark 3.5.2
The Yang–Mills curvature transforms under a local gauge transformation
\Lambda_{ij}(m)
(see Definition 3.2.5) in the following way

F^{(j)}_{\mu\nu}(m)\ =\ \Lambda_{ij}(m)^{-1}\,F^{(i)}_{\mu\nu}(m)\,\Lambda_{ij}(m)\ ,

(2)

and is thus global, i.e.
F\in\Omega^{2}(M)
.

Remark 3.5.3
The Yang–Mills equation on a Lorentzian manifold
M
(with
D
as in
12
) is given by

*D(*F)\ =\ J\ ,

(3)

where the
1
-form
J\in\Omega^{1}(M)
is the
current
that arises from the presence of any source “charge" on the manifold.

Remark 3.5.4
The Yang–Mills action (coupling constant
g
)

S_{YM}\ =\ \frac{1}{2g^{2}}\int_{M}\mathrm{Tr}(F\wedge*F)

(4)

together with the following action for the source
J
:

S_{J}\ =\ \frac{1}{g^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}(A\wedge*J)

(5)

gives rise to the above Yang–Mills equation by variational principle.

Remark 3.5.5
Maxwell’s theory of electromagnetism that we saw in the last chapter is a special case of Yang–Mills theory where the gauge group is
U(1)
.

Chapter 3
Yang–Mills equations on
\mathrm{d}{S}_{4}

Here we review the conformal relation of the
4
-dimensional de Sitter space with a finite Lorentzian cylinder with
S^{3}
-slicing, study calculus on
S^{3}
and present the Yang–Mills field equations on the cylinder. The content of this chapter is partially taken from
[
22
,
23
,
24
]
1
1

1

The role of
L_{a}
and
R_{a}
in
[
22
]
is interchanged as compared to this thesis.
.

1
The de Sitter-Minkowski correspondence via
S^{3}
-cylinder

The de Sitter space in four dimensions
\mathrm{d}S_{4}
has a natural embedding as a single-sheeted hyperboloid in five-dimensional Minkowski space
\mathds{R}^{1,4}
with coordinates
(q_{0},q_{{}_{A}});A=1,2,3,4
and global length scale
\ell
and is given by

-q_{0}^{2}\,+q_{1}^{2}\,+q_{2}^{2}\,+q_{3}^{2}\,+q_{4}^{2}\,\ =\ \ell^{2}.

(1)

One can use the flat metric on
\mathds{R}^{1,4}
:

\mathrm{d}s_{(1,4)}^{2}\ =\ -\mathrm{d}q_{0}^{2}\,+\mathrm{d}q_{1}^{2}\,+\mathrm{d}q_{2}^{2}\,+\mathrm{d}q_{3}^{2}\,+\mathrm{d}q_{4}^{2}

(2)

to construct the corresponding metric on
\mathrm{d}S_{4}
using the coordinate constraint (
1
). The metric thus obtained is conformally equivalent to the metric on a finite Lorentzian cylinder over the
3
-sphere
S^{3}
:
\mathcal{I}\times S^{3}
with
\mathcal{I}=\left(0,\pi\right)
. To see this, we employ the following coordinates

q_{{}_{A}}\ =\ \ell\,\omega_{{}_{A}}\,\csc\tau\quad\textrm{and}\quad q_{{}_{5}}\ =\ -\ell\,\cot\tau\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad\quad\tau\,\in\,(0,\pi)\ ,

(3)

where
\omega_{{}_{A}}
are the natural embedding coordinates of
S^{3}\hookrightarrow\mathds{R}^{4}
:
\omega_{{}_{A}}\omega_{{}_{A}}\!=\!1
2
2

2

Repeated indices are summed over.
. A natural hyperspherical parametrisation of
\omega_{{}_{A}}
is given by

\omega_{1}\ =\ \sin\chi\,\sin\theta\,\cos\phi\ ,\quad\omega_{2}\ =\ \sin\chi\,\sin\theta\,\sin\phi\ ,\quad\omega_{3}\ =\ \sin\chi\,\cos\theta\ ,\quad\omega_{4}\ =\ \cos\chi\ ,

(4)

with
0\leq\chi,\theta\leq\pi
and
0\leq\phi\leq 2\pi
. The modified metric has the following form:

\mathrm{d}s^{2}\ =\ \mathrm{d}s_{(1,4)}^{2}|_{\mathrm{d}S_{4}}\ =\ \frac{\ell^{2}}{\sin^{2}\tau}\left(-\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}+\mathrm{d}\Omega_{3}^{2}\right);\quad\mathrm{d}\Omega_{3}^{\ 2}\ =\ \mathrm{d}\omega_{A}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{A}|_{\mathrm{d}S_{4}}\ .

(5)

By gluing two copies of such Lorentzian cylinders at
\tau{=}0
by taking
\tau\in\widetilde{\cal I}:=\left(-\pi,\pi\right)
one finds that half of the resultant cylinder
\widetilde{\cal I}\times S^{3}
is conformally equivalent to the
4
-dimensional Minkowski space via following parametrization of
(t,x,y,z)\in\mathds{R}^{1,3}
:

\cot\tau\ =\ \frac{r^{2}{-}t^{2}{+}\ell^{2}}{2\,\ell\,t}\ ,\ \omega_{1}\ =\ \gamma\,\frac{x}{\ell}\ ,\ \omega_{2}\ =\ \gamma\,\frac{y}{\ell}\ ,\ \omega_{3}\ =\ \gamma\,\frac{z}{\ell}\ ,\ \omega_{4}\ =\ \gamma\frac{r^{2}{-}t^{2}{-}\ell^{2}}{2\,\ell^{2}}\ ,

(6)

where we have abbreviated

Figure 1
:
An illustration of the map between a cylinder
2\mathcal{I}\times S^{3}
and Minkowski space
R^{1,3}
.
The Minkowski coordinates cover the shaded area. The boundary of this area is given by the curve
\omega_{4}{=}\cos\chi{=}\cos\tau
.
Each point is a two-sphere spanned by
\{\hat{\omega}_{1},\hat{\omega}_{2},\hat{\omega}_{3}\}
, which is mapped to a sphere of constant
r
and
t
.

r^{2}=x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}\ \ \&\ \ \gamma\ =\ \frac{2\,\ell^{2}}{\sqrt{4\,\ell^{2}t^{2}+(r^{2}-t^{2}+\ell^{2})^{2}}}=\frac{2\,\ell^{2}}{\sqrt{4\,\ell^{2}r^{2}+(t^{2}-r^{2}+\ell^{2})^{2}}}\ .

(7)

A straightforward computation yields

\cos\tau-\cos\chi\ =\ \gamma\ >0\ ,

(8)

which shows that only half of the cylinder—constrained by
\cos\tau<\cos\chi
—is allowed by the map (
6
). Plugging this map back into the de Sitter metric (
5
) we obtain

\mathrm{d}s^{2}\ =\ \frac{\ell^{2}}{t^{2}}\,\bigl(-\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{2}+\mathrm{d}z^{2}\bigr)\quad\textrm{with}\quad(x,y,z)\equiv(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})\in\mathds{R}^{3}\ \&\ t\in\mathds{R}\ ,

(9)

which is the Minkowski metric up to a conformal factor
3
3

3

In fact, the map (
6
) covers only the positive half of the Minkowski space, i.e.
t\in\mathds{R}_{+}
for the original cylinder
{\cal I}\times S^{3}
.
. A smooth gluing of the two cylinders across the time slice
t\!=\tau\!=0
is nicely depicted in Figure
1
. Now, looking at the maps (
4
) and (
6
) we see a SO(3)-symmetry that we can exploit by writing

\omega_{a}\ =\ \sin\chi\;\hat{\omega}_{a}\ \penalty\ \textrm{and}\penalty\ \ x^{a}\ =\ r\,\hat{\omega}_{a}\ \penalty\ \textrm{for}\penalty\ \ a\ =\ 1,2,3\ \penalty\ \textrm{and}\penalty\ r\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{\ell}{\gamma}}\,\sin\chi

(10)

where the unit
S^{2}
coordinates
\hat{\omega}_{a}
are given by

\hat{\omega}_{1}\ =\ \sin\theta\,\cos\phi\ ,\quad\hat{\omega}_{2}\ =\ \sin\theta\,\sin\phi\quad\textrm{and}\quad\hat{\omega}_{3}\ =\ \cos\theta\ .

(11)

Figure 2
:

Penrose diagram of Minkowski space
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
. Each point hides a two-sphere
S^{2}{\ni}\{\theta,\phi\}
.
Blue curves indicate
t{=}\textrm{const}
slices while brown curves depict the world volumes of
r{=}\textrm{const}
spheres.
The lightcone of the Minkowski-space origin is drawn in red.

We can identify the unit
S^{2}
between the Minkowski space and the de Sitter space using (
6
) and (
10
), so that, we have an effective map between coordinates
(t,r)
and
(\tau,\chi)
:

\frac{t}{\ell}\ =\ \frac{\sin\tau}{\cos\tau\,-\,\cos\chi}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\frac{r}{\ell}\ =\ \frac{\sin\chi}{\cos\tau\,-\,\cos\chi}\qquad\quad\textrm{for}\quad\quad\chi>|\tau|\ ,

(12)

which reveals that the triangular
(\tau,\chi)
domain (where the points represent unit
S^{2}
) is nothing but the Penrose diagram of Minkowski space (See Figure
2
). The special lines and points in Figure

Equation (
12
) can be used to obtain the Jacobian for the transformation between the coordinates
y^{m}\in\{\tau,\chi,\theta,\phi\}
and
x^{\mu}\in\{t,r,\theta,\phi\}
:

\bigl(J^{m}_{\ \ \mu}\bigr)\ :=\ \frac{\mbox{$\partial$}(\tau,\chi,\theta,\phi)}{\mbox{$\partial$}(t,r,\theta,\phi)}\ =\ \frac{1}{\ell}\biggl(\begin{matrix}p&-q\\[4.0pt]
q&-p\end{matrix}\biggr)\oplus\mathds{1}_{2}\ ,

(13)

where the polynomials
p,q
are given by

p\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{2}}}\,(r^{2}{+}t^{2}{+}\ell^{2})/2\ =\ 1{-}\cos\tau\cos\chi\quad\textrm{and}\quad q\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{2}}}\,t\,r\ =\ \sin\tau\sin\chi\ .

(14)

A more direct way of visualizing the conformal correspondence between Minkwoski space and the
S^{3}
-cylinder is via Carter-Penrose transformation that readily produces the lightcone picture (see Appendix
7
for details).

1
Structure of
3
-sphere and its harmonics

The presence of
S^{3}
in the metric (
5
) is an added advantage which can be exploited to write an
SO(4)
-invariant gauge connection and obtain the corresponding fields by solving Yang–Mills equation on the Lorentzian cylinder. These quantities can be later exported to the Minkowski spacetime owing to the conformal invariance of the vacuum Yang–Mills equation in
4
-dimensions. To this end, we start with the group
SO(4)
—which is isomorphic to two copies of
SU(2)
(up to a
\mathds{Z}_{2}
grading). Each of these
SU(2)
has a group action that generates a left (right) multiplication (aka translation) on
S^{3}
. This can easily be checked using the map

g:\;S^{3}\rightarrow SU(2);\penalty\ \penalty\ (\omega_{1},\ \omega_{2},\ \omega_{3},\ \omega_{4})\,\mapsto\,-i\begin{pmatrix}\beta&\alpha^{*}\\
\alpha&-\beta^{*}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(15)

with
\alpha:=\omega_{1}+\mathrm{i}\omega_{2}\ \penalty\ \textrm{and}\penalty\ \ \beta:=\omega_{3}+\mathrm{i}\omega_{4}
. This parameterization of
g
ensures that the identity element
e=\mathds{1}_{2}
of the group
SU(2)
can be obtained from
(0,0,0,1)
, i.e. the North pole of
S^{3}
. It is well known that
S^{3}
is the group manifold of
SU(2)
. Keeping this in mind, we consider the Maurer–Cartan one-form

\Omega_{l}(g)\,:=\,g^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}g\ =\ e^{a}\,T_{a},\penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ T_{a}=-i\sigma_{a}

(16)

being the
SU(2)
generators. The left-invariant one-form
4
4

4

Named so because they remain invariant under the dragging induced by the left SU(2) multiplication.

e^{a}
can, alternatively, be expressed using the so called self-dual ’t Hooft symbol
\eta^{a}_{{}_{BC}}
:

e^{a}\ =\ -\eta^{a}_{{}_{\ BC}}\,\omega_{{}_{B}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{{}_{C}}\quad\textrm{where}\quad\eta^{a}_{\ bc}\ =\ \varepsilon_{abc}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\eta^{a}_{\ b4}\ =\ -\eta^{a}_{\ 4b}\ =\ \delta^{a}_{b}\ .

(17)

They satisfy following useful identities

\delta_{ab}\,e^{a}\,e^{b}\ =\ \mathrm{d}\Omega_{3}^{2}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\mathrm{d}e^{a}+\varepsilon_{abc}\,e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\ =\ 0.

(18)

The left-invariant vector fields
L_{a}
—generating the right translations—are dual to
e^{a}
and are given by

L_{a}\ =\ -\eta^{a}_{{}_{\ BC}}\,\omega_{{}_{B}}\,{\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega_{{}_{C}}}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\left[L_{a},L_{b}\right]\ =\ 2\,\varepsilon_{abc}\,L_{c}\ .

(19)

In a similar way, the right-invariant vector fields
R_{a}
(generating the left translations) are given by

R_{a}\ =\ -\tilde{\eta}^{a}_{{}_{\ BC}}\,\omega_{{}_{B}}\,{\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega_{{}_{C}}}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\left[R_{a},R_{b}\right]=2\,\varepsilon_{abc}\,R_{c}\ ,

(20)

where the anti self–dual ’t Hooft symbols
\tilde{\eta}^{a}_{{}_{\ BC}}
are obtained from (
17
) by flipping the sign in the previous defintion whenever
B,C\!=4
. Furthermore, the vector fields
L_{a}
and
R_{a}
act on the one-forms
e^{a}
via their Lie derivative, which can be performed using Cartan formula (see Section
6
):

\displaystyle L_{a}\,e^{b}\ :=\ \mathcal{L}_{L_{a}}\,e^{b}

\displaystyle=\ \mathrm{d}\circ\iota_{L_{a}}e^{b}+\iota_{L_{a}}\circ\mathrm{d}{e^{b}}

(21)

\displaystyle=\ \mathrm{d}{\left(e^{b}(L_{a})\right)}-\varepsilon^{b}_{\ ij}\,\iota_{L_{a}}\left(e^{i}\wedge e^{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\ \mathrm{d}{\left(\delta^{b}_{a}\right)}-\varepsilon^{b}_{\ ij}\left(e^{i}(L_{a})e^{j}-e^{j}(L_{a})e^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\ 2\,\varepsilon_{abc}\,e^{c}\ ,

where in the second line we have used (
18
). A similar calculation for the action of
R_{a}
yields

R_{a}\,e^{b}\ =\ 0\ .

(22)

We can now write differential
\mathrm{d}
of the functions
f\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{I}\times S^{3}\right)
using
L_{a}
5
5

5

One can use
R_{a}
and its dual
1
-form as well.
as

\mathrm{d}f\ =\ \mathrm{d}\tau\;\partial_{\tau}f\ +\ e^{a}\,L_{a}f\ .

(23)

Functions on
S^{3}
can be expanded in a basis of harmonics
Y_{j}(\chi,\theta,\phi)
with
2j\in\mathds{N}_{0}
,
which are eigenfunctions of the scalar Laplacian
6
6

6

The
SO(4)
spin of these functions is actually
2j
, but we label them with their half-spin for reasons to be clear below.
:

-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{3}Y_{j}\ =\ 2j(2j{+}2)\,Y_{j}\ =\ 4j(j{+}1)\,Y_{j}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(L^{2}+R^{2})\,Y_{j}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}({\cal D}^{2}+{\cal P}^{2})\,Y_{j}

(24)

where
L^{2}=L_{a}L_{a}
and
R^{2}=R_{a}R_{a}
are (minus four times) the Casimirs of
su(2)_{L}
and
su(2)_{R}
, respectively,

-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}L^{2}\,Y_{j}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}R^{2}\,Y_{j}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{3}Y_{j}\ =\ j(j{+}1)\,Y_{j}\ .

(25)

We have also introduced
{\cal D}^{2}={\cal D}_{a}{\cal D}_{a}
and
{\cal P}^{2}={\cal P}_{a}{\cal P}_{a}
with

{\cal D}_{a}\ :=\ R_{a}+L_{a}\ =\ -2\,\varepsilon_{a}^{\ bc}\,\omega_{b}\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{c}\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\cal P}_{a}\ :=\ R_{a}-L_{a}\ =\ 2\,\omega_{[a}\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{4]}

(26)

with
\mbox{$\partial$}_{{}_{A}}\equiv{\textstyle\frac{\mbox{$\partial$}}{\mbox{$\partial$}\omega_{A}}}
so that

\left[{\cal D}_{a},{\cal D}_{b}\right]\ =\ 2\,\varepsilon_{ab}^{\ \ c}\,{\cal D}_{c}\ ,\qquad\left[{\cal D}_{a},{\cal P}_{b}\right]\ =\ 2\,\varepsilon_{ab}^{\ \ c}\,{\cal P}_{c}\ ,\qquad\left[{\cal P}_{a},{\cal P}_{b}\right]\ =\ 2\,\varepsilon_{ab}^{\ \ c}\,{\cal D}_{c}\ .

(27)

Hence,
\{{\cal D}_{a}\}
spans the diagonal subalgebra
su(2)_{D}\subset so(4)
, which generates the stabilizer subgroup
SO(3)

in the coset representation
S^{3}\cong SO(4)/SO(3)
. Therefore,
{\cal D}^{2}
is (minus four times) the Casimir of
su(2)_{D}
, with eigenvalues
l(l{+}1)
for
l=0,1,\ldots,2j
,
and
{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}{\cal D}^{2}=\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{2}
is the scalar Laplacian on the
S^{2}
slices traced out in
S^{3}
by the
SO(3)_{D}
action.

To further characterize a complete basis of
S^{3}
harmonics, there are two natural options,
corresponding to two different complete choices of mutually commuting operators to be diagonalized. First, the left-right (or toroidal) harmonics
Y_{j;m,n}
are eigenfunctions of
L^{2}=R^{2}
,
L_{3}
and
R_{3}
:

{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,L_{3}\,Y_{j;m,n}\ =\ n\,Y_{j;m,n}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,R_{3}\,Y_{j;m,n}\ =\ m\,Y_{j;m,n}\ ,

(28)

and hence the corresponding ladder operators

L_{\pm}\ =\ (L_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}L_{2})/\sqrt{2}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad R_{\pm}\ =\ (R_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}R_{2})/\sqrt{2}

(29)

act, in their Hermitian avatar, as

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,L_{\pm}\,Y_{j;m,n}

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{(j{\mp}n)(j{\pm}n{+}1)/2}\,Y_{j;m,n\pm 1}\ ,

(30)

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,R_{\pm}\,Y_{j;m,n}

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{(j{\mp}m)(j{\pm}m{+}1)/2}\,Y_{j;m\pm 1,n}\ .

The normalized harmonics
Y_{j;m,n}
can be expanded in terms of functions
\alpha,\beta
and their complex conjugates as

\displaystyle Y_{j;m,n}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{k=0}^{2j}\,K_{j,m,n}(k)\,\alpha^{n+m+k}\,\bar{\alpha}^{k}\,\beta^{j-m-k}\,\bar{\beta}^{j-n-k}\qquad\quad\textrm{with}\quad

(31)

\displaystyle K_{j,m,n}(k)

\displaystyle=\ (-1)^{m+n+k}\,\sqrt{\frac{2j+1}{2\pi^{2}}}\,\frac{\sqrt{(j+m)!(j-m)!(j+n)!(j-n)!}}{(n+m+k)!(j-n-k)!(j-m-k)!k!}\ .

They satisfy the orthonormality condition

\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,Y_{j;m,n}\,\bar{Y}_{j^{\prime};m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\ =\ \delta_{j,j^{\prime}}\,\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\,\delta_{n,n^{\prime}}\quad\textrm{with}\quad\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\ =\ \sin^{2}\chi\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\chi\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\phi\ .

(32)

Second, the adjoint (or hyperspherical) harmonics
{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}
are eigenfunctions of
L^{2}=R^{2}
,
{\cal D}^{2}
and
{\cal D}_{3}
(its Hermitian version to be precise):

-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\,{\cal D}^{2}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}\ =\ l(l{+}1)\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,{\cal D}_{3}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}\ =\ M\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}\ ,

(33)

with the ladder-operator actions
[
18
]

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,{\cal D}_{\pm}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{(l{\mp}M)(l{\pm}M{+}1)/2}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M\pm 1}\ ,

(34)

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,{\cal P}_{\pm}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}

\displaystyle=\ \mp\sqrt{(l{\mp}M{-}1)(l{\mp}M)/2}\,c_{j,l}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l-1,M\pm 1}

\displaystyle\pm\ \sqrt{(l{\pm}M{+}1)(l{\pm}M{+}2)/2}\,c_{j,l+1}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l+1,M\pm 1}\ ,

\displaystyle{\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}}\,{\cal P}_{3}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{l^{2}{-}M^{2}}\,c_{j,l}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l-1,M}\ +\ \sqrt{(l{+}1)^{2}{-}M^{2}}\,c_{j,l+1}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l+1,M}\ ,

where

c_{j,l}\ =\ \sqrt{\bigl((2j{+}1)^{2}-l^{2}\bigr)/\bigl((2l{-}1)(2l{+}1)\bigr)}\ .

(35)

In this case, there exists a recursive construction for harmonics on
S^{k+1}
from those on
S^{k}
:

{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}(\chi,\theta,\phi)\ =\ R_{j,l}(\chi)\,Y_{l,M}(\theta,\phi)\ ;\ R_{j,l}(\chi)\ =\ \mathrm{i}^{2j+l}\,\sqrt{{\textstyle\frac{2j+1}{\sin\chi}}{\textstyle\frac{(2j+l+1)!}{(2j-l)!}}}\,P_{2j+\frac{1}{2}}^{-l-\frac{1}{2}}(\cos\chi)\ ,

(36)

where
Y_{l,M}
are the standard
S^{2}
spherical harmonics and
P_{a}^{b}
denote associated Legendre polynomials of the first kind.
7
7

7

With fractional indices, it is rather a Gegenbauer polynomial, but also a hypergeometric function (see eq. (2.8) of
[
25
,
26
]
).

The two bases of harmonics are related via the standard Clebsch–Gordan series for the angular momentum addition

j\otimes j=0\oplus 1\oplus\ldots\oplus 2j
,

Y_{j;m,n}\ =\ \sum\limits_{l=0}^{2j}\sum\limits_{M=-l}^{l}\,C_{m,n}^{l,M}\,{\widetilde{Y}}_{j;l,M}\ ,\qquad\textrm{with}\qquad C_{m,n}^{l,M}=\langle 2j;l,M|j,m;j,n\rangle

(37)

being the Clebsch–Gordan coefficients enforcing
m{+}n{=}M
and
l\in\{0,1,\ldots,2j\}
.

A somewhat cumbersome calculation involving (
6
), (
7
) and (
17
) gives us the cylinder one–forms in Minkowski coordinates:

\displaystyle e^{0}

\displaystyle:=\ \mathrm{d}{\tau}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{3}}}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}{+}r^{2}{+}\ell^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t-t\,x^{k}\mathrm{d}x^{k}\bigr)

(38)

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{3}}}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}{+}r^{2}{+}\ell^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t-t\,r\,\mathrm{d}r\bigr)\qquad\quad\textrm{and}\quad

\displaystyle e^{a}

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{3}}}\bigl(t\,x^{a}\mathrm{d}t-\bigl[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}{-}r^{2}{+}\ell^{2})\,\delta^{ak}+x^{a}x^{k}+\ell\,\varepsilon^{ajk}x^{j}\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}x^{k}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{3}}}\bigl(\hat{x}^{a}\bigl[r\,t\,\mathrm{d}t-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}{+}r^{2}{+}\ell^{2})\,\mathrm{d}r\bigr]-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(t^{2}{-}r^{2}{+}\ell^{2})\,r\,\mathrm{d}\hat{x}^{a}-\ell\,r^{2}\varepsilon^{ajk}\hat{x}^{j}\mathrm{d}\hat{x}^{k}\bigr)\ .

We further note down the expressions for the vector fields
L_{a}
in terms of
S^{3}
-angles
(\chi,\theta,\phi)
, using (
4
) and (
19
), for later purposes

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=\ \sin\theta\cos\phi\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\chi}\ +\ (\cot\chi\cos\theta\cos\phi+\sin\phi)\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\theta}\ -\ (\cot\chi\csc\theta\sin\phi-\cot\theta\cos\phi)\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\phi}\ ,

(39)

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=\ \sin\theta\sin\phi\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\chi}\ +\ (\cot\chi\cos\theta\sin\phi-\cos\phi)\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\theta}\ +\ (\cot\chi\csc\theta\cos\phi+\cot\theta\sin\phi)\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\phi}\ ,

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle=\ \cos\theta\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\chi}\ -\ \cot\chi\sin\theta\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\theta}\ -\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\phi}\ .

2
Yang-Mills field equations

One can make use of the left-invariant one forms
e^{a}
to expand a generic Yang–Mills gauge potential
\mathcal{A}
(in the temporal gauge
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}{=}0
) as

\mathcal{A}\ =\ X_{a}(\tau,g)\,e^{a},

(1)

where the three
SU(2)
matrices
X_{a}
depends, in general, on both the cylinder parameter
\tau
and internal
S^{3}
-coordinates
\omega
via the map
g
(
15
). The corresponding field strength
\mathcal{F}
, for this gauge connection
\mathcal{A}
, is given by

\mathcal{F}\ =\ \mathrm{d}{{\cal A}}+\mathcal{A}\wedge\mathcal{A}\ =\ \dot{X}_{a}\,e^{0}\wedge e^{a}+\frac{1}{2}\left(L_{[b}\,X_{c]}-2\,\epsilon_{abc}\,X_{a}+\left[X_{b},X_{c}\right]\right)e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\ ,

(2)

where
e^{0}:=\mathrm{d}{\tau}
and the dot on
X_{a}
refers to its derivative w.r.t.
\tau
. The Yang–Mills equation, for this gauge field
{\cal A}
,

\mathrm{d}*\mathcal{F}+{\cal A}\wedge*\mathcal{F}-*\mathcal{F}\wedge\mathcal{A}\ =\ 0\ ,

(3)

after a straightforward calculation, yields the constraint condition

-2\,\mathrm{i}\,J_{a}\,X_{a}+[X_{a},\dot{X}_{a}]\ =\ 0\ ,

(4)

along-with the field equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ddot{X}_{a}=&-4\left(J^{2}{+}1\right)X_{a}-4\,\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,J_{b}\,X_{c}+4\,J_{a}\,J_{b}\,X_{b}+3\,\epsilon_{abc}\,[X_{b},X_{c}]\\
&+2\,\mathrm{i}\,[X_{a},J_{b}\,X_{b}]+2\,\mathrm{i}\,[X_{b},J_{a}\,X_{b}]+4\,\mathrm{i}\,[J_{b}\,X_{a},X_{b}]-[X_{b},[X_{a},X_{b}]]\ ,\end{split}

(5)

where the operators
J_{a}
and
J^{2}
are given by

J_{a}\ :=\ \frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,L_{a}\quad\textrm{and}\quad J^{2}\ :=\ J_{a}\,J_{a}\ .

(6)

Finding a general solution for this equation is a daunting task, but progress can be made in two limiting cases as follows.

One of the limiting case of (
5
) is the Abelian one, where commutators vanish to yield

\ddot{X}_{a}=-4\left(J^{2}{+}1\right)X_{a}+2\,\mathrm{i}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,J_{b}\,X_{c}\ .

(7)

Furthermore the constraint (
4
) in this case becomes

J_{a}\,X_{a}\ =\ 0\ .

(8)

One thing to note here is that the above constraint, along-with the temporal gauge
A_{\tau}\!=0
, is not the usual Coulomb gauge on Minkowski space. In fact, we can make use of the inverse Jacobian (
13
), while promoting the gauge potential to the Minkowski space
{\cal A}\!={\cal A}_{a}\,e^{a}\!=A_{\mu}\,dx^{\mu}\!=A
, to get

0={\cal A}_{\tau}={\cal A}\left(\partial_{\tau}\right)=A\left({\textstyle\frac{\ell^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}}\left(p\,\partial_{t}+q\,\partial_{r}\right)\right)\implies\left(r^{2}+t^{2}+\ell^{2}\right)A_{t}+2\,r\,t\,A_{r}=0\ .

(9)

Solution to (
7
) was obtained in
[
3
]
using hyperspherical harmonics
Y_{j;m,n}
as we will see in the Chapter
4
.

Another limiting case of (
5
) is when, a more symmetric,
SO(4)
-equivariant condition is imposed yielding
[
10
,
7
]
:

X_{a}\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\left(1+\psi(\tau)\right)T_{a}

(10)

with some function
\psi:\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathds{R}
and
SU(2)
generators
T_{a}
satisfying the Lie algebra

[T_{a},T_{b}]=2\,\epsilon_{abc}\,T_{c}\ .

(11)

Moreover, we work in the adjoint representation for the Lie algebra genertors
T_{a}
where
\mathrm{tr}{(T_{a}\,T_{b})}{=}{-8}\,\delta_{ab}
. The constraint (
4
) for this symmetric ansatz, i.e.

[X_{a},\dot{X}_{a}]\ =\ 0

(12)

is automatically satisfied and the field-equation (
5
) becomes

\ddot{X}_{a}=-4\,X_{a}+3\,\epsilon_{abc}\,[X_{b},X_{c}]-[X_{b},[X_{a},X_{b}]]\ .

(13)

Solution to this equation was obtained in
[
27
]
as we will see in Chapter
5
.

Chapter 4
Abelian solutions:
U(1)

In this chapter we present the knotted electromagnetic fields arising via the “de Sitter" method and study its various properties. The content of this chapter has generated three published works:
[
22
,
23
,
28
]
. Section
4
on null solutions is due to Olaf Lechtenfeld with verification by Colin Becker and some clarifications from Harald Skarke. I have been involved at all stages of research works for rest of the sections in this chapter.

1
Family of "knot" solutions

It was shown in
[
3
]
, that the general solution of (
7
) decomposes into spin-
j
representations of
so(4)
and are labelled with hyperspherical harmonics of
S^{3}
. We review the construction of these solutions below in two steps: (a) we first solve (
7
) on the
S^{3}
-cylinder and (b) we then pull these solution back to Minkowski space using
(\tau,\chi)\rightarrow(t,r)
coordinate transformation.

1
Generic solution on the
S^{3}
-cylinder

To solve (
7
) we first write it down in terms of ladder operators
J_{\pm}
and
J_{3}
(
6
) together with the redefined functions

X_{\pm}\ :=\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(X_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}X_{2}\right)

(1)

as follows:

\displaystyle\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}^{2}\,X_{+}

\displaystyle=\ -4\,(J^{2}-J_{3}+1)X_{+}-4\,J_{+}\,X_{3}\ ,

(2)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}^{2}\,X_{3}

\displaystyle=\ -4\,(J^{2}+1)X_{3}+4\,J_{+}\,X_{-}-4\,J_{-}\,X_{+}\ ,

\displaystyle\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}^{2}\,X_{-}

\displaystyle=\ -4\,(J^{2}+J_{3}+1)X_{-}+4\,J_{-}\,X_{3}\ .

Similarly, the constraint condition (
8
) takes becomes

0\ =\ J_{3}\,X_{3}+J_{+}\,X_{-}+J_{-}\,X_{+}\ .

(3)

We can solve (
2
) by the following ansatz

\displaystyle X_{+}

\displaystyle=\ \sum\limits_{j,m,n}Z^{j;m,n}_{+}\,e^{i\Omega_{j,m,n}\tau}\ ;\quad Z^{j;m,n}_{+}\ =\ c_{+}\,Y_{j;m,n+1}\ ,

(4)

\displaystyle X_{3}

\displaystyle=\ \sum\limits_{j,m,n}Z^{j;m,n}_{3}\,e^{i\Omega_{j,m,n}\tau}\ ;\quad Z^{j;m,n}_{3}\ =\ c_{3}\,Y_{j;m,n}\ ,\quad\textrm{and}\quad

\displaystyle X_{-}

\displaystyle=\ \sum\limits_{j,m,n}Z^{j;m,n}_{-}\,e^{i\Omega_{j,m,n}\tau}\ ;\quad Z^{j;m,n}_{-}\ =\ c_{-}\,Y_{j;m,n-1}\ ,

where
X_{*}(j,m,n)
with
*\in\{+,3,-\}
has been expanded in terms of
S^{3}
-harmonics. Plugging this ansatz back in (
2
) and using (
30
) we find, for every mode
(j,m,n)
, an eigenvalue equation for the vector
(c_{+}\ c_{3}\ c_{-})^{T}
:

\displaystyle M\begin{pmatrix}c_{+}\\
c_{3}\\
c_{-}\end{pmatrix}\ =\ \Omega(j,m,n)^{2}\begin{pmatrix}c_{+}\\
c_{3}\\
c_{-}\end{pmatrix}\ ,\ \quad\textrm{with}\quad

(5)

\displaystyle M\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}4(j^{2}+j-n)&2\sqrt{2(j-n)(j+n+1)}&0\\
2\sqrt{2(j-n)(j+n+1)}&4(j^{2}+j+1)&-2\sqrt{2(j+n)(j-n+1)}\\
0&-2\sqrt{2(j+n)(j-n+1)}&4(j^{2}+j+n)\end{pmatrix}\ ,

which admits an eigensystem with
3
distinct eigenvalues
\Omega_{j}^{2}
(that turns out to be independent of
m
and
n
) and their corresponding eigenvectors. One of these eigenvectors does not satisfy the constraint (
3
) and is, therefore, discarded. We label the remaining two eigensystems as type I, with eigenfrequency
\Omega_{j}^{2}=4(j{+}1)^{2}
, and type II, with eigenfrequency
\Omega_{j}^{2}=4\,j^{2}
, as follows

•

type I :
j{\geq}0\ ,\quad m=-j,\ldots,+j\ ,\quad n=-j{-}1,\ldots,j{+}1\ ,\ \Omega_{j}=\pm 2\,(j{+}1)\ ,

\displaystyle Z_{+\ \textrm{I}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{(j{-}n)(j{-}n{+}1)/2}\ \ Y_{j;m,n+1}(\omega)\ ,

(6)

\displaystyle Z_{3\ \textrm{I}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{(j{+}1)^{2}-n^{2}}\ \ Y_{j;m,n}(\omega)\ ,

\displaystyle Z_{-\ \textrm{I}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ -\sqrt{(j{+}n)(j{+}n{+}1)/2}\ \ Y_{j;m,n-1}(\omega)\ .

•

type II :
j{\geq}1\ ,\quad m=-j,\ldots,+j\ ,\quad n=-j{+}1,\ldots,j{-}1\ ,\ \Omega_{j}=\pm 2\,j\ ,

\displaystyle Z_{+\ \textrm{II}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ -\sqrt{(j{+}n)(j{+}n{+}1)/2}\ \ Y_{j;m,n+1}(\omega)\ ,

(7)

\displaystyle Z_{3\ \textrm{II}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{j^{2}-n^{2}}\ \ Y_{j;m,n}(\omega)\ ,

\displaystyle Z_{-\ \textrm{II}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ \sqrt{(j{-}n)(j{-}n{+}1)/2}\ \ Y_{j;m,n-1}(\omega)\ .

We can take a linear combination of these basis solutions and write down a real-valued connection
1
-form as

{\cal A}\ =\ \Bigl\{\sum_{2j=0}^{\infty}X_{a\ \textrm{I}}^{j}(\omega)\ \mathrm{e}^{2(j+1)\mathrm{i}\tau}\ +\ \textrm{c.c.}\Bigr\}e^{a}\ +\ \Bigl\{\sum_{2j=2}^{\infty}X_{a\ \textrm{II}}^{j}(\omega)\ \mathrm{e}^{2j\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\ +\ \textrm{c.c.}\Bigr\}e^{a}

(8)

where we have reorganized the complex angular functions
X_{a}^{j}
as

X_{1}^{j}={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\bigl(Z_{+}^{j}+Z_{-}^{j}\bigr)\ ,\qquad X_{2}^{j}={\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}}\bigl(Z_{-}^{j}-Z_{+}^{j}\bigr)\ ,\qquad X_{3}^{j}=Z_{3}^{j}

(9)

for both types and expanded the functions
Z^{j}_{\pm}
and
Z^{j}_{3}
into the above spin-
j
basis solutions of type I (
6
) and type II (
7
) (for
*\in\{+,3,-\}
),

\displaystyle Z_{*\ \textrm{I}}^{j}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{m=-j}^{j}\sum_{n=-j-1}^{j+1}\lambda_{j;m,n}^{\textrm{I}}\ Z_{*\ \textrm{I}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)\ ,

(10)

\displaystyle Z_{*\ \textrm{II}}^{j}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{m=-j}^{j}\sum_{n=-j+1}^{j-1}\lambda_{j;m,n}^{\textrm{II}}\ Z_{*\ \textrm{II}}^{(j;m,n)}(\omega)

with
(2j{+}1)(2j{+}3)
arbitrary complex coefficients
\lambda^{\textrm{I}}_{j;m,n}

and
(2j{+}1)(2j{-}1)
coefficients
\lambda^{\textrm{II}}_{j;m,n}
1
1

1

Note that type-II solutions are absent for
j{=}0
and
j{=}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}
.
.

Inserting (
6
) and (
7
) into (
10
) and the resulting expression into (
9
) provides a harmonic expansion

X_{a}^{j}(\omega)\ =\ \sum_{m=-j}^{j}\sum_{n=-j}^{j}X_{a}^{j;m,n}\ Y_{j;m,n}(\omega)

(11)

for both types of angular functions in (
8
)
2
2

2

Note the different range of
n
for
X_{a}^{j;m,n}
and
Z_{*}^{j;m,n}
; they are not easily related as
X_{a}^{j}
and
Z_{*}^{j}
are in (
9
).
.

It is useful for later purposes to introduce here the “sphere-frame" electric
{\cal E}_{a}
and magnetic
{\cal B}_{a}
fields,

{\cal F}\ =\ {\cal E}_{a}\,e^{a}{\wedge}e^{\tau}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\,{\cal B}_{a}\,\varepsilon^{a}_{\ bc}\,e^{b}{\wedge}e^{c}\ .

(12)

For a fixed type (I or II) and spin
j
, we may eliminate
L_{[b}A_{c]}
in (
2
) (without the commutator term) by using (
7
) and employ

\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}^{2}{\cal A}^{(j)}=-\Omega_{j}^{2}\,{\cal A}^{(j)}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad L^{2}\,{\cal A}^{(j)}\ =\ -4j(j{+}1)\,{\cal A}^{(j)}

(13)

to obtain

{\cal E}_{a}^{(j)}\ =\ -\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}X_{a}^{(j)}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad{\cal B}_{a}^{(j)}\ =\ \mp\Omega_{j}\,X_{a}^{(j)}\ ,

(14)

where the upper sign pertains to type I and the lower one to type II.
We note in passing that, due to the compactness of the Lorentzian cylinder, the sphere-frame energy and action are always finite.

Due to the linearity of Maxwell theory, the overall scale of any solution is arbitrary.
Furthermore, the parity transformation
L\leftrightarrow R
—that corresponds to
m\leftrightarrow n
—interchanges a spin-
j
solution of type I with a spin-
(j{+}1)
solution of type II.
Finally, electromagnetic duality at fixed
j
is realized by shifting
\Omega_{j}\tau
by
{\textstyle\frac{\pi}{2}}
for type I
or by
-{\textstyle\frac{\pi}{2}}
for type II, which maps
{\cal A}
to a dual configuration
{\cal A}_{\textrm{D}}

and likewise
{\cal F}
to
{\cal F}_{\textrm{D}}
.

2
Pulling the solution back to Minkowski space

We have completely solved the vacuum Maxwell equations on the Lorentzian cylinder
{\cal I}\times S^{3}
, which, by conformal invariance, carries over to any conformally equivalent spacetime including
de Sitter space dS
4
and Minkowski space
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
. We can translate our Maxwell solutions from
\widetilde{{\cal I}}\times S^{3}
to
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
simply by the coordinate change

\displaystyle\tau=\tau(t,x,y,z)

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad\omega_{{}_{A}}=\omega_{{}_{A}}(t,x,y,z)

(15)

\displaystyle\textrm{or}\qquad\tau=\tau(t,r)

\displaystyle\textrm{and}\quad\chi=\chi(t,r)\ .

In other words, abbreviating
x\equiv\{x^{\mu}\}
and
y\equiv\{y^{\rho}\}
and expanding

\displaystyle{\cal A}

\displaystyle=\ X_{a}\bigl(\tau(x),g(x)\bigr)\,e^{a}(x)\ =\ A_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\ =\ A_{\rho}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y^{\rho}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad

(16)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{\cal A}

\displaystyle=\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}X_{a}\,e^{0}{\wedge}e^{a}+\bigl(L_{b}\,X_{c}-X_{a}\,\varepsilon^{a}_{\ bc}\bigr)\,e^{b}{\wedge}e^{c}

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}F_{\mu\nu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}{\wedge}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}F_{\rho\lambda}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y^{\rho}{\wedge}\mathrm{d}y^{\lambda}

using (
38
) we may read off
A_{\mu}
(note that
A_{t}\neq 0
, as discussed before) and

F_{\mu\nu}
and thus the electric and magnetic fields

{\cal F}\ =\ F\ =\ E_{a}\,\mathrm{d}{x}^{a}\wedge\mathrm{d}{t}+\frac{1}{2}B_{a}\,\varepsilon^{a}_{\ bc}\,\mathrm{d}{x}^{b}{\wedge}\mathrm{d}{x}^{c}

(17)

in Cartesian or in spherical coordinates. In general, the knotted electromagnetic fields arising from the basis configurations (
6
) are complex, and thus the basis configurations on Minkowski space will also be complex. Hence, they combine two physical solutions, namely the real and imaginary parts
3
3

3

The notation should not be confused with the type I configuration (
6
).
, which we denote as

(j;m,n)_{R}\penalty\ \textrm{configuration}\quad\textrm{and}\quad(j;m,n)_{I}\penalty\ \textrm{configuration}\ ,

respectively. Such knotted electromagnetic fields (
17
) for the basis configurations (
6
) and (
7
) increase in complexity with increasing
j
, as shown in Figure
1
.

Furthermore, it comes in handy that
{\cal A}
, after computation, contains only even powers of
\gamma

and depends on
\tau
only through integral powers of

\exp(2\mathrm{i}\,\tau)\ =\ \frac{[(\ell+\mathrm{i}t)^{2}+r^{2}]^{2}}{4\,\ell^{2}t^{2}+(r^{2}-t^{2}+\ell^{2})^{2}}\ .

(18)

Therefore, our Minkowski solutions have the remarkable property of being rational functions of
(t,x,y,z)
.
More precisely, their electric and magnetic fields are of the form

\textrm{type I:}\quad\frac{P_{2(2j+1)}(x)}{Q_{2(2j+3)}(x)}\ ,\qquad\textrm{type II:}\quad\frac{P_{2(2j-1)}(x)}{Q_{2(2j+1)}(x)}

(19)

where
P_{r}
and
Q_{r}
denote polynomials of degree
r
.
Thus, as expected, their energy and action are finite.
Indeed, the fields fall off like
r^{-4}
at spatial infinity for fixed time, but they decay
merely like
(t{\pm}r)^{-1}
along the light-cone.
Hence, the asymptotic energy flow is concentrated on past and future null infinity
\mathscr{I}^{\pm}
,
as it should be, but peaks on the light-cone of the spacetime origin.
Since our basis solutions (
6
) and (
7
) form a complete set on de Sitter space, their Minkowski relatives are also complete on the space of finite-action configurations.

Figure 1
:

Electric (red) and magnetic (green) field lines at
t{=}0
with 4 fixed seed points.
Left:
(j;m,n)=(0;0,1)_{I}
configuration, Center:
(j;m,n)=({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}};-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},{\textstyle\frac{3}{2}})_{R}
configuration, Right:
(j;m,n)=(1;1,2)_{I}
configuration. More self-knotted field lines start appearing with additional seed points in the simulation.

For illustration, we display a type I basis solution with
(j;m,n)=(1;0,0)

obtained from

X_{\pm}\ \propto\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\,(\omega_{1}{\pm}\mathrm{i}\omega_{2})(\omega_{3}{\pm}\mathrm{i}\omega_{4})\,\cos 4\tau\quad\textrm{and}\quad X_{3}\ \propto\ (\omega_{1}^{2}{+}\omega_{2}^{2}{-}\omega_{3}^{2}{-}\omega_{4}^{2})\,\cos 4\tau\ .

(20)

It bodes well to combine these field configurations into the Riemann–Silberstein vector

{\bf S}\ =\ {\bf E}+\mathrm{i}{\bf B}

(21)

whose components, for (
20
), read (up to some overall scale)

\displaystyle S_{x}

\displaystyle=\ -\frac{2\mathrm{i}}{N}\Bigl\{2y+3\mathrm{i}ty-xz+2t^{2}y+2\mathrm{i}txz-8x^{2}y-8y^{3}+4yz^{2}+4\mathrm{i}t^{3}y

(22)

\displaystyle-6t^{2}xz-8\mathrm{i}tx^{2}y-8\mathrm{i}ty^{3}+4\mathrm{i}tyz^{2}+10x^{3}z+10xy^{2}z-2xz^{3}

\displaystyle+2(\mathrm{i}txz+yr^{2})(-t^{2}{+}r^{2})+(\mathrm{i}ty-xz)(-t^{2}{+}r^{2})^{2}\Bigr\}\ ,\hskip 9.24994pt{}

\displaystyle S_{y}

\displaystyle=\ \frac{2\mathrm{i}}{N}\Bigl\{2x+3\mathrm{i}tx+yz+2t^{2}x-2\mathrm{i}tyz-8x^{3}-8xy^{2}+4xz^{2}+4\mathrm{i}t^{3}x

(23)

\displaystyle+6t^{2}yz-8\mathrm{i}tx^{3}-8\mathrm{i}txy^{2}+4\mathrm{i}txz^{2}-10x^{2}yz-10y^{3}z+2yz^{3}

\displaystyle+2(-\mathrm{i}tyz{+}xr^{2})(-t^{2}{+}r^{2})+(\mathrm{i}tx+yz)(-t^{2}{+}r^{2})^{2}\Bigr\}\ ,\hskip 18.49988pt\&

\displaystyle S_{z}

\displaystyle=\ \frac{\mathrm{i}}{N}\Bigl\{1+2\mathrm{i}t+t^{2}-11x^{2}-11y^{2}+3z^{2}+4\mathrm{i}t^{3}-16\mathrm{i}tx^{2}-16\mathrm{i}ty^{2}+4\mathrm{i}tz^{2}-t^{4}

(24)

\displaystyle-2t^{2}x^{2}-2t^{2}y^{2}-2t^{2}z^{2}+11x^{4}+22x^{2}y^{2}-10x^{2}z^{2}+11y^{4}-10y^{2}z^{2}

\displaystyle+3z^{4}+2\mathrm{i}t(t^{2}{-}3r^{2}{+}2z^{2})(t^{2}{-}r^{2})-(t^{2}{+}r^{2})(-t^{2}{+}r^{2})^{2}\Bigr\}

with
N=\bigl((t{-}\mathrm{i})^{2}-r^{2}\bigr)^{5}
. We can also obtain electromagnetic field configurations corresponding to the gauge field (
8
) that consists of complex linear combinations of these basis solutions. We demonstrate this for the famous Hopf–Ranãda field configuration, which was first discovered by Ranãda in 1989
[
1
]
using the Hopf fibration. The Riemann–Silberstein vector
{\bf S}
for this field configuration is given by
[
3
]

{\bf S}\ =\ \frac{1}{((t{-}\mathrm{i})^{2}{-}r^{2})^{3}}\begin{pmatrix}(x{-}\mathrm{i}\,y)^{2}{-}(t{-}\mathrm{i}{-}z)^{2}\\
\mathrm{i}(x{-}\mathrm{i}\,y)^{2}{+}\mathrm{i}(t{-}\mathrm{i}{-}z)^{2}\\
-2(x{-}\mathrm{i}\,y)(t{-}\mathrm{i}{-}z)\end{pmatrix}\ .

(25)

We find that this solution, in our construction, is related to
(0,0,1)_{I}
basis configurations and is obtained by using following coefficients in (
10
)

\lambda^{I}_{0;0,-1}\ =\ 0\ ,\quad\lambda^{I}_{0;0,0}\ =\ 0\ ,\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lambda^{I}_{0;0,1}\ =\ \frac{\pi}{4}\ .

(26)

Moreover, we find that some of our basis configurations are related to the
(p,q)
-torus knots arising from Bateman’s construction
[
2
]
. We illustrate this point in Figure
1
where we find the following correspondences:

\textrm{Hopfion}\leftrightarrow(1,1)\ ,\qquad({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},{\textstyle\frac{3}{2}})_{R}\leftrightarrow(2,1)\ ,\qquad(1,1,2)_{I}\leftrightarrow(1,3)\ .

(27)

2
Symmetry analysis

The main advantage of constructing Minkowski-space electromagnetic field configurations
via the detour over de Sitter space is the enhanced manifest symmetry of our construction.
The isometry group SO(1,4) of dS
4
is generated by
(
A,B=1,2,3,4
and
a,b,c=1,2,3
, abbreviate
\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{{}_{B}}}\equiv\partial_{{}_{B}}
)

\{{\cal M}_{{}_{AB}}{\equiv}\,{-}q_{[{\scriptscriptstyle A}}\partial_{{\scriptscriptstyle B}]}\,,\ {\cal M}_{0{\scriptscriptstyle B}}{\equiv}\,q_{({\scriptstyle 0}}\partial_{{\scriptscriptstyle B})}\}\ =\ \{{\cal M}_{ab}{=}\varepsilon_{abc}{\cal D}_{c}\,,\ {\cal M}_{4a}{=}{\cal P}_{a}\,,\ {\cal M}_{04}{=}{\cal P}_{0}\,,\ {\cal M}_{0b}{=}{\cal K}_{b}\}\ ,

(1)

which can be contracted (with
\ell{\to}\infty
) to the isometry group ISO(1,3) of
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
(the Poincaré group)
generated by (
\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3
and
i,j,k=1,2,3
)

\{M_{\mu\nu}\,,\ P_{\mu}\}\ =\ \{M_{ij}{=}\varepsilon_{ijk}D_{k}\,,\ P_{i}\,,\ P_{0}\,,\ M_{0j}{=}K_{j}\}\ ,

(2)

where the two sets are ordered likewise,
and we employ (as aleady earlier) calligraphic symbols for de Sitter quantities and straight symbols for Minkowskian ones.
Here,
D
denotes spatial rotations,
P
are translations, and
K
stand for boosts in Minkowski space.

Since the two spaces are conformally equivalent already at
\ell{<}\infty
via (
6
),
the corresponding generators should be related. Indeed, the common SO(3) subgroup in

SO(1,4)\supset SO(4)\supset SO(3)\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad ISO(1,3)\supset SO(1,3)\supset SO(3)

(3)

is identified,
{\cal D}_{i}{=}D_{i}{=}{-}2\varepsilon_{ij}^{\ k}x^{j}\partial_{k}
.
However, any other generator becomes nonlinearly realized when
mapped to the other space via (
12
).
For example, the would-be translation
{\cal P}_{3}
defined in (
26
) reads

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{3}=L_{3}-R_{3}

\displaystyle=\ -2\,\cos\theta\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\chi}+2\,\cot\chi\sin\theta\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\theta}

(4)

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{\ell}}\,\cos\theta\,\bigl(2\,r\,t\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{t}+(t^{2}{+}r^{2}{+}\ell^{2})\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{r}\bigr)-{\textstyle\frac{1}{\ell\,r}}(t^{2}{-}r^{2}{+}\ell^{2})\,\sin\theta\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\theta}

\displaystyle\to\ 2\ell\,\bigl(\cos\theta\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{r}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{r}}\,\sin\theta\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\theta}\bigr)\ =\ 2\ell\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{z}\ =\ \ell\,P_{z}\quad\quad\textrm{for}\quad\ell\to\infty

as it should be. Similarly,
{\cal P}_{0}\to\ell\,P_{0}
and
{\cal K}_{b}\to K_{j}
for
\ell{\to}\infty

when expanded around
(t,r)=(\ell,0)
corresponding to the
S^{3}
south pole at
q_{0}{=}0
.
Nevertheless, the de Sitter construction enjoys an SO(4) covariance (generated by
{\cal D}_{a}
and
{\cal P}_{a}
)
which extends the obvious SO(3) covariance in Minkowski space.
It allows us to connect all solutions of a given type (I or II) with a fixed value of the spin
j

by the action of SO(4) ladder operators
L_{\pm}
and
R_{\pm}
or
{\cal D}_{\pm}
and
{\cal P}_{\pm}
,
which is non-obvious on the Minkowski side.
On the other hand, Minkowski boosts and translations have no simple realization on de Sitter space.

Actually, Maxwell theory on either space is also invariant under conformal transformations.
These may be generated by the isometry group together with a conformal inversion.
On the Minkowski side, the latter is

J:\quad x^{\mu}\ \mapsto\ \frac{x^{\mu}}{x\cdot x}\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad x\cdot x=r^{2}-t^{2}\ .

(5)

We have to distinguish two cases:

\displaystyle\textrm{spacelike:}

\displaystyle t^{2}<r^{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad J_{>}:\quad\bigl(t,r,\theta,\phi\bigr)\ \mapsto\ \bigl({\textstyle\frac{t}{r^{2}-t^{2}}},{\textstyle\frac{r}{r^{2}-t^{2}}},\theta,\phi\bigr)\ ,

(6)

\displaystyle\textrm{timelike:}

\displaystyle t^{2}>r^{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad J_{<}:\quad\bigl(t,r,\theta,\phi\bigr)\ \mapsto\ \bigl({\textstyle\frac{-t}{t^{2}-r^{2}}},{\textstyle\frac{r}{t^{2}-r^{2}}},\pi{-}\theta,\phi{+}\pi\bigr)\ .

On the de Sitter side, this is either (spacelike) a reflection on the
S^{3}
equator
\chi{=}\frac{\pi}{2}

or (timelike) a
\pi
-shift in cylinder time
\tau
plus an
S^{2}
antipodal flip,

\displaystyle\textrm{spacelike:}

\displaystyle|\tau|{+}\chi<\pi\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\cal J}_{>}:\quad\bigl(\tau,\chi,\theta,\phi\bigr)\ \mapsto\ \bigl(\tau,\pi{-}\chi,\theta,\phi\bigr)\ ,

(7)

\displaystyle\textrm{timelike:}

\displaystyle|\tau|{+}\chi>\pi\quad\Rightarrow\quad{\cal J}_{<}:\quad\bigl(\tau,\chi,\theta,\phi\bigr)\ \mapsto\ \bigl(\tau{\pm}\pi,\chi,\pi{-}\theta,\phi{+}\pi\bigr)\ .

In the spacelike case, merely the sign of
\omega_{4}{\equiv}\cos\chi
gets flipped,
which amounts to a parity flip
L\leftrightarrow R
.
In the timelike case, both
\cos\tau
and
\sin\tau
change sign,
which combines a time reversal with a reflection at
\tau{=}\frac{\pi}{2}
or
\tau{=}{-}\frac{\pi}{2}
.
Note that it is different from the
S^{3}
antipodal map, which
is not a reflection but a proper rotation,
\omega_{{}_{A}}\mapsto-\omega_{{}_{A}}
or

(\chi,\theta,\phi)\mapsto(\pi{-}\chi,\pi{-}\theta,\phi{+}\pi)
.
The lightcone is singular under the inversion; it is mapped to the conformal boundary

r{=}{\pm}t{=}\infty
or
\chi{=}{\pm}\tau
.
We infer that the conformal inversion allows us to relate type-I and type-II solutions of the same spin.
It is easily checked that the spatial fall-off behavior of our rational solutions is not modified by the inversion.

Finally, one may consider dilatations in Minkowski space,

x^{\mu}\ \mapsto\ \lambda\,x^{\mu}\quad\quad\textrm{for}\quad\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{+}\ .

(8)

However, this amounts to a trivial rescaling also achieved by changing the de Sitter radius,

\ell\mapsto\lambda\,\ell
, as the scale
\ell
was removed on the Lorentzian cylinder.

3
Conformal group and Noether charges

It is well known
[
29
]
that free Maxwell theory on
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
arising from the action

S\left[A_{\mu}\right]\ =\ \int d^{4}x\,\mathcal{L}\ ;\qquad\mathcal{L}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}

(1)

is invariant under the conformal group
SO(2,4)
. Furthermore, the above action is also invariant under the gauge transformations:
A_{\mu}(x)\rightarrow A_{\mu}(x)+\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\lambda(x)
. The conformal group is generated by transformations
x^{\mu}\rightarrow x^{\mu}+\zeta^{\mu}(x)
, where the vector fields
\zeta^{\mu}
obey the conformal Killing equations:

\zeta_{\mu,\nu}\,+\,\zeta_{\nu,\mu}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\,\eta_{\mu\nu}\,\zeta^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ ,\alpha}\quad\textrm{with}\quad\{\eta_{\mu\nu}\}\ =\ \textrm{diag}(-1,1,1,1)\ .

(2)

The conserved Noether current
J^{\mu}
is obtained by equating the “on-shell variation" of the action where the variations
\delta A_{\mu}
are arbitrary and the fields
A_{\mu}
satisfies the Euler-Langrange equations with its “symmetry variation" where the fields
A_{\mu}
are arbitrary but variations
\delta A_{\mu}
satisfy the symmetry condition. The correct variation
\delta A_{\mu}
is obtained by imposing the gauge invariance on the Lie derivative of
A_{\mu}
w.r.t. the vector field
\zeta^{\mu}
:

\mathcal{L}_{\zeta^{\alpha}}A_{\mu}:=A^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)-A_{\mu}(x)=-\zeta^{\alpha}\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\alpha}A_{\mu}-A_{\alpha}\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\zeta^{\alpha}\penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \delta A_{\mu}\ =\ F_{\mu\nu}\zeta^{\nu}\ .

(3)

Finally, the conserved current is obtained as

J^{\mu}\ =\ \frac{\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{L}}{\mbox{$\partial$}A_{\rho,\mu}}\delta A_{\rho}+\zeta^{\mu}\mathcal{L}\ =\ \zeta^{\rho}\left(F^{\mu\alpha}F_{\rho\alpha}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}F^{2}\right)\quad\textrm{with}\quad F^{2}=F_{\beta\gamma}F^{\beta\gamma}\ ,

(4)

which satisfies the continuity equation and gives the conserved (in time) charge
Q
4
4

4

For source-free fields the current
J
is assumed to vanish when the surface
\mbox{$\partial$}V
is taken to infinity.
:

\mbox{$\partial$}_{\mu}\,J^{\mu}=0\quad\implies\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}Q}{\mathrm{d}t}\ =\ -\int_{\scriptsize\mbox{$\partial$}V}\mathrm{d}^{2}{\bf s}\cdot{\bf J}\ =\ 0\ ;\quad Q\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,J^{0}\ .

(5)

Before proceeding further, a couple of remarks pertaining to the subsequent calculations are in order:

•

All the charges
Q
are computed at
t{=}0
owing to the simple
J^{0}
expressions on this time-slice. To that end, we record the following useful identities at
t{=}\tau{=}0
:

\displaystyle e^{a}_{i}

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{\ell}}\left(\gamma\,\omega_{4}\,\delta^{a}_{i}-\omega_{a}\,\omega_{i}+\epsilon_{aic}\,\gamma\,\omega_{c}\right)\ ,\quad e^{a}_{i}\,e^{b}_{i}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{2}}}\delta^{ab}

(6)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\ 1-\omega_{4}\ ,\penalty\ \mathrm{d}^{3}x\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{\ell^{3}}{\gamma^{3}}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\ \penalty\ ;\penalty\ \mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}:=e^{1}\wedge e^{2}\wedge e^{3}\ .

Moreover, the electromagnetic fields at
t{=}0
are given in terms of tetrads
e^{\tau}{=}e^{\tau}_{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}
and
e^{a}{=}e^{a}_{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}
as follows:

E_{i}\ =\ e^{\tau}_{0}\,e^{a}_{i}\,\mathcal{E}_{a}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad B_{i}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\,\epsilon_{ijk}\,\epsilon_{abc}\,e^{b}_{j}\,e^{c}_{k}\,\mathcal{B}_{a}\

(7)

using the
2
-form (
12
) and its Minkowski counterpart (
17
).

•

The charges are computed for type I (
6
) solutions only (except for the energy and the related helicity) and we relabel the complex coefficients (
10
)
\lambda^{I}_{j;m,n}
as
\Lambda_{j;m,n}
for convenience.

•

Furthermore, these charges
Q
are computed for a fixed spin-
j
and, thus, we will suppress the index
j
from now onward, unless necessary. Note that the sphere-frame EM fields for fixed
j
can be obtained by using the expansion (
8
) (for type I only) in (
14
) as

{\cal A}_{a}\ =\ X_{a}(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}+\bar{X}_{a}(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\Rightarrow\biggl\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\cal E}_{a}\ =\ -\mathrm{i}\,\Omega\,X_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}+\mathrm{i}\,\Omega\,\bar{X}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\\[2.0pt]
{\cal B}_{a}\ =\ \,-\,\Omega\,X_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\,-\,\Omega\,\bar{X}_{a}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\end{array}\biggr\}

(8)

with
\bar{X}_{a}
denoting the complex conjugate of
X_{a}
. Notice that for type II the overall sign in
{\cal B}_{a}
flips.

•

The simplifications below for the charge density
J^{0}
are carried out using the harmonic expansion (
11
) for the type I solution (
6
).

•

We frequently use below the well known fact that an odd
\{\omega_{{}_{A}}\}
integral over
S^{3}
vanishes because of the opposite contributions coming from the antipodal points on the sphere. In particular, it can be checked that the following integral vanishes
5
5

5

Note that the power of
\omega_{{}_{A}}
in
Y_{j;m,n}\bar{Y}_{j;m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}
is always even. One way to check this is by employing the toroidal coordinates:
\omega_{1}=\cos\eta\cos\kappa_{1},\omega_{2}=\cos\eta\sin\kappa_{1},\omega_{3}=\sin\eta\cos\kappa_{2},\omega_{4}=\sin\eta\sin\kappa_{2}
with
\eta\in(0,{\textstyle\frac{\pi}{2}})
and
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,2\pi)
in (
31
). The resultant selection rules coming from
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
integral would yield
m-m^{\prime}\in{\textstyle\frac{2k+1}{2}}
with
k\in\mathds{N}_{0}
, which is not feasible for fixed
j
.
:

\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,(\omega_{1})^{a}\,(\omega_{2})^{b}\,(\omega_{3})^{c}\,(\omega_{4})^{d}\,Y_{j;m,n}\,\overline{Y}_{j;m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\ =\ 0\quad\textrm{for}\quad a+b+c+d\ \in\ 2\mathds{N}_{0}+1\ .

(9)

Having made these remarks, we now proceed to compute the charges
Q
for various conformal transformations
\zeta^{\mu}
obeying (
2
) in following four categories.

1
Translations

An easily seen solution to (
2
) is the set of four constant translations

\zeta^{\mu}=\epsilon^{\mu}\ ,

(10)

which also partly generates the Poincaré group and give rise to the usual stress-energy tensor of electrodynamics

J^{\mu}\ =\ T^{\mu}_{\ \;\nu}\ =\ F^{\mu\alpha}F_{\nu\alpha}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}F^{2}\ ,

(11)

corresponding to the
\mu
-component of the translation for an arbitrary
\epsilon^{\nu}
. The corresponding charges are the energy
E
and the momentum
P
.

Energy.
The expression of the energy density
e:=T^{00}
simplifies to

e\,:=\,\frac{1}{2}\left(E_{i}^{2}+B_{i}^{2}\right)\ =\ \left(\frac{\gamma}{\ell}\right)^{4}\,\rho\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad\quad\rho\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\left({\cal E}_{a}^{2}+{\cal B}_{a}^{2}\right)\ ,

(12)

which, in turn, simplifies the expression for the energy
E
to

E\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\;e\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2\ell}}\int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\ (1-\cos\chi)\,\bigl({\cal E}_{a}^{2}+{\cal B}_{a}^{2}\bigr)\ .

(13)

Notice here that the orientation of the
S^{3}
volume measure
\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}
is chosen to provide a positive result. From (
8
) we find that the “sphere-frame” energy density is time-independent and has similar expression for both solution types (with appropriate eigenfrequency
\Omega
):

{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\bigl({\cal E}_{a}^{2}+{\cal B}_{a}^{2}\bigr)\ =\ 2\,\Omega^{2}\,X_{a}\bar{X}_{a}(\omega)\ .

(14)

The resultant expression for
E
in terms of complex parameters
\lambda_{m,n}
(for both solution types) is given by

E\ =\ \frac{1}{\ell}\,(2j+1)\,\Omega^{3}\sum_{m,n}\,|\lambda_{m,n}|^{2}\ .

(15)

Notice again here that for type I solutions we would have
\Lambda_{m,n}
in the above expression.

Helicity.
Although helicity is not a Noether charge of the conformal group, it is nevertheless a conserved quantity for the Maxwell system and turns out to be related to the energy. The expression for the helicity is metric-free and can thus be evaluated over any spatial slice.
Choosing again
t{=}\tau{=}0
,

h=h_{\textrm{mag}}+h_{\textrm{el}}\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathds{R}^{3}}\ \bigl(A\wedge F+A_{D}\wedge F_{D}\bigr)\ =\ -\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\ (1-\cos\chi)\,\bigl({\cal A}_{a}{\cal B}_{a}+{\cal A}^{D}_{a}{\cal E}_{a}\bigr)\ ,

(16)

where the subscript/superscript
`D^{\prime}
refers to the dual fields. Once again, taking type I (upper sign) or type II (lower sign) and fixing the spin
j
we obtain

{\cal A}^{D}_{a}\ =\ \pm\mathrm{i}\,X_{a}(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\mp\mathrm{i}\,\bar{X}_{a}(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\ ,

(17)

which yields a constant “sphere-frame” helicity density

-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\bigl({\cal A}_{a}{\cal B}_{a}+{\cal A}^{D}_{a}{\cal E}_{a}\bigr)\ =\ \pm 2\,\Omega\,X_{a}\bar{X}_{a}(\omega)\ .

(18)

As a result, even before performing the
S^{3}
integration, we find a linear helicity-energy relation

\Omega\,h\ =\ \pm\ell\,E\qquad\textrm{for fixed spin and type}\ .

(19)

Since the helicity measure an average of the linking numbers of any two electric or magnetic field lines
[
30
,
31
,
32
]
,
the latter must be related to the value
j
of the spin. The individual linking number of two field lines, however, appears neither to be independent of the lines chosen nor constant in time, as our observations indicate.
An exception are the Rañada–Hopf knots discussed before,
which display a conserved linking number of unity between any pair of electric or magnetic field lines.

Momentum.
For the momentum densities
p_{i}=T^{0i}=(\textbf{E}\times\textbf{B})_{i}
we obtain an interesting correspondence relating the one-form
p:=p_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}
on Minkowski space with a similar one on de Sitter space:

p\ =\ ({\textstyle\frac{\gamma}{\ell}})^{3}\,\mathcal{P}_{a}\,e^{a}\ =:\ ({\textstyle\frac{\gamma}{\ell}})^{3}\,\mathcal{P}\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad\quad\mathcal{P}_{a}\ :=\ \varepsilon_{abc}\,\mathcal{E}_{b}\,\mathcal{B}_{c}\ .

(20)

A straightforward calculation then yields the expression of momenta
P_{i}
:

P_{i}\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,p_{i}\ =\ \int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,\mathcal{P}_{a}\,e^{a}_{i}\ =\ 2\mathrm{i}\ \Omega^{2}\ \varepsilon_{abc}\ \int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,e^{a}_{i}\,X_{b}\,\bar{X}_{c}

(21)

with
e^{a}_{i}
given by (
6
). The results for
j=0
are

\displaystyle P_{1}^{(j=0)}

\displaystyle=\ -{\textstyle\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\ell}}\left(\left(\bar{\Lambda}_{0,-1}+\bar{\Lambda}_{0,1}\right)\Lambda_{0,0}+\bar{\Lambda}_{0,0}\left(\Lambda_{0,-1}+\Lambda_{0,1}\right)\right)\ ,

(22)

\displaystyle P_{2}^{(j=0)}

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{2}}{\ell}}\left(\left(-\bar{\Lambda}_{0,-1}+\bar{\Lambda}_{0,1}\right)\Lambda_{0,0}+\bar{\Lambda}_{0,0}\left(\Lambda_{0,-1}-\Lambda_{0,1}\right)\right)\ ,

\displaystyle P_{3}^{(j=0)}

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{2}{\ell}}\left(|\Lambda_{0,-1}|^{2}-|\Lambda_{0,1}|^{2}\right)\ .

As a consistency requirement, we check that the vector charges
P_{i}
are rotated according to the algebra of
\mathcal{D}_{a}
(
26

8
):

\mathcal{D}_{a}\,P_{b}\ =\ 2\,\varepsilon_{abc}\,P_{c}\ .

(23)

\displaystyle+(\cos\theta\sin\phi+\cot\chi\cos\phi)\,\mathcal{P}_{2}-\sin\theta\,\mathcal{P}_{3}\Big)\ .
