2
Lorentz transformations

Another solution of (
2
) is given by

\zeta^{\mu}(x)\ =\ \epsilon^{\mu}_{\ \;\nu}\,x^{\nu}\quad\textrm{where}\quad\epsilon_{\mu\nu}\ =\ -\epsilon_{\nu\mu}\ ,

(26)

which correspond to the six generators of the Lorentz group
SO(1,3)
. These six together with the above four translations generates the full Poincaré group. The corresponding six charges are grouped into the boost
{\bf K}
and the angular momentum
{\bf L}
.

Boost.
The conserved charge densities arising from
J^{0}
(
4
) corresponding to
\epsilon_{0i}
are the boost densities
{\bf k}=\rho\ {\bf x}-{\bf p}\ t
, which simplify for
t{=}0
to

k_{i}\ =\ ({\textstyle\frac{\gamma}{\ell}})^{3}\,\mathcal{K}_{i}\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad\quad\mathcal{K}_{i}\ =\ \rho\,\omega_{i}\ .

(27)

The corresponding charges
K_{i}
vanishes because of the odd integrand as discussed in an earlier remark:

K_{i}\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,k_{i}\ =\ \int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,\mathcal{K}_{i}\ =\ 2\int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,\omega_{i}\,X_{a}^{j}\,\bar{X}_{a}^{j}\ =\ 0\ .

(28)

Angular momentum.
The other three conserved charge densities
J^{0}
corresponding to
\epsilon_{ij}
in (
4
) are the angular momentum densities
{\bf l}={\bf p}\times{\bf x}
, which takes a simple form just like in momentum (
20
):

l_{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}\ =\ ({\textstyle\frac{\gamma}{\ell}})^{2}\,\mathcal{L}_{a}\,e^{a}\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad\quad\mathcal{L}_{a}\ =\ \varepsilon_{abc}\,\mathcal{P}_{b}\,\omega_{c}\ .

(29)

The expressions for the charges
L_{i}
simplify to

L_{i}\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,l_{i}\ =\ \int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,({\textstyle\frac{\ell}{\gamma}})\,\mathcal{L}_{a}\,e^{a}_{i}\ =\ 2\mathrm{i}\ell\ \Omega^{2}\ \int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{\gamma}}\,e^{a}_{i}\left(\bar{X}_{a}\,\omega_{b}X_{b}-X_{a}\,\omega_{b}\bar{X}_{b}\right)\ .

(30)

Explicit calculation show that for
j{=}0
the angular momenta
L_{i}
are proportional to the momenta
P_{i}
:

L_{i}^{(j=0)}\ =\ -\ell^{2}\,P_{i}^{(j=0)}\ .

(31)

This is, however, not true for higher spin
j
. The angular momenta
L_{i}
has the same rotation behaviour as for the momenta
P_{i}
(
23
). We therefore note down the results of
L_{3}
for
j=1/2
and
1
in table
1
, from which the corresponding expressions of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
can be obtained using the table in Appendix
8
.

We can again compute the spherical components of the angular momentum
(L_{r},L_{\theta},L_{\phi})
using the relations (
24
) by replacing
\mathcal{P}
with
\mathcal{L}
in it. We realize that the
S^{2}
integrand for
L_{r}
would only have terms like
\hat{\omega}_{a}\hat{\omega}_{b}\mathcal{P}_{c}
, which are all odd functions
7
7

7

Here again the terms of
\mathcal{P}_{c}
, which goes like
Y_{l,M}Y_{l^{\prime},M^{\prime}}
(
36
), are all even functions of
\phi
for a fixed
j
.
of
\phi
and, therefore, the
\phi
integral over the domain
(0,2\pi)
would make
L_{r}
vanish. We also find, with explicit computations, that the charges
L_{\theta}
and
L_{\phi}
for
j{=}0
are proportional to
P_{3}
:

L_{\theta}^{(j=0)}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{4}{3}}\,\ell^{2}\,P_{3}^{(j=0)}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad L_{\phi}^{(j=0)}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{1}{3}}\,\ell^{2}\,P_{3}^{(j=0)}\ .

(32)

As a non trivial example, we collect these charges for
j=1/2
below:

\displaystyle L_{\theta}^{\left(j=1/2\right)}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{12}{5}}\ell\,\Big(

\displaystyle 9|\Lambda_{-1/2,-3/2}|^{2}+6|\Lambda_{-1/2,-1/2}|^{2}+|\Lambda_{-1/2,1/2}|^{2}-6|\Lambda_{-1/2,3/2}|^{2}

(33)

\displaystyle+6|\Lambda_{1/2,-3/2}|^{2}-|\Lambda_{1/2,-1/2}|^{2}-6|\Lambda_{1/2,1/2}|^{2}-9|\Lambda_{1/2,3/2}|^{2}\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle L_{\phi}^{\left(j=1/2\right)}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{3}{5}}\ell\,\Big(

\displaystyle 6|\Lambda_{-1/2,-3/2}|^{2}-|\Lambda_{-1/2,-1/2}|^{2}-6|\Lambda_{-1/2,1/2}|^{2}-9|\Lambda_{-1/2,3/2}|^{2}

(34)

\displaystyle+9|\Lambda_{1/2,-3/2}|^{2}+6|\Lambda_{1/2,-1/2}|^{2}+|\Lambda_{1/2,1/2}|^{2}-6|\Lambda_{1/2,3/2}|^{2}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{3}\big(\bar{\Lambda}_{1/2,-3/2}\Lambda_{-1/2,-1/2}-\bar{\Lambda}_{1/2,1/2}\Lambda_{-1/2,3/2}

\displaystyle+\bar{\Lambda}_{-1/2,-1/2}\Lambda_{1/2,-3/2}-\bar{\Lambda}_{-1/2,3/2}\Lambda_{1/2,1/2}\big)\Big)\ .

3
Dilatation

It is easy to verify that a constant rescaling by
\lambda
:

\zeta^{\mu}\ =\ \lambda\,x^{\mu}

(35)

is also a solution of (
2
). The charge density corresponding to this single generator of the conformal group is
{\bf p}\cdot{\bf x}-e\,t
, which for
t{=}0
simplifies to

p_{i}\,x_{i}\ =\ ({\textstyle\frac{\gamma}{\ell}})^{3}\,\mathcal{P}_{a}\,\omega_{a}\ .

(36)

The corresponding charge
D
vanishes because of the odd integrand:

D\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,p_{i}\,x_{i}\ =\ 2\mathrm{i}\,\Omega^{2}\,\varepsilon_{abc}\int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,\omega_{a}\,X_{b}\,\bar{X}_{c}\ =\ 0\ .

(37)

4
Special conformal transformations

A fairly straightforward calculation shows that the following not so obvious transformation

\zeta^{\mu}\ =\ 2\,x^{\mu}\,b_{\nu}x^{\nu}\ -\ b^{\mu}\,x^{\nu}x_{\nu}

(38)

also satisfies (
2
). The four generators corresponding to
b_{\mu}
give rise to four different charges
V_{0}
and
{\bf V}
.

Scalar SCT.
The charge density
J^{0}
corresponding to
b_{0}
is

v_{0}\ =\ ({\bf x}^{2}+t^{2})\,e-2t\,{\bf p}\cdot{\bf x}\ ,

(39)

which for
t{=}0
simplifies to

v_{0}\ =\ {\bf x}^{2}\,e\ =\ ({\textstyle\frac{\gamma}{\ell}})^{2}\rho\,\omega_{a}^{2}\ .

(40)

The expression for the corresponding charge
V_{0}
takes the following simple form:

V_{0}\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,v_{0}\ =\ \int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,({\textstyle\frac{\ell}{\gamma}})\,(1-\omega_{4}^{2})\,\rho\ =\ 2\ell\,\Omega^{2}\int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,(1+\omega_{4})\,X_{a}\,\bar{X}_{a}\ .

(41)

Here again the
\omega_{4}
term of the integral, being odd, vanishes and yields

V_{0}\ =\ \ell^{2}\,E\ =\ 8\,\ell\,(j+1)^{3}(2j+1)\,\sum_{m,n}\,|\Lambda_{m,n}|^{2}\ .

(42)

Vector SCT.
The charge densities
J^{0}
that correspond to
b_{i}
read:

{\bf v}\ =\ 2{\bf x}({\bf x}\cdot{\bf p})-2t\,{\bf x}\,e-({\bf x}^{2}-t^{2}){\bf p}\ .

(43)

This simplify at
t{=}0
and takes a structure similar to the momentum densities
p_{i}
(
20
):

v_{i}\,dx^{i}\ =\ ({\textstyle\frac{\gamma}{\ell}})\,\mathcal{V}_{a}\,e^{a}\quad\textrm{with}\quad\mathcal{V}_{a}\ =\ 2\omega_{a}\,(\mathcal{P}_{b}\,\omega_{b})-\omega_{b}^{2}\,\mathcal{P}_{a}\ .

(44)

The expressions for the charges
V_{i}
then simplify to

V_{i}\ =\ \int_{V}\mathrm{d}^{3}x\,v_{i}\ =\ 2\mathrm{i}\,\ell^{2}\,\Omega^{2}\int_{S^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\,\gamma^{-2}e^{a}_{i}\left(2\varepsilon_{bcd}\,\omega_{a}\,\omega_{b}\,X_{c}\,\bar{X}_{d}-\varepsilon_{abc}\,(1-\omega_{4}^{2})\,X_{b}\,\bar{X}_{c}\right)\ .

(45)

With explicit computation we observe that the charges
V_{i}
are proportional to the momenta
P_{i}
(verified explicitly for up to
j{=}1
)

V_{i}\ =\ \ell^{2}\,P_{i}\ .

(46)

As before, we can compute the spherical components
(V_{r},V_{\theta},V_{\phi})
by using the expressions (
24
) and replacing
\mathcal{P}
with
\mathcal{V}
in it. We notice that the charge
V_{r}
vanishes owing to the odd
S^{2}
integrand
8
8

8

Observe that the terms in
\mathcal{V}_{a}
(
44
) are all even in
\hat{\omega}_{a}
.
just like in the case of
P_{r}
. However, unlike
P_{\theta}
here the charge
V_{\theta}
is non-vanishing. Explicit calculations show that for
j=0
the charges
V_{\theta}
and
V_{\phi}
are proportional to the momentum
P_{3}
:

V_{\theta}^{(j=0)}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{4}{3}}\ell^{3}\,P_{3}^{(j=0)}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad V_{\phi}^{(j=0)}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{5}{3}}\ell^{3}\,P_{3}^{(j=0)}\ .

(47)

Additionally, we record below the charges
V_{\theta}
and
V_{\phi}
for the non-trivial case of
j{=}1/2

\displaystyle V_{\theta}^{\left(j=1/2\right)}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{6}{5}}\ell^{2}\,\Big(

\displaystyle 9|\Lambda_{-1/2,-3/2}|^{2}+|\Lambda_{-1/2,-1/2}|^{2}-9|\Lambda_{-1/2,1/2}|^{2}-21|\Lambda_{-1/2,3/2}|^{2}

(48)

\displaystyle+21|\Lambda_{1/2,-3/2}|^{2}+9|\Lambda_{1/2,-1/2}|^{2}-|\Lambda_{1/2,1/2}|^{2}-9|\Lambda_{1/2,3/2}|^{2}\Big)\ ,

\displaystyle V_{\phi}^{\left(j=1/2\right)}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{3}{5}}\ell^{2}\,\Big(

\displaystyle 42|\Lambda_{-1/2,-3/2}|^{2}+33|\Lambda_{-1/2,-1/2}|^{2}+8|\Lambda_{-1/2,1/2}|^{2}-33|\Lambda_{-1/2,3/2}|^{2}

(49)

\displaystyle+33|\Lambda_{1/2,-3/2}|^{2}-8|\Lambda_{1/2,-1/2}|^{2}-33|\Lambda_{1/2,1/2}|^{2}-42|\Lambda_{1/2,3/2}|^{2}

\displaystyle+8\sqrt{3}\big(-\bar{\Lambda}_{1/2,-3/2}\Lambda_{-1/2,-1/2}+\bar{\Lambda}_{1/2,1/2}\Lambda_{-1/2,3/2}

\displaystyle-\bar{\Lambda}_{-1/2,-1/2}\Lambda_{1/2,-3/2}+\bar{\Lambda}_{-1/2,3/2}\Lambda_{1/2,1/2}\big)\Big)\ .

One can compute these charges for higher spin-
j
by following the same strategy.

5
Applications

The method of constructing rational electromagnetic fields presented in this paper has the added advantage that it produces a complete set labelled by
(j,m,n)
; any electromagnetic field configuration having finite energy can, in principle, be obtained from an expansion like in (
8
-
10
), albeit with a varying
j
. The operational difficulty involved in this procedure has to do with the fact that this set is infinite as
j\in{\textstyle\frac{\mathds{N}}{2}}
. There are, however, many important cases where only a finite number of knot-basis solutions (sometimes only with a fixed
j
) need to be combined to get the desired EM field configuration. One such very important case is that of the Hopfian solution discussed before. Below we analyse two very interesting generalisations of the Hopfian solution presented in
[
14
]
in the context of present construction. It is imperative to note here that while the scope of construction of a new solution from the known ones as presented in
[
14
]
is limited, the same is not true for the method presented in this paper, which by design can produce arbitrary number of new field configurations. Some of these possible new field configurations obtained from the
j{=}0
sector (possibly from
j{=}1/2\penalty\ \textrm{or}\penalty\ 1
as well) could find experimental application with improved experimental techniques like in
[
5
]
.

Figure 2
:

Sample electric (red) and magnetic (green) field lines at
t{=}0
.
Left: time-translated Hopfian with
c=60
, Right: Rotated Hopfian with
\theta=1
.

Bateman’s construction, employed in
[
14
]
, hinges on a judicious ansatz for the Riemann-Silberstein vector (
21
) satisfying Maxwell’s equations:

{\bf S}\ =\ \nabla\alpha\times\nabla\beta\quad\implies\quad\nabla\cdot{\bf S}\ =\ 0\penalty\ \ \&\penalty\ \ \mathrm{i}\,\partial_{t}{\bf S}\ =\ \nabla\times{\bf S}\ ,

(50)

using a pair of complex functions
(\alpha,\beta)
. An interesting generalization of the Hopfian solution is obtained in equations (3.16-3.17) of
[
14
]
using (complex) time-translation (TT) to obtain the following
(\alpha,\beta)
pair (up to a normalization)

(\alpha,\beta)_{TT}\ =\ \left(\frac{A-1-\mathrm{i}z}{A+\mathrm{i}(t+\mathrm{i}c)},\frac{x-\mathrm{i}y}{A+\mathrm{i}(t+\mathrm{i}c)}\right)\ ;\quad A\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\left(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}-(t+\mathrm{i}c)^{2}+1\right)\ ,

(51)

where
c
is a constant real parameter. The corresponding EM field configuration is obtained, in our case, by choosing
\ell{=}1{-}c
and only the following
j{=}0
, and hence type I, complex coefficients in (
10
)

\left(\Lambda_{0;0,1}\ ,\penalty\ \Lambda_{0;0,0}\ ,\penalty\ \Lambda_{0;0,-1}\right)_{TT}\ =\ \left(0\ ,\penalty\ 0\ ,\penalty\ -\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2\ell^{2}}\right)\ .

(52)

A sample electromagnetic knot configuration of this modified Hopfian is illustated in figure
2
. Another interesting generalization of the Hopfian is constructed in equations (3.20-3.21) of
[
14
]
using a (complex) rotation (R) to get the following
(\alpha,\beta)
pair (again, up to a normalization)

(\alpha,\beta)_{R}\ =\ \left(\frac{A-1+\mathrm{i}(z\cos{\mathrm{i}\theta}+x\sin{\mathrm{i}\theta})}{A+\mathrm{i}t},\frac{x\cos{\mathrm{i}\theta}-z\sin{\mathrm{i}\theta}-\mathrm{i}y}{A+\mathrm{i}t}\right)\ ,

(53)

where
A={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\left(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}-t^{2}+1\right)
. To get this particular EM field configuration we need to set
\ell{=}1
and use the following combination of only
j{=}0
type I coefficients in (
10
)

\left(\Lambda_{0;0,1}\ ,\penalty\ \Lambda_{0;0,0}\ ,\penalty\ \Lambda_{0;0,-1}\right)_{R}\ =\ \left(\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}(\cosh{\theta}-1)\ ,\penalty\ -\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2}}\sinh{\theta}\ ,\penalty\ -\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{4}(\cosh\theta+1)\right)\ .

(54)

We illustrate the EM field lines for a sample
\theta
value of this modified Hopfian in figure
2
. We note down the conformal charges corresponding to these solutions in table
2
by plugging the
j{=}0
coefficients (
52
) and (
54
) in the appropriate formulae of the previous section. The results matches with the ones given in
[
14
]
up to a rescaling of the energy, which can be achieved by an appropriate choice of normalization.

4
Null fields

An interesting subset of vacuum electromagnetic fields are those with vanishing Lorentz invariants,

\vec{E}^{2}-\vec{B}^{2}=0\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}=0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\bigl(\vec{E}\pm\mathrm{i}\vec{B}\bigr)^{2}=\ 0\ .

(1)

As a scalar equation it must equally hold on the de Sitter side, and so
we can try to characterize such configurations with our SO(4) basis above. For a given type and spin,
the expressions in (
8
) immediately give the Riemann-Silberstein vector on the
S^{3}
cylinder,

{\cal E}_{a}\pm\mathrm{i}\,{\cal B}_{a}\ =\ -2\mathrm{i}\,\Omega\,X_{a}(\omega)\,\mathrm{e}^{\Omega\,\mathrm{i}\tau}\ ,

(2)

where the upper (lower) sign pertains to type I (II).
Note that the negative-frequency part of this field has cancelled.
The vanishing of
({\cal E}_{a}{\pm}\mathrm{i}{\cal B}_{a})({\cal E}_{a}{\pm}\mathrm{i}{\cal B}_{a})
is then equivalent to a condition on the angular functions,

0\ =\ X_{1}(\omega)^{2}+X_{2}(\omega)^{2}+X_{3}(\omega)^{2}\ =\ 2\,Z_{+}(\omega)Z_{-}(\omega)+Z_{3}(\omega)^{2}\ .

(3)

When expanding the angular functions
Z^{j}_{*\ \textrm{I}}
or
Z^{j}_{*\ \textrm{II}}
into basis solutions with (
10
),
one arrives at a system of homogeneous quadratic equations for the free coefficients
\lambda_{j;m,n}^{\textrm{I/II}}
.

Let us analyze the situation for type I and spin
j
.
The functions
Z^{j}_{*}(\omega)
transform under a
(j,j)
representation of
su(2)_{L}\oplus su(2)_{R}
.
The null condition (
3
) then yields a representation content of
(0,0)\oplus(1,1)\oplus\ldots\oplus(2j,2j)

and may thus be expanded into the corresponding harmonics. Furthermore, The independent vanishing of all coefficients produces

\frac{1}{6}(4j{+}1)(4j{+}2)(4j{+}3)
equations for the
(2j{+}1)(2j{+}3)
parameters
\lambda_{j;m,n}
(note the ranges of
m
and
n
for type I).
Clearly, this system is vastly overdetermined. However, it turns out that only
4j^{2}{+}6j{+}1
equations are independent,
still leaving
2j{+}2
free complex parameters for the solution space. The independent equations can be organized as (suppressing
j
)

\displaystyle\lambda_{m,n}^{2}

\displaystyle\sim\ \lambda_{m,n-1}\,\lambda_{m,n+1}\qquad\ \quad\textrm{for}\quad m,n=-j\,\ldots,j\ ,

(4)

\displaystyle\lambda_{m,j+1}\,\lambda_{m+1,-j-1}

\displaystyle=\ \lambda_{m+1,j+1}\,\lambda_{m,-j-1}\quad\quad\textrm{for}\quad m=-j,\ldots,j{-}1\ .

We have checked for
j{\leq}5
that the upper equations are solved by
9
9

9

These are the generic solutions. There exist also special solutions given by
(
6
) and
\lambda_{m,n}=0
for
|n|\neq j{+}1
, for arbitrarily selected choices of
m\in\{-j,\ldots,j\}
.

\lambda_{m,n}^{2j+2}\ =\ {\textstyle\sqrt{\binom{2j+2}{j+1-n}}}\ \lambda_{m,-j-1}^{j+1-n}\,\lambda_{m,j+1}^{j+1+n}\quad\quad\textrm{for}\quad m=-j,\ldots,j\quad\textrm{and}\quad n=-j{-}1,\ldots,j{+}1\ ,

(5)

while the lower ones imply that the highest weights
n{=}j{+}1
are proportional to the lowest weights
n{=}{-}j{-}1
(independent of
m
),

\lambda_{m,-j-1}\ =\ w\,\lambda_{m,j+1}\quad\quad\textrm{for}\quad w\in\mathds{C}^{*}\ .

(6)

Therefore, the full (generic) solution reads

\lambda_{m,n}\ =\ {\textstyle\sqrt{\binom{2j+2}{j+1-n}}}\ w^{\frac{j+1-n}{2j+2}}\ \mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}k_{m}\frac{j+1-n}{2j+2}}\ z_{m}\qquad\quad\textrm{with}\quad z_{m}\in\mathds{C}\quad\textrm{and}\quad k_{m}\in\{0,1,\ldots,2j{+}1\}\ ,

(7)

containing
2j{+}2
complex parameters
z_{m}
and
q
as well as
2j
discrete choices
\{k_{m}\}

(one of them can be absorbed into
z_{m}
). This completely specifies the type-I null fields for a given spin.
Type-II null fields are easily obtained by applying electromagnetic duality to type-I null fields.

In the simplest case of
j{=}0
, the single equation
\lambda_{0,0}^{2}=2\lambda_{0,-1}\lambda_{0,1}
describes
a generic rank-3 quadric in
\mathds{C}P^{2}
, or a cone over a sphere
\mathds{C}P^{1}
inside the parameter space
\mathds{C}^{3}
. For higher spin,
the moduli space of type-I null fields remains a complete-intersection projective variety of complex dimension
2j{+}1
.

We conclude the Section with a display of typical field lines (see Figure
3
) for a type I
j{=}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}
and
j{=}1
null field at
t{=}0
. For
t{\neq}0
the pictures get smoothly distorted.

Figure 3
:

Sample electric (red) and magnetic (green) field lines at
t{=}0
.
Left: a pair of electric and a pair of magnetic field lines for the
(j;m,n)=\left({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}};{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},{\textstyle\frac{3}{2}}\right)_{R}
field configuration. Right: a pair of electric field lines, a magnetic field line
of self-linking one and a magnetic field line of self-linking seven for the
(j;m,n)=(1;0,2)_{R}
field configuration.

5
Flux transport

We have seen that electromagnetic energy is radiated away along the light-cones.
Let us try to quantify its amount over future null infinity
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Before proceeding further we note down the determinant of the Jacobian
J
(
13
)

|\textrm{det}J|\ =\ \frac{p^{2}{-}q^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\ =\ \frac{\gamma^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\ =\ \frac{\sin^{2}\!\tau}{t^{2}}\ =\ \frac{\sin^{2}\!\chi}{r^{2}}\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad\gamma^{2}=p^{2}{-}q^{2}

(1)

and the spherical Minkowski components

A_{t}\ =\ {\cal A}_{\tau}J^{\tau}_{\ t}+{\cal A}_{\chi}J^{\chi}_{\ t}\ ,\qquad A_{r}\ =\ {\cal A}_{\tau}J^{\tau}_{\ r}+{\cal A}_{\chi}J^{\chi}_{\ r}\ ,\qquad A_{\theta}={\cal A}_{\theta}\ ,\qquad A_{\phi}={\cal A}_{\phi}\ ,

(2)

or any other such tensor component arising due to (
13
).
For later use, we also note here the transformation of the volume form

\mathrm{d}^{4}x\ =\ \mathrm{d}t\,r^{2}\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ =\ r^{2}\,|\textrm{det}J|^{-1}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}\chi\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ =\ \sin^{2}\!\chi\,|\textrm{det}J|^{-2}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}\chi\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ =\ \frac{\ell^{4}}{\gamma^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\,\mathrm{d}^{3}\Omega_{3}\ .

(3)

The energy flux at time
t_{0}
passing through a two-sphere of radius
r_{0}
centered at the spatial origin is given by

\Phi(t_{0},r_{0})\ =\ \int_{S^{2}(r_{0})}\!\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\vec{\sigma}\cdot\bigl(\vec{E}\times\vec{B}\bigr)(t_{0},r_{0},\theta,\phi)\ =\ \int_{S^{2}}r_{0}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ T_{t\,r}^{\mathrm{(M)}}(t_{0},r_{0},\theta,\phi)\ ,

(4)

where
\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}=\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\phi
,
and
T_{t\,r}^{\mathrm{(M)}}
is the
(t,r)
component of the Minkowski-space stress-energy tensor

T_{\mu\,\nu}^{\mathrm{(M)}}\ =\ F_{\mu\rho}F_{\nu\lambda}\,g^{\rho\lambda}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}g_{\mu\nu}F^{2}\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad(g_{\mu\nu})=\textrm{diag}(-1,1,r^{2},r^{2}\sin^{2}\!\theta)\

(5)

for
\mu,\nu,\ldots\in\{t,r,\theta,\phi\}
. We carry out this computation in the
S^{3}
-cylinder frame by using the conformal relations

\ell^{2}\,T_{\mu\,\nu}^{\mathrm{(dS)}}\ =\ t^{2}\,T_{\mu\,\nu}^{\mathrm{(M)}}\ =\ \sin^{2}\!\tau\,T_{\mu\,\nu}^{\mathrm{(cyl)}}\ =\ \sin^{2}\!\tau\,T_{m\,n}^{\mathrm{(cyl)}}\,J^{m}_{\ \ \mu}\,J^{n}_{\ \ \nu}\quad\textrm{for}\quad m,n\in\{\tau,\chi,\theta,\phi\}

(6)

with the Jacobian (
13
) and the fact that
r\sin\tau=t\sin\chi
so that

\Phi(\tau_{0},\chi_{0})\ =\ \int_{S^{2}}\sin^{2}\!\chi\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ T_{t\,r}^{\mathrm{(cyl)}}(\tau_{0},\chi_{0},\theta,\phi)\ =\ \int_{S^{2}}\sin^{2}\!\chi\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ T_{m\,n}^{\mathrm{(cyl)}}\,J^{m}_{\ \ t}\,J^{n}_{\ \ r}\ .

(7)

A straightforward computation using
(g_{mn})=\textrm{diag}(-1,1,\sin^{2}\!\chi,\sin^{2}\!\chi\sin^{2}\!\theta)
then yields

\displaystyle\Phi(\tau_{0},\chi_{0})

\displaystyle=\ \frac{p\,q}{\ell^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\,\Bigl(({\cal F}_{\tau\theta})^{2}+({\cal F}_{\chi\theta})^{2}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\!\theta}}\bigl[({\cal F}_{\tau\phi})^{2}+({\cal F}_{\chi\phi})^{2}\bigr]\Bigr)

(8)

\displaystyle+\ \frac{p^{2}{+}q^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\,\Bigl({\cal F}_{\tau\theta}\,{\cal F}_{\chi\theta}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\!\theta}}{\cal F}_{\tau\phi}\,{\cal F}_{\chi\phi}\Bigr)\ .

The sphere-frame components
{\cal F}_{mn}
can be computed by expanding

e^{a}=e^{a}_{\ m}\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{m}
in

{\cal F}\ =\ {\cal E}_{a}\,e^{a}{\wedge}e^{\tau}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\,{\cal B}_{a}\,\varepsilon^{a}_{\ bc}\,e^{b}{\wedge}e^{c}\ =\ {\cal F}_{mn}\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{m}{\wedge}\mathrm{d}\xi^{n}\quad\quad\textrm{with}\quad\xi^{n}\in\{\tau,\chi,\theta,\phi\}\ .

(9)

The expression for the flux in sphere-frame fields then becomes

\displaystyle\ell^{2}\,\Phi

\displaystyle=\ p\,q\,\sin^{2}\!\chi\int_{S^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ \Bigl[(\sin\phi\,{\cal E}_{1}-\cos\phi\,{\cal E}_{2})^{2}+(\cos\theta\cos\phi\,{\cal E}_{1}+\cos\theta\sin\phi\,{\cal E}_{2}

(10)

\displaystyle-\sin\theta\,{\cal E}_{3})^{2}+(\sin\phi\,{\cal B}_{1}-\cos\phi\,{\cal B}_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle+(\cos\theta\cos\phi\,{\cal B}_{1}+\cos\theta\sin\phi\,{\cal B}_{2}-\sin\theta\,{\cal B}_{3})^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle+\ (p^{2}{+}q^{2})\,\sin^{2}\!\chi\int_{S^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ \Bigl[(\sin\phi\,{\cal B}_{1}-\cos\phi\,{\cal B}_{2})(\cos\theta\cos\phi\,{\cal E}_{1}

\displaystyle+\cos\theta\sin\phi\,{\cal E}_{2}-\sin\theta\,{\cal E}_{3})-(\sin\phi\,{\cal E}_{1}-\cos\phi\,{\cal E}_{2})

\displaystyle\cdot(\cos\theta\cos\phi\,{\cal B}_{1}+\cos\theta\sin\phi\,{\cal B}_{2}-\sin\theta\,{\cal B}_{3})\Bigr]\ .

The total energy flux across future null infinity is obtained by evaluating this expression on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and integrating over it. Introducing cylinder light-cone coordinates

u=\tau{+}\chi\quad\textrm{and}\quad v=\tau{-}\chi\qquad\textrm{so that}\qquad t{+}r=-\ell\,\cot{\textstyle\frac{v}{2}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad t{-}r=-\ell\,\cot{\textstyle\frac{u}{2}}

(11)

we characterize
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as

\biggl\{\begin{array}[]{l}t{+}r\to\infty\\[4.0pt]
t{-}r\in\mathds{R}\end{array}\biggr\}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\biggl\{\begin{array}[]{l}u\in(0,2\pi)\\[4.0pt]
v=0\end{array}\biggr\}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad p=q=\sin^{2}\!\chi\quad\textrm{and}\quad\gamma=0\ .

(12)

Further noticing that

\mathrm{d}(t{-}r)\ =\ \frac{\ell\ \mathrm{d}u}{p{+}q}\ =\ \frac{\ell\ \mathrm{d}u}{1-\cos u}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\sin^{2}\!\chi\ =\ \sin^{2}\!{\textstyle\frac{u-v}{2}}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(1-\cos(u{-}v)\bigr)\ ,

(13)

we may express this total flux as

\Phi_{+}\ =\ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}(t{-}r)\ \Phi\big|_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}\ =\ \int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\ell\ \mathrm{d}u}{1-\cos u}\ \Phi({\textstyle\frac{u}{2}},{\textstyle\frac{u}{2}})

(14)

to obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{+}

\displaystyle=\ \frac{1}{8\ell}\int\!\mathrm{d}u\,(1{-}\cos u)^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\ \Bigl[\bigl\{\cos\theta\cos\phi\,{\cal E}_{1}+\cos\theta\sin\phi\,{\cal E}_{2}-\sin\theta\,{\cal E}_{3}+\sin\phi\,{\cal B}_{1}

(15)

\displaystyle-\cos\phi\,{\cal B}_{2}\bigr\}^{2}+\ \bigl\{\cos\theta\cos\phi\,{\cal B}_{1}+\cos\theta\sin\phi\,{\cal B}_{2}-\sin\theta\,{\cal B}_{3}-\sin\phi\,{\cal E}_{1}+\cos\phi\,{\cal E}_{2}\bigr\}^{2}\Bigr]

The square bracket expression above can be further simplified for a fixed spin and type
by employing (
8
) along with (
9
), (
10
), (
6
) and (
7
) to get

\Phi_{+}^{(j)}=\frac{\Omega^{2}}{4\ell}\int\!\mathrm{d}u\,(1{-}\cos u)^{2}\int\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\Omega_{2}\,\big|\pm Z_{+}^{j}e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi}(1\pm\cos\theta)\mp Z_{-}^{j}e^{\mathrm{i}\phi}(1\mp\cos\theta)-\sqrt{2}Z_{3}^{j}\sin\theta\,\big|^{2}\ ,

(16)

where the upper (lower) sign corresponds to a type-I (type-II) solution.
In the special case of
j=0

(\Omega{=}2)
the contribution to the two-sphere integral only comes
from the part which is independent of
(\theta,\phi)
, i.e.
\frac{4}{3}\left(|Z^{0}_{+}|^{2}+|Z^{0}_{-}|^{2}+|Z^{0}_{3}|^{2}\right)
,
so that the integration can easily be performed by passing to the adjoint harmonics

\tilde{Y}_{j;l,M}
(
37
) and using (
36
) to get

\Phi_{+}^{(0)}\ =\ \frac{16}{3\,\ell}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\!\mathrm{d}u\ \sin^{4}\!{\textstyle\frac{u}{2}}\,\bigl|R_{0,0}({\textstyle\frac{u}{2}})\bigr|^{2}\sum\limits_{n=-1}^{1}|\lambda_{0,n}|^{2}\ =\ \frac{8}{\ell}\sum\limits_{n=-1}^{1}|\lambda_{0,n}|^{2}\ =\ E^{(0)}\ .

(17)

The same equality
\Phi_{+}=E
continues to hold true as we go up in spin
j

(we verified it for
j{=}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}
and
j{=}1
),
thus validating the energy conservation
\mbox{$\partial$}^{\mu}T_{\mu 0}=0
.

6
Trajectories

Given a knotted electromagnetic field configuration, a natural question that arises is how do charged particles propagate in the background of such a field? We proceed to address this issue here by analyzing, with numerical simulations, the trajectories of several (identical) charged point particles for the family of knotted field configurations (
17
) that we encountered before. We will consider type I (
6
) basis field configurations (up to
j{=}1
for simplicity) and the Hopf–Rañada field configuration (
25
).

In some of the simulations we employ the maximum of the energy density at time
t
, i.e.
E_{max}(t)
(that occurs at several points
{\bf x}_{max}
that are located symmetrically with respect to the origin), for different initial conditions and field configurations. In such cases we have employed a parameter
R_{max}(t)
of “maximal” radius defined via

E\left(t,{\bf x}_{max}(t)\right)\ =\ E_{max}(t)\quad\implies\quad R_{max}(t)\ :=\ |{\bf x}_{max}(t)|\ .

(1)

A characteristic feature of these basis knot electromagnetic fields is that they have a preferred
z
-axis direction due to our convention to diagonalize the
J_{3}
action in (
28
); notice here that the SO(3) isometry subgroup, and hence its generators
J_{a}
, are identified on the cylinder and the Minkowski side as shown in (
3
). This is clearly exemplified in Figure
4
, where the energy density
E:={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}({\bf E}^{2}+{\bf B}^{2})
decreases along the
z
-axis. As a result, the basis fields along the
z
-axis (i.e.
{\bf E}(t,x{=}0,y{=}0,z)
and
{\bf B}(t,x{=}0,y{=}0,z)
) are either directed in the
xy
-plane or along the
z
-axis. In fact, for extreme field configurations
(j;\pm j,\pm(j{+}1))
, for any
j{>}0
, the fields along the
z
-axis vanish for all times.

Figure 4
:

Contour plots for energy densities at
t{=}0
(yellow),
t{=}1
(cyan) and
t{=}1.5
(purple) with contour value
0.9E_{max}(t{=}1.5)
.
Left: Hopf–Ranãda configuration, Center:
(j;m,n)=({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}};-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},-{\textstyle\frac{3}{2}})_{R}
configuration, Right:
(j;m,n)=(1;-1,1)_{R}
configuration.

The trajectories of these particles are governed by the relativistic Lorentz equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bf p}}{\mathrm{d}t}=q({\bf E}_{\ell}+{\bf v}\times{\bf B}_{\ell})\ ,

(2)

where
q
is the charge of the particle,
{\bf p}=\widetilde{\gamma}m{\bf v}
is the relativistic three-momentum,
{\bf v}
is the usual three-velocity of the particle,
m
is its mass,
\widetilde{\gamma}=(1-{\bf v}^{\,2})^{-1/2}
is the Lorentz factor, and
{\bf E}_{\ell}
and
{\bf B}_{\ell}
are dimensionful electric and magnetic fields respectively. With the energy of the particle
E_{p}=\widetilde{\gamma}m
and
\mathrm{d}E_{p}/\mathrm{d}{t}=q\,{\bf v}\cdot{\bf E}
, one can rewrite (
2
) in terms of the derivative of
{\bf v}

[
33
]
as

\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bf v}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{q}{\gamma m}\left({\bf E}_{\ell}+{\bf v}\times{\bf B}_{\ell}-({\bf v}\cdot{\bf E}_{\ell})\,{\bf v}\right)\ .

(3)

Equations (
2
) and (
3
) are equivalent, and either one can be used for a simulation purpose; they only differ by the position of the nonlinearity in
{\bf v}
. In natural units
\hbar{=}c{=}\epsilon_{0}{=}1
, every dimensionful quantity can be written in terms of a length scale. We relate all dimensionful quantities to the de Sitter radius
\ell
from equation (
1
) and work with the corresponding dimensionless ones as follows:

T:=\frac{t}{\ell}\ ,\quad{\bf X}:=\frac{{\bf x}}{\ell}\ ,\quad{\bf V}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}X}{\mathrm{d}T}\equiv{\bf v}\ ,\quad{\bf E}:=\ell^{2}{\bf E}_{\ell},\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\bf B}:=\ell^{2}{\bf B}_{\ell}\ .

(4)

Moreover, the fields are solutions of the homogeneous (source-free) Maxwell equations, so they can be freely rescaled by any dimensionless constant factor
\lambda
. Combining the above considerations, one can rewrite (
2
) (or analogously (
3
)) fully in terms of dimensionless quantities as

\frac{\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\gamma}{\bf V})}{\mathrm{d}T}=\kappa({\bf E}+{\bf V}\times{\bf B})\ ,

(5)

where
\kappa={\textstyle\frac{q\ell^{3}\lambda}{m}}
is a dimensionless parameter. One consequence of this parameter is that we can tune the values of each of the constants separately. In particular, we can make the charge as small as needed without changing
\kappa
such that the effect of the backreaction on the trajectories becomes negligible. As for the initial conditions, we mostly work in the following two main scenarios:

(1)

N
identical charged particles with
{\bf V}_{0}{\equiv}{\bf V}(T{=}0){=}0
located symmetrically (with respect to the origin), or

(2)

N
identical charged particles with
\,{\bf X}_{0}{\equiv}{\bf X}(T{=}0){=}0\,
with particle velocities directed radially outward in a symmetric fashion (with respect to the origin; shown in colored arrows),

with the following 3 sub-cases for both of these conditions:

(A)

along a line,

(B)

on a circle of radius
r
,

(C)

on a sphere of radius
r
.

We vary several parameters including the initial conditions with different directions of lines and planes for each configuration, the value of
\kappa
, and the simulation time in order to study the behavior of the trajectories. In several field configurations studied below, we find that
R_{max}(0)=0
, so we use a small radius
r
for the initial condition of kind (1) to be able to probe the particles around a region of maximum energy of the field. In this scenario, the effect of the field on the trajectories of the particles is more prominent, as expected, and this helps us understand small perturbations of the trajectories as compared to a particle starting at rest from the origin. The effect of the fields on particles starting near the maximum of the energy density is also more prominent for
R_{max}(0){\neq}0
, as illustrated in Figure
5
. Moreover, for the initial condition of kind (2) we use the particle initial speeds in the range where it is (i) non-relativistic, (ii) relativistic (usually between
0.1
and
0.9
), and (iii) ultrarelativistic (here,
0.99
or higher).

Figure 5
:
Simulation of
N{=}18
particles in scenario (1C) for
({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}};-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},-{\textstyle\frac{3}{2}})_{R}
with
\kappa{=}10
and for
t\in[0,1]
. Left:
r{=}R_{max}(0){\approx}0.447
. Right:
r{=}R_{max}(0)/3
.

We observe a variety of different behaviors for these trajectories, some of which we summarize below with the aid of figures. Firstly, it is worth noticing that, even with all fields decreasing as powers of both space and time coordinates, in most field configurations we observe particles getting accelerated from rest up to ultrarelativistic speeds. The limit of these ultrarelativistic speeds for higher times depend on the magnitude of the fields (see, for example, Figure
6
).

Figure 6
:

Trajectory of a charged particle for
({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}};-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},-{\textstyle\frac{3}{2}})_{R}
configuration with initial conditions
{\bf X}_{0}{=}(0.01,0.01,0.01)
and
{\bf V}_{0}{=}0
simulated for
t\in[-1,1]
.
Left: Particle trajectory. Center: absolute velocity profile for
\kappa{=}10
. Right: absolute velocity profile for
\kappa{=}100
.

With fixed initial conditions (of kind (1) or (2)) and for higher values of
\kappa
one can expect, in general, that the initial conditions may become increasingly less relevant. For some fields configurations we indeed found that, with increasing
\kappa
, the particles get more focused and accumulate like a beam of charged particles along some specific region of space and move asymptotically for higher simulation times. This is exemplified below with two
j{=}0
configurations: the
(0,0,-1)_{I}
configuration in Figure
7
, and the HR configuration in Figure
8
. We have verified this feature not just with symmetric initial conditions of particles like that with initial conditions (1) and (2) (as in Figure
7
), but also in several initial conditions asymmetric with respect to the origin, like particles located randomly inside a sphere of fixed radius about the origin with zero initial velocity, and particles located at the origin but with different magnitudes of velocities. Figure
8
is an illustrative example for both of these latter scenarios of asymmetric initial conditions.

and

. Right: scenario (2C) with

.

and

r{=}0.01
(
R_{max}=0
). Right: particles located at origin and directed randomly (shown with colored arrows) with
|{\bf V}_{0}|{=}0.45
.

This is not always the case though. For some
j{=}\tfrac{1}{2}
and
j{=}1
configurations, and with initial particle positions in a sphere of very small radius about the origin, we are able to observe the splitting of particle trajectories (starting in some specific solid angle regions around the origin) into two, three or even four such asymptotic beams that converge along some particular regions of space (depending on the initial location of these particles in one of these solid angle regions). Trajectories generated by two such
j{=}1
configurations have been illustrated in Figure
9
.

Naturally, there are also regions of unstable trajectories for particles starting between these solid angle regions (see Figure

), which generally include the preferred
z
-axis, since in some cases trajectories that start at rest in the
z
-axis never leave it.

(

) in the the following Figures

,

, and

One very interesting feature of trajectories for some of these field configurations is that they twist and turn in a coherent fashion owing to the symmetry of the background field. For particles with initial condition of kind (2), we see that their trajectories take sharp turns, up to two times, with mild twists before going off asymptotically. This has to do with the presence of strong background electromagnetic fields with knotted field lines. This is clearly demonstrated below in Figures
12
,
13
, and
14
. It is worthwhile to notice in Figure
12
that the particle which was initially at rest moves unperturbed along the
z
-axis; again, this has to do with the fact that these fields have preferred
z
-direction. This feature is even more pronounced in Figure
13
and (the right subfigure of) Figure
14
where we see that particles with ultrarelativistic initial speeds are forced to turn (almost vertically upwards) due to the strong electromagnetic field. These particles later take very interesting twists in a coherent manner. This twisting feature is much more refined for the case where initial particle velocities were directed along the
xy
-plane. Here also, we can safely attribute this behavior of the particle trajectories to the special field configurations, with preferred
z
-direction, that we are working with.

We see in Figure
15
that the trajectories of particles that were initially located on a circle whose normal is along the
z
-axis flow quite smoothly with mild twists for some time before they all turn symmetrically in a coherent way and go off asymptotically. Comparing this with the other case in Figure
15
, where particles split into two asymptotic beams, we realize that this is yet another instance of the preferred choice of direction for the electromagnetic fields influencing the trajectories of particles.
