-axis.

In Figures
16
and
13
we find examples of kind (1) and (2) respectively where both twisting as well as turning of trajectories is prominant. We see in Figure
16
that the particles that start very close to the origin take a longer time to show twists as compared to the ones that start off on a sphere of radius
R_{max}
. This is due to the fact that the field is maximal at
R_{max}
and hence its effect on particles is prominent, as discussed before. We also notice here that the particles sitting along the
z
-axis at
T{=}0
(either on the north pole or on the south pole of this sphere) keep moving along the
z
-axis without any twists or turns. This exemplifies again the fact that these background fields have a preferred direction.

Figure 16
:

Simulation of
N{=}18
particles in scenario (1C) for
(1,-1,1)_{R}
configuration with
t\in[0,1]
. Left:
r{=}0.01
and
\kappa{=}10
. Right:
r{=}0.1
and
\kappa{=}100
.

For higher-spin configurations the maximum of the energy density increases but it gets localized into an increasing number of lobes centered around the origin, due to the presence of higher-spin harmonics. Thus, only particles located very close to the tip of these lobes of maximum energy density get accelerated to ultrarelativistic speeds, while particles located outside (which effectively means most of the space) remain unaffected.

Chapter 5
Non-Abelian solutions:
SU(2)

Here we present cosmic Yang–Mills solution with
SU(2)
gauge group and study their stability behaviour. The contents of this chapter, in large parts, are taken from the published work
[
24
]
. All the graphics in this chapter is due to Gabriel Picanço Costa. I have been thoroughly involved at all stages of this research project.

1
SO(4)
-symmetric cosmic Yang–Mills solution

The Yang–Mills action on this ansatz simplifies to

S\ =\ \frac{-1}{4g^{2}}\int_{{\cal I}\times S^{3}}\!\!\!\mathrm{tr}\ {\cal F}\wedge*{\cal F}\ =\ \frac{6\pi^{2}}{g^{2}}\int_{\cal I}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\ \bigl[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\dot{\psi}^{2}-V(\psi)\bigr]\quad\textrm{with}\quad V(\psi)\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\psi^{2}{-}1)^{2}\ ,

(1)

where
g
here denotes the gauge coupling.
Due to the principle of symmetric criticality
[
34
]
, solutions to the mechanical problem

\ddot{\psi}+V^{\prime}(\psi)\ =\ 0

(2)

will, via (
10
), provide Yang–Mills configurations which extremize the action.
Conservation of energy implies that

{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\dot{\psi}^{2}+V(\psi)\ =\ E\ =\ \textrm{constant}\ ,

(3)

and the generic solution in the double-well potential
V
is periodic in
\tau
with a period
T(E)
.

Hence, fixing a value for
E
and employing time translation invariance to set
\dot{\psi}(0)=0

uniquely determines the classical solution
\psi(\tau)
up to half-period shifts.
Its explicit form is

\psi(\tau)\ =\ \begin{cases}\ {\textstyle\frac{k}{\epsilon}}\,\mathrm{cn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k\bigr)\qquad\qquad\ \ \,\textrm{with}\quad T=4\,\epsilon\,K(k)&\textrm{for}\quad{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}<E<\infty\\[4.0pt]
\ 0\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\!\textrm{with}\quad T=\infty&\textrm{for}\quad E={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\\[4.0pt]
\ \pm\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{sech}\bigl(\sqrt{2}\,\tau\bigr)\qquad\ \textrm{with}\quad T=\infty&\textrm{for}\quad E={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\\[4.0pt]
\ \pm{\textstyle\frac{k}{\epsilon}}\,\mathrm{dn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{k\,\tau}{\epsilon}},{\textstyle\frac{1}{k}}\bigr)\qquad\quad\ \textrm{with}\quad T=2\,{\textstyle\frac{\epsilon}{k}}\,K({\textstyle\frac{1}{k}})\quad&\textrm{for}\quad 0<E<{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\\[4.0pt]
\ \pm 1\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\textrm{with}\quad T=\pi&\textrm{for}\quad E=0\end{cases}\ ,

(4)

where cn and dn denote Jacobi elliptic functions,
K
is the complete elliptic integral of the first kind, and

2\,\epsilon^{2}\ =\ 2k^{2}{-}1\ =\ 1/\sqrt{2E}\qquad\textrm{with}\quad k={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}},1,\infty\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad E=\infty,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},0\ .

(5)

For
E{\gg}\frac{1}{2}
, we have
k^{2}{\to}\frac{1}{2}
, and the solution is well approximated by

\frac{2}{\epsilon}\cos\bigl(\frac{2\sqrt{\pi^{3}}}{\Gamma(1/4)^{2}}\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}\bigr)
.
At the critical value of
E{=}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}
(
k{=}1
), the unstable constant solution coexists
with the celebrated bounce solution, and below it the solution bifurcates into oscillations
in the left or right well of the double-well potential, which halfens the oscillation period.
The two constant minima
\psi=\pm 1
correspond to the vacua
{\cal A}=0
and
{\cal A}=g^{-1}\mathrm{d}g
.
Actually, the time translation freedom is broken by the finite range of
{\cal I}
,
so that time-shifted solutions differ in their boundary values
and also in their values for the total energy and action.

Figure 1
:
Plots of
\psi(\tau)
over one period, for different values of
k^{2}
:

0.500001
(top left),
0.9999999
(top right),
1.0000001
(bottom left) and
2
(bottom right).

The corresponding color-electric and -magnetic field strengths read

{\cal E}_{a}\ =\ {\cal F}_{0a}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\dot{\psi}\,T_{a}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad{\cal B}_{a}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon_{a}^{\ bc}{\cal F}_{bc}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\psi^{2}{-}1)\,T_{a}\ ,

(6)

which yields a finite total energy (on the cylinder) of
6\pi^{2}E/g^{2}
and a finite action
[
13
,
27
]

g^{2}S[\psi]\ =\ 6\pi^{2}\int_{\cal I}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\ \bigl[E-(\psi^{2}{-}1)^{2}\bigr]\ =\ 6\pi^{2}\int_{\cal I}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\ \bigl[\dot{\psi}^{2}-E\bigr]\ \geq\ -3\pi^{2}|{\cal I}|\ .

(7)

The energy-momentum tensor of our SO(4)-symmetric Yang–Mills solutions is readily found as

T\ =\ \frac{3\,E}{g^{2}a^{2}}\bigl(\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{3}}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{3}^{2}\bigr)\ ,

(8)

which is traceless as expected.

The Einstein equations for a closed FLRW universe with cosmological constant
\Lambda

reduce to two independent relations, which can be taken to be its trace and its time-time component.
In conformal time one gets, respectively,

\left.\begin{cases}\quad-R+4\,\Lambda\ =\ 0\\[4.0pt]
\quad R_{\tau\tau}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}R\,a^{2}-\Lambda\,a^{2}\ =\ \kappa\,T_{\tau\tau}\end{cases}\right\}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\left.\begin{cases}\quad\ddot{a}+W^{\prime}(a)\ =\ 0\\[4.0pt]
\quad{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\dot{a}^{2}+W(a)\ =\ \frac{\kappa}{2g^{2}}E\ =:\ E^{\prime}\end{cases}\right\}

(9)

with a gravitational coupling
\kappa=8\pi G
,
a gravitational energy
E^{\prime}

and a cosmological potential

W(a)\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}a^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{\Lambda}{6}}a^{4}\ .

(10)

The two anharmonic oscillators, with potential
V
for the gauge field and potential
W
for gravity,
are coupled only via the balance of their conserved energies,

\frac{1}{\kappa}\bigl[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\dot{a}^{2}+W(a)\bigr]\ =\ \frac{1}{2g^{2}}\bigl[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\dot{\psi}^{2}+V(\psi)\bigr]\ ,

(11)

which is nothing but the Wheeler–DeWitt constraint
H=0
.

Figure 2
:
Plots of the cosmological potential
W(a)
for
\lambda{=}1
and the double-well potential
V
.

The Friedmann equation (
9
), being a mechanical system with an inverted anharmonic
potential (
10
), is again easily solved analytically,

a(\tau)\ =\ \begin{cases}\ \sqrt{{\textstyle\frac{3}{\Lambda}}}\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{\prime}}}\,\sqrt{\frac{1-\mathrm{cn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon^{\prime}}},k^{\prime}\bigr)}{1+\mathrm{cn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon^{\prime}}},k^{\prime}\bigr)}}\qquad\qquad\textrm{with}\quad T^{\prime}=2\,\epsilon^{\prime}K(k^{\prime})&\textrm{for}\quad{\textstyle\frac{3}{8\Lambda}}<E^{\prime}<\infty\\[4.0pt]
\ \sqrt{{\textstyle\frac{3}{2\Lambda}}}\,\tanh\bigl(\tau/\sqrt{2}\bigr)\qquad\qquad\quad\ \textrm{with}\quad T^{\prime}=\infty&\textrm{for}\quad E^{\prime}={\textstyle\frac{3}{8\Lambda}}\\[4.0pt]
\ \sqrt{{\textstyle\frac{3}{\Lambda}}}\,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{\prime}}}\,\sqrt{\frac{1-\mathrm{dn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{k^{\prime}\tau}{\epsilon^{\prime}}},{\textstyle\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}}\bigr)}{1+\mathrm{dn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{k^{\prime}\tau}{\epsilon^{\prime}}},{\textstyle\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}}\bigr)}}\qquad\quad\textrm{with}\quad T^{\prime}=2\,{\textstyle\frac{\epsilon^{\prime}}{k^{\prime}}}K({\textstyle\frac{1}{k^{\prime}}})\quad&\textrm{for}\quad 0<E^{\prime}<{\textstyle\frac{3}{8\Lambda}}\\[10.0pt]
\ 0\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \;\textrm{with}\quad T^{\prime}=\pi&\textrm{for}\quad E^{\prime}=0\end{cases}\ ,

(12)

where we abbreviated
1
1

1

Our
{k^{\prime}}^{2}
should not be confused with the dual modulus
1{-}k^{2}
, which is often denoted this way.

2\,{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{2}\ =\ 2{k^{\prime}}^{2}{-}1\ =\ 1/\sqrt{\smash{{\textstyle\frac{8\Lambda}{3}}}\,E^{\prime}}\qquad\textrm{so that}\quad k^{\prime}={\textstyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}},1,\infty\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad E^{\prime}=\infty,{\textstyle\frac{3}{8\Lambda}},0\ .

(13)

Figure 3
:
Plots of
a(\tau)
over one lifetime, for
\Lambda{=}1
and different values of
{k^{\prime}}^{2}
:

0.505
(top left),
0.9999999
(top right),
1.0000001
(bottom left) and
1.1
(bottom right).

For
E^{\prime}{\gg}\frac{3}{8\Lambda}
, we have
{k^{\prime}}^{2}{\to}\frac{1}{2}
, and the solution is well approximated by

\sqrt{\frac{3}{\Lambda}}\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{\prime}}\tan\bigl(\frac{\sqrt{\pi^{3}}}{\Gamma(1/4)^{2}}\frac{\tau}{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigr)
.
We only listed solutions with initial value
a(0)=0
(big bang).
There exist also (for
E^{\prime}<{\textstyle\frac{3}{8\Lambda}}
) bouncing solutions,
where the universe attains a minimal radius

a_{\textrm{min}}=\bigl[\frac{3}{2\Lambda}(1+\sqrt{1-\smash{{\textstyle\frac{8\Lambda}{3}}}E^{\prime}})\bigr]^{1/2}

between infinite extension in the far past (
t{=}{-}\infty\leftrightarrow\tau{=}0
) and the
far future (
t{=}{+}\infty\leftrightarrow\tau{=}T^{\prime}
). For
E^{\prime}{>}0
they are obtained by
sending
\mathrm{dn}\to-\mathrm{dn}
in (
12
) above.
The quantity
T^{\prime}
listed there is the (conformal) lifetime of the universe,
from the big bang until either the big rip (for
E^{\prime}>\frac{3}{8\Lambda}
)
or the big crunch of an oscillating universe (for
E^{\prime}<\frac{3}{8\Lambda}
).
The solution relevant to our Einstein–Yang–Mills system is entirely determined by the Newtonian energy
E

characterizing the cosmic Yang–Mills field: above the critical value of

E_{\textrm{crit}}\ =\ \frac{2\,g^{2}}{\kappa}\,\frac{3}{8\,\Lambda}

(14)

the universe expands forever (until
t_{\textrm{max}}{=}\infty
),
while below this value it recollapses (at
t_{\textrm{max}}{=}\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\,a(\tau)
).
It demonstrates the necessity of a cosmological constant (whose role may be played
by the Higgs expectation value) as well as the nonperturbative nature of the cosmic Yang–Mills field,
whose contribution to the energy-momentum tensor is of
O(g^{-2})
.

2
Natural perturbation frequencies

Our main task in this paper is an investigation of the stability of the cosmic Yang–Mills solutions
reviewed in the previous section. For this, we should distinguish between global and local stability.
The former is difficult to assess in a nonlinear dynamics but clear from the outset in case of a compact
phase space. The latter refers to short-time behavior induced by linear perturbations around
the reference configuration. We shall look at this firstly, in the present section and the following one.
Here, we set out to diagonalize the fluctuation operator for our time-dependent Yang–Mills backgrounds
and find the natural frequencies.

Even though our cosmic gauge-field configurations are SO(4)-invariant, we must allow for all kinds of
fluctuations on top of it, SO(4)-symmetric perturbations being a very special subclass of them.
A generic gauge potential “nearby” a classical solution
{\cal A}
on
{\cal I}\times S^{3}
can be expanded as

{\cal A}+\Phi\ =\ {\cal A}(\tau,g)\ +\ \sum_{p=1}^{3}\Phi_{0}^{p}(\tau,g)\,T_{p}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\ +\ \sum_{a=1}^{3}\sum_{p=1}^{3}\Phi_{a}^{p}(\tau,g)\,T_{p}\,e^{a}(g)

(1)

with, using
(\mu)=(0,a)
,

\Phi_{\mu}^{p}(\tau,g)\ =\ \sum_{j,m,n}\Phi_{\mu|j;m,n}^{p}(\tau)\,Y_{j;m,n}(g)\ ,

(2)

on which we notice the following actions (suppressing the
\tau
and
g
arguments),

\displaystyle(L_{a}\Phi_{\mu}^{p})_{j;m,n}\ =\ \Phi_{\mu|j;m,n^{\prime}}^{p}\bigl(L_{a})^{n^{\prime}}_{\ n}\ ,\qquad(S_{a}\Phi)_{0}^{p}\ =\ 0\ ,

(3)

\displaystyle(S_{a}\Phi)_{b}^{p}\ =\ -2\varepsilon_{abc}\,\Phi_{c}^{p}\ ,\qquad(T_{a}\Phi)_{\mu}^{p}\ =\ -2\varepsilon_{apq}\,\Phi_{\mu}^{q}\ ,

where
L_{a}
matrix elements are determined from (
28
) and (
30
),
while
S_{a}
are spin operator components. The (metric and gauge) background-covariant derivative reads

D_{\tau}\Phi\ =\ \partial_{\tau}\Phi\quad\textrm{and}\quad D_{a}\Phi\ =\ L_{a}\Phi+[{\cal A}_{a},\Phi]\quad\textrm{with}\quad{\cal A}_{a}\ =\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(1+\psi(\tau)\bigr)\,T_{a}\ ,

(4)

which is equivalent to

D_{a}\Phi_{b}^{p}\ =\ L_{a}\Phi_{b}^{p}-\varepsilon_{abc}\Phi_{c}^{p}+[{\cal A}_{a},\Phi_{b}]^{p}\quad\textrm{since}\quad D_{a}\,e^{b}\ =\ L_{a}\,e^{b}-\varepsilon_{abc}\,e^{c}\ =\ \varepsilon_{abc}\,e^{c}\ .

(5)

The background
{\cal A}
obeys the Coulomb gauge condition,

{\cal A}_{\tau}=0\qquad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\qquad L_{a}{\cal A}_{a}=0\ ,

(6)

but we cannot enforce these equations on the fluctuation
\Phi
.
However, we may impose the Lorenz gauge condition,

D^{\mu}\Phi^{p}_{\mu}\ =\ 0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{0}^{p}-L_{a}\Phi^{p}_{a}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1{+}\psi)(T_{a}\Phi_{a})^{p}\ =\ 0\ ,

(7)

which is seen to couple the temporal and spatial components of
\Phi
in general.
We then linearize the Yang–Mills equations around
{\cal A}
and obtain

D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\Phi_{\mu}-R_{\mu\nu}\Phi^{\nu}+2[{\cal F}_{\mu\nu},\Phi^{\nu}]\ =\ 0

(8)

with the Ricci tensor

R_{\mu 0}\ =\ 0\qquad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\qquad R_{ab}\ =\ 2\delta_{ab}\ .

(9)

After a careful evaluation, the
\mu{=}0
equation yields

\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}-L_{b}L_{b}+2(1{+}\psi)^{2}\bigr]\Phi_{0}^{p}-(1{+}\psi)L_{b}(T_{b}\Phi_{0})^{p}-\dot{\psi}(T_{b}\Phi_{b})^{p}\ =\ 0\ ,

(10)

while the
\mu{=}a
equations read

\displaystyle\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}-L_{b}L_{b}+2(1{+}\psi)^{2}{+}4\bigr]\Phi_{a}^{p}

\displaystyle-(1{+}\psi)L_{b}(T_{b}\Phi)_{a}^{p}-L_{b}(S_{b}\Phi)_{a}^{p}

(11)

\displaystyle-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1{+}\psi)(2{-}\psi)(S_{b}T_{b}\Phi)_{a}^{p}-\dot{\psi}(T_{a}\Phi_{0})^{p}\ =\ 0\ .

It is convenient to package the orbital, spin, isospin, and fluctuation triplets into formal vectors,

\vec{L}=(L_{a})\ ,\qquad\vec{S}=(S_{a})\ ,\qquad\vec{T}=(T_{a})\ ,\qquad\vec{\Phi}=(\Phi_{a})\ ,

(12)

respectively, but they act in different spaces, hence on different indices,
such that
\vec{S}^{2}=\vec{T}^{2}=-8
on
\Phi
.
In this notation, (
7
), (
10
) and (
11
) take the compact form
(suppressing the color index
p
)

\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{0}-\vec{L}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1{+}\psi)\vec{T}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi}\ =\ 0\ ,

(13)

\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}{-}\vec{L}^{2}+2(1{+}\psi)^{2}\bigr]{\Phi}_{0}-(1{+}\psi)\vec{L}{\cdot}\vec{T}\,\Phi_{0}-\dot{\psi}\,\vec{T}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi}\ =\ 0\ ,

(14)

\begin{split}\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}{-}\vec{L}^{2}{-}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\vec{S}^{2}{+}2(1{+}\psi)^{2}\bigr]{\Phi}_{a}&-(1{+}\psi)\vec{L}{\cdot}\vec{T}\,\Phi_{a}-\vec{L}{\cdot}(\vec{S}\,\Phi)_{a}\\
&-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1{+}\psi)(2{-}\psi)\vec{T}{\cdot}(\vec{S}\,\Phi)_{a}-\dot{\psi}\,T_{a}\Phi_{0}\ =\ 0\ .\end{split}

(15)

A few remarks are in order.
First, except for the last term, (
14
) is obtained from (
15
) by setting
\vec{S}=0
,
since
\Phi_{0}
carries no spin index.
Second, both equations can be recast as

\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1{-}\psi}{2}}\vec{L}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1{+}\psi}{2}}(\vec{L}{+}\vec{T})^{2}-2(1{+}\psi)(1{-}\psi)\bigr]\Phi_{0}\ =\ \dot{\psi}\,\vec{T}\cdot\vec{\Phi}\ ,

(16)

\begin{split}\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{(1{-}\psi)(2{+}\psi)}{4}}\vec{L}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{\psi(1{+}\psi)}{4}}(\vec{L}{+}\vec{T})^{2}+{\textstyle\frac{\psi(1{-}\psi)}{4}}(\vec{L}&{+}\vec{S})^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{(1{+}\psi)(2{-}\psi)}{4}}(\vec{L}{+}\vec{T}{+}\vec{S})^{2}\\
&-2(1{+}\psi)(1{-}\psi)\bigr]\vec{\Phi}=\dot{\psi}\vec{T}\Phi_{0}\ ,\end{split}

(17)

which reveals a problem of addition of three spins and a corresponding symmetry under

\psi\ \leftrightarrow\ -\psi\ ,\qquad\vec{L}\ \leftrightarrow\ \vec{L}{+}\vec{T}{+}\vec{S}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\vec{L}{+}\vec{S}\ \leftrightarrow\ \vec{L}{+}\vec{T}\ .

(18)

Third, for constant backgrounds
(\dot{\psi}{=}0)
the temporal fluctuation
\Phi_{0}
decouples
and may be gauged away.
Still, the fluctuation operator in (
17
) is easily diagonalized only when
the coefficient of one of the first three spin-squares vanishes, i.e. for
\vec{L}{=}0
(
j{=}0
),
for the two vacua
\psi{=}{\pm}1
, or for the “meron”
\psi{=}0
.
The latter case has been analyzed by Hosotani
[
11
]
.

Let us decompose the fluctuation problem (
13
)–(
15
) into finite-dimensional blocks
according to a fixed value of the spin
j\in{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mathds{N}
,

\vec{L}^{2}\,\Phi^{p}_{\mu|j}\ =\ -4\,j(j{+}1)\,\Phi^{p}_{\mu|j}

(19)

and suppress the
j
subscript. We employ the following coupling scheme,
2
2

2

Another (less convenient) scheme couples
\vec{L}{+}\vec{S}
, then
(\vec{L}{+}\vec{S}){+}\vec{T}=:\vec{V}
.

\vec{L}{+}\vec{T}=:\vec{U}\qquad\textrm{then}\qquad\vec{U}{+}\vec{S}=(\vec{L}{+}\vec{T}){+}\vec{S}=:\vec{V}\ .

(20)

Clearly,
\vec{U}
and
\vec{V}
act on
\vec{\Phi}
in
su(2)
representations

j\otimes 1
and
j\otimes 1\otimes 1
, respectively. On
\Phi_{0}
, we must put
\vec{S}{=}0

and have just
\vec{V}{=}\vec{U}
act in a
j\otimes 1
representation.
Combining the coupled equations (
14
) and (
15
) to a single linear system for

(\Phi^{p}_{\mu})=(\Phi^{p}_{0},\Phi^{p}_{a})
, we get a
12(2j{+}1)\times 12(2j{+}1)
fluctuation matrix
\Omega^{2}_{(j)}
,

\bigl[\delta_{\mu\nu}^{pq}\,\partial_{\tau}^{2}\ +\ (\Omega^{2}_{(j)})_{\mu\nu}^{pq}\bigr]\,\Phi_{\nu}^{q}\ =\ 0\ .

(21)

Actually, there is an additional overall
(2j{+}1)
-fold degeneracy present due to the trivial action of
the
su(2)_{\textrm{R}}
generators
R_{a}
, which plays no role here and will be suppressed.
Roughly speaking, the
3(2j{+}1)
modes of
\Phi_{0}
are related to gauge modes,
3
3

3

Strictly, they are gauge modes only when
\dot{\psi}{=}0
.
Otherwise, the gauge modes are mixtures with the
\Phi_{a}
modes.

and we still must impose the gauge condition (
13
), which also has
3(2j{+}1)
components.
Therefore, a subspace of dimension
6(2j{+}1)
inside the space of all fluctuations will
represent the physical gauge-equivalence classes in the end.

Our goal is to diagonalize the fluctuation operator (
21
) for a given fixed value of
j
.
It has a block structure,

\Omega^{2}_{(j)}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}\bar{N}&-\dot{\psi}\,T^{\top}\\[6.0pt]
-\dot{\psi}\,T&N\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(22)

where
\bar{N}
and
N
are given by the left-hand sides of (
16
) and (
17
), respectively.
We introduce a basis where
\vec{U}^{2}
,
\vec{V}^{2}
and
V_{3}
are diagonal, i.e.

\vec{U}^{2}\,|uvm\rangle\ =\ -4\,u(u{+}1)\,|uvm\rangle\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\vec{V}^{2}\,|uvm\rangle\ =\ -4\,v(v{+}1)\,|uvm\rangle\ ,

(23)

with
m=-v,\ldots,v
and denote the irreducible
su(2)_{v}
representations with those quantum numbers as
\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}v\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
.
On the
\Phi_{0}
subspace,
u
is redundant since
u{=}v
as
\vec{S}{=}0
.
Working out the tensor products, we encounter the values

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}v\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\ \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{-}2\\
j{-}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\ ;\!\!\!

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{-}1\\
j{-}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle,\ \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{-}1\\
j\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\ ;\ \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j\\
j{-}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\ ,\!\!\!

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j\\
j\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle,\ \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j\\
j{+}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\ ;\ \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{+}1\\
j\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\ ,\!\!\!

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{+}1\\
j{+}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle;\ \bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{+}2\\
j{+}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{on}\ \vec{\Phi}\ ,

(24)

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}v\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{-}1\\
j{-}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle;

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j\\
j\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle;

\displaystyle\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}j{+}1\\
j{+}1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

\displaystyle\quad\textrm{on}\ \Phi_{0}

\displaystyle,

with some representations obviously missing for
j{<}2
.

Let us treat the
\dot{\psi}\,T
term in (
22
) as a perturbation and momentarily put it to zero,
so that
\Omega^{2}_{(j)}
is block-diagonal for the time being.
Then, it is easy to see from (
16
) and (
17
) that
[\vec{V},\bar{N}]=[\vec{U},\bar{N}]=0

and
[\vec{V},N]=0
, even though
[\vec{U},N]\neq 0
because
(\vec{L}{+}\vec{S})^{2}

is not diagonal in our basis. Therefore, we have a degeneracy in
m
.
Furthermore, both
\bar{N}
and
N
decompose into at most three respectively five blocks
with fixed values of
v
ranging from
j{-}2
to
j{+}2
and separated by semicolons in (
24
).
Moreover, the
\bar{N}
blocks are irreducible and trivially also carry a value of
u{=}v
.
In contrast,
N
is not
simply
reducible; its
\vec{V}
representations have multiplicity one, two or three.
Only the
N
blocks with extremal
v
values in (
24
) are irreducible.
The other ones are reducible and contain more than one
\vec{U}
representation,
hence the
u
-spin distinguishes between their (two or three) irreducible
v
subblocks.
The only non-diagonal term in
N
is the
(\vec{L}{+}\vec{S})^{2}
contribution, which couples different copies
of the same
v
-spin to each other, but of course not to any
u{=}v
block of
\bar{N}
,
and does not lift the
V_{3}{=}m
degeneracy.
As a consequence, the unperturbed fluctuation equations
for
\Phi_{0}{=}\Phi_{(\bar{v})}
and
\vec{\Phi}{=}\Phi_{(v,\alpha)}
take the form
(suppressing the
m
index)

\begin{split}\mathbbm{1}_{(\bar{v})}\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}\ +\ \bar{\omega}^{2}_{(\bar{v})}\bigr]\,\Phi_{(\bar{v})}\ =\ 0\qquad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\qquad\mathbbm{1}_{(v)}\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}\ +\ \omega^{2}_{(v,\alpha)}\bigr]\,\Phi_{(v,\alpha)}\ =\ 0\\[4.0pt]
\quad\textrm{for}\quad\dot{\psi}=0\quad\textrm{with}\quad\bar{v}\in\{j{-}1,\ j,\ j{+}1\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad v\in\{j{-}2,\ j{-}1,\ j,\ j{+}1,\ j{+}2\}\ ,\end{split}

(25)

where
\mathbbm{1}_{(v)}
denotes a unit matrix of size
2v{+}1
, and
\alpha
counts the multiplicity
of the
v
-spin representation in
N
(between one and three).
According to (
16
) the unperturbed frequency-squares for
\bar{N}
are the eigenvalues

\bar{\omega}^{2}_{(\bar{v})}\ =\ 2(1{-}\psi)\,j(j{+}1)+2(1{+}\psi)\,\bar{v}(\bar{v}{+}1)-2(1{+}\psi)(1{-}\psi)

(26)

with multiplicity
2\bar{v}{+}1
, hence we get

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}^{2}_{(j-1)}

\displaystyle=\ 2\,\psi^{2}-4j\,\psi+2(2j^{2}{-}1)\ ,

(27)

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}^{2}_{(j)}

\displaystyle=\ 2\,\psi^{2}+2(2j^{2}{+}2j{-}1)\,

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}^{2}_{(j+1)}

\displaystyle=\ 2\,\psi^{2}+4(j{+}1)\,\psi+2(2j^{2}{+}4j{+}1)\ .

Considering
N
in (
17
), we can read off the eigenvalues at
v=j{\pm}2

because in these two extremal cases
(\vec{L}{+}\vec{S})^{2}=\vec{U}^{2}

is already diagonal in the
\bigl\{|uvm\rangle\bigr\}
basis. For the other
v
-values
we must diagonalize a
2{\times}2
or
3{\times}3
matrix to find

\displaystyle\omega^{2}_{(j-2)}

\displaystyle=\ \textrm{root of}\ Q_{j-2}(\lambda)\ =\ -2(2j{-}1)\,\psi+2(2j^{2}{-}2j{+}1)\ ,

(28)

\displaystyle\omega^{2}_{(j-1,\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\ \textrm{two roots of}\ Q_{j-1}(\lambda)\ ,

\displaystyle\omega^{2}_{(j,\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\ \textrm{three roots of}\ Q_{j}(\lambda)\ ,

\displaystyle\omega^{2}_{(j+1,\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\ \textrm{two roots of}\ Q_{j+1}(\lambda)\ ,

\displaystyle\omega^{2}_{(j+2)}

\displaystyle=\ \textrm{root of}\ Q_{j+2}(\lambda)\ =\ 2(2j{+}3)\,\psi+2(2j^{2}{+}6j{+}5)\ ,

each with multiplicity
2v{+}1
, where
Q_{v}
denotes a linear, quadratic or cubic polynomial.
4
4

4

For
j{<}2
some obvious modifications occur due to the missing of
v{<}0
representations.

Let us now turn on the perturbation
\dot{\psi}\,T
, which couples
N
with
\bar{N}
,
and consider the characteristic polynomial
{\cal P}_{j}(\lambda)
of our fluctuation problem,

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{j}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\ \det\,\Bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\bar{N}{-}\lambda&-\dot{\psi}T^{\top}\\[4.0pt]
-\dot{\psi}T&N{-}\lambda\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr)\ =\ \det(N{-}\lambda)\cdot\det\bigl[(\bar{N}{-}\lambda)-\dot{\psi}^{2}\,T^{\top}(N{-}\lambda)^{-1}T\bigr]

(29)

\displaystyle=\ \bigl[{\textstyle\prod}_{v}\det(N_{(v)}{-}\lambda)\bigr]\cdot\det\bigl[(\bar{N}{-}\lambda)-\dot{\psi}^{2}\,T^{\top}\{{\textstyle\bigoplus}_{v}(N_{(v)}{-}\lambda)^{-1}\}\,T\bigr]\ ,

where we made use of

\langle u\,v\,m|\,N\,|u^{\prime}v^{\prime}m^{\prime}>\ =\ \bigl(N_{(v)}\bigr)_{uu^{\prime}}\,\delta_{vv^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\ .

(30)

Since
T
furnishes an
su(2)
representation (and not an intertwiner)
it must be represented by square matrices and thus cannot connect different
v
representations.
Hence the perturbation does not couple different
v
sectors but only links
N
and
\bar{N}

in a common
\bar{v}{=}v
sector. Therefore, it does not affect the extremal sectors
v=j{\pm}2
.
Moreover, switching to a diagonal basis
\{|\alpha vm\rangle\}
for
N
we can simplify to

\displaystyle T^{\top}\{{\textstyle\bigoplus}_{v}(N_{(v)}{-}\lambda)^{-1}\}\,T\bigr]

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\bigoplus}_{\bar{v}}\{T^{\top}(N{-}\lambda)^{-1}T\}_{(\bar{v})}

(31)

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\bigoplus}_{\bar{v}}\Bigl\{{\textstyle\sum}_{\alpha}(\omega^{2}_{(\bar{v},\alpha)}{-}\lambda)^{-1}\bigl(T^{\top}|\alpha\rangle\!\langle\alpha|\,T\bigr)_{(\bar{v})}\Bigr\}\ .

Observing that

\bigl(T^{\top}|\alpha\rangle\!\langle\alpha|\,T\bigr)_{(\bar{v})}=-t_{\bar{v},\alpha}\bigl(\vec{T}^{2}\bigr)_{(\bar{v})}=8\,t_{\bar{v},\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{(\bar{v})}

with some coefficient functions
t_{\bar{v},\alpha}(\psi)
, with
\sum_{\alpha}t_{\bar{v},\alpha}=1
,
we learn that the
V_{3}
degeneracy remains intact and arrive at (
\bar{v}\in\{j{-}1,j,j{+}1\}
)

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{j}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\ \bigl[{\textstyle\prod}_{v}Q_{v}(\lambda)^{2v+1}\bigr]\cdot{\textstyle\prod}_{\bar{v}}\bigl\{(\bar{\omega}^{2}_{(\bar{v})}{-}\lambda)-8\dot{\psi}^{2}{\textstyle\sum}_{\alpha}t_{\bar{v},\alpha}(\omega^{2}_{(\bar{v},\alpha)}{-}\lambda)^{-1}\bigr\}^{2\bar{v}+1}

(32)

\displaystyle=\ (\omega^{2}_{(j-2)}{-}\lambda)^{2j-3}\cdot(\omega^{2}_{(j+2)}{-}\lambda)^{2j+5}\cdot{\textstyle\prod}_{\bar{v}}\bigl\{(\bar{\omega}^{2}_{(\bar{v})}{-}\lambda)\,Q_{\bar{v}}(\lambda)-8\dot{\psi}^{2}P_{\bar{v}}(\lambda)\bigr\}^{2\bar{v}+1}

\displaystyle=\ (\omega^{2}_{(j-2)}{-}\lambda)^{2j-3}\cdot(\omega^{2}_{(j+2)}{-}\lambda)^{2j+5}\cdot{\textstyle\prod}_{\bar{v}}R_{\bar{v}}(\lambda)^{2\bar{v}+1}\ ,

where
P_{\bar{v}}=Q_{\bar{v}}\sum_{\alpha}t_{\bar{v},\alpha}(\omega^{2}_{(\bar{v},\alpha)}{-}\lambda)^{-1}

is a polynomial of degree one less than
Q_{\bar{v}}
since all poles cancel, and
R_{\bar{v}}
is a
polynomial of one degree more.
We list the polynomials
Q_{v}
,
P_{\bar{v}}
and
R_{\bar{v}}
for
j{\leq}2
in the Appendix.

To summarize, by a successive basis change (
m^{\prime}=-j,\ldots,j
and
m=-v,\ldots,v
)

\bigl\{|\mu\,p\,m^{\prime}\rangle\bigr\}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\bigl\{|\bar{v}m\rangle,|uvm\rangle\bigr\}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\bigl\{|\bar{v}m\rangle,|\alpha vm\rangle\bigr\}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\bigl\{|\beta vm\rangle\bigr\}

(33)

we have diagonalized (
21
) to

\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}+\Omega^{2}_{(j,v,\beta)}\bigr]\,\Phi_{(v,\beta)}\ =\ 0\qquad\textrm{with}\quad v\in\{j{-}2,j{-}1,j,j{+}1,j{+}2\}\ ,

(34)

where
\Omega^{2}_{(j,v,\beta)}
are the distinct roots of the characteristic polynomial
{\cal P}_{j}
in (
32
),
and (for
j{\geq}2
) the multiplicity
\beta
takes
1,3,4,3,1
values, respectively:

\Omega^{2}_{(j,j\pm 2)}=\omega^{2}_{(j\pm 2)}\ ,\quad\Omega^{2}_{(j,j\pm 1,\beta)}=\textrm{three roots of}\ R_{j\pm 1}(\lambda)\ ,\quad\Omega^{2}_{(j,j,\beta)}=\textrm{four roots of}\ R_{j}(\lambda)\ .

(35)

The reflection symmetry (
18
) implies that

\Omega^{2}_{(v,j,\cdot)}(\psi)=\Omega^{2}_{(j,v,\cdot)}(-\psi)
.
For
j{<}2
, obvious modifications occur due to the absence of some
v
representations.

We still have to discuss the gauge condition (
13
), which can be cast into the form

0\ =\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}\Phi_{0}-\bigl[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1{-}\psi)\vec{L}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1{+}\psi)\vec{U}\bigr]\cdot\vec{\Phi}\ =\ \mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}\Phi_{(\bar{v},\bar{m})}-K_{\bar{v},\bar{m}}^{\ v,m,\alpha}(\psi)\,\Phi_{(v,m,\alpha)}

(36)

with a
3(2j{+}1){\times}7(2j{+}1)
linear (in
\psi
) matrix function
K
.
5
5

5

We have to bring back the
m
indices because the gauge condition is not diagonal in them.

Here the
v
sum runs over
(j{-}1,j,j{+}1)
only,
since the gauge condition (
13
) has components only in the middle three
v
sectors,
like the gauge-mode equation (
14
).
It does not restrict the extremal
v
sectors
v=j{\pm}2
, since these fluctuations
do not couple to the gauge sector
\Phi_{0}
and are entirely physical.
For the middle three
v
sectors (labelled by
\bar{v}
), the
\dot{\psi}\,T
perturbation leads to
a mixing of the
N
modes with the
\bar{N}
gauge modes, so their levels will avoid crossing.
Performing the corresponding final basis change, the gauge condition takes the form

\bigl[L_{\bar{v},\bar{m}}^{\ \bar{v}^{\prime}\!,\bar{m}^{\prime}\!,\beta}(\psi)\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}-M_{\bar{v},\bar{m}}^{\ \bar{v}^{\prime}\!,\bar{m}^{\prime}\!,\beta}(\psi)\bigr]\,\Phi_{(\bar{v}^{\prime}\!,\bar{m}^{\prime}\!,\beta)}\ =\ 0

(37)

with certain
3(2j{+}1){\times}10(2j{+}1)
matrix functions
L
and
M
.
This linear equation represents conditions on the normal mode functions
\Phi_{(\bar{v},\bar{m},\beta)}

and defines a
7(2j{+}1)
-dimensional subspace of physical fluctuations,
which of course still contains a
3(2j{+}1)
-dimensional subspace of gauge modes.
For
j{<}1
, these numbers are systematically smaller.
Together with the two extremal
v
sectors, we end up with
(7-3+2)(2j{+}1)=6(2j{+}1)

physical degrees of freedom for any given value of
j({\geq}2)
, as advertized earlier.

We conclude this section with more details for the simplest examples,
which are constant backgrounds and
j{=}0
backgrounds.
For the vacuum background, say
\psi=-1
, which is isospin degenerate, one gets

\bigl(\partial_{\tau}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\vec{L}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\vec{L}{+}\vec{S})^{2}\bigr)\,\vec{\Phi}\ =\ 0,\qquad\vec{L}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi}=0\ ,\qquad\Phi_{0}=0\ .

(38)

It yields the positive eigenfrequency-squares

\omega^{2}_{(j,u^{\prime})}\ =\ 2j(j{+}1)+2u^{\prime}(u^{\prime}{+}1)\ =\ \begin{cases}\ 4j^{2}\ \textrm{at}\ j{{\geq}1}&\quad\textrm{for}\quad u^{\prime}=j{-}1\\
\ 4j(j{+}1)&\quad\textrm{for}\quad u^{\prime}=j\\
\ 4(j{+}1)^{2}&\quad\textrm{for}\quad u^{\prime}=j{+}1\end{cases}

(39)

for
j=0,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},1,\ldots
,
but the
\vec{L}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi}=0
constraint removes the
u^{\prime}{=}j
modes.
Clearly, all (constant) eigenfrequency-squares are positive, hence the vacuum is stable.

For the “meron” background,
\psi\equiv 0
, one has

\bigl(\partial_{\tau}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\vec{L}^{2}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\vec{L}{+}\vec{T}{+}\vec{S})^{2}-2\bigr)\,\vec{\Phi}\ =\ 0,\qquad\bigl(\vec{L}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\vec{T}\bigr)\cdot\vec{\Phi}=0\ ,\qquad\Phi_{0}=0\ .

(40)

In this case, we read off

\omega^{2}_{(j,v)}+2\ =\ 2j(j{+}1)+2v(v{+}1)\ =\ \begin{cases}\ 4(j^{2}{-}j{+}1)&\quad\textrm{for}\quad v=j{-}2\qquad(0\ \textrm{to}\ 1\times)\\
\ 4j^{2}&\quad\textrm{for}\quad v=j{-}1\qquad(0\ \textrm{to}\ 2\times)\\
\ 4j(j{+}1)&\quad\textrm{for}\quad v=j\qquad\quad\ (1\ \textrm{to}\ 3\times)\\
\ 4(j{+}1)^{2}&\quad\textrm{for}\quad v=j{+}1\qquad(1\ \textrm{to}\ 2\times)\\
\ 4(j^{2}{+}3j{+}3)&\quad\textrm{for}\quad v=j{+}2\qquad(1\times)\end{cases}\ ,

(41)

but the constraint removes one copy from each of the three middle cases (and less when
j{<}1
).
We end up with a spectrum
\{\omega^{2}\}=\{-2,1,6,7,10,\ldots\}
with certain degeneracies
[
11
]
.
The single non-degenerate negative mode
\omega^{2}_{(0,0)}{=}{-}2
is a singlet,
\Phi_{a}^{p}=\delta_{a}^{p}\phi(\tau)
,
and it corresponds to rolling down the local maximum of the double-well potential.
The meron is stable against all other perturbations.

For a time-varying background, the natural frequencies
\Omega_{(j,v,\beta)}

inherit a
\tau
dependence from the background
\psi(\tau)
.
Direct diagonalization is still possible for
j{=}0
, where we should solve

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{0}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(1{+}\psi)\vec{T}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi}\ =\ 0\ ,

(42)

\displaystyle\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}+2(1{+}\psi)^{2}\bigr]{\Phi}_{0}-\dot{\psi}\,\vec{T}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi}\ =\ 0\ ,

\displaystyle\bigl[\partial_{\tau}^{2}+2(3\psi^{2}{-}1)-{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}(1{+}\psi)(2{-}\psi)(\vec{S}{+}\vec{T})^{2}\bigr]\ \vec{\Phi}-\dot{\psi}\,\vec{T}\,\Phi_{0}\ =\ 0\ ,

with

(\vec{S}{+}\vec{T})^{2}\ =\ \vec{V}^{2}\ =\ -4\,v(v{+}1)\ =\ 0,-8,-24\qquad\textrm{for}\quad v=0,1,2\ .

(43)

It implies the unperturbed frequencies (suppressing the
j
index)

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}_{(1)}^{2}

\displaystyle=2(\psi{+}1)^{2}\ \ (3\times)\ ,\quad\omega_{(0)}^{2}=2(3\psi^{2}{-}1)\ \ (1\times)\ ,

(44)

\displaystyle\omega_{(1)}^{2}

\displaystyle=2(2\psi^{2}{+}\psi{+}1)\ \ (3\times),\quad\omega_{(2)}^{2}=2(3\psi{+}5)\ \ (5\times)

for

\displaystyle(\Phi_{0})^{p}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigl(\Phi_{(\bar{v}=1)}\bigr)^{p}\ =:\ \delta^{pb}\bar{\phi}_{b}\ ,

(45)

\displaystyle(\vec{\Phi})_{a}^{p}

\displaystyle\equiv\bigl(\Phi_{(0)}+\Phi_{(1)}+\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)^{p}_{a}\ =:\ \phi\,\delta_{a}^{p}+\epsilon^{p}_{\ ab}\,\phi_{b}+(\phi_{(ab)}{-}\delta_{ab}\phi)\delta^{bp}\ ,

as long as
\dot{\psi}
is ignored.
There are no
v
-spin multiplicities (larger than one) here.
Turning on
\dot{\psi}
and observing that
(\vec{T}{\cdot}\vec{\Phi})^{p}\sim\delta^{pb}\phi_{b}
,
the characteristic polynomial of the coupled
12{\times}12
system in the
|uvm\rangle
basis reads

{\cal P}_{0}(\lambda)\ =\ \det\begin{pmatrix}(\bar{\omega}_{(1)}^{2}{-}\lambda)\mathbbm{1}_{3}&0&-\dot{\psi}\,T_{(1)}^{\top}&0\\[4.0pt]
0&(\omega_{(0)}^{2}{-}\lambda)\mathbbm{1}_{1}&0&0\\[4.0pt]
-\dot{\psi}\,T_{(1)}&0&(\omega_{(1)}^{2}{-}\lambda)\mathbbm{1}_{3}&0\\[4.0pt]
0&0&0&(\omega_{(2)}^{2}{-}\lambda)\mathbbm{1}_{5}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(46)

Specializing the general discussion above to
j{=}0
, we find just
t_{1}{=}1
so that
P_{1}{=}1
and arrive at

\displaystyle{\cal P}_{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\ (\omega_{(0)}^{2}{-}\lambda)^{1}(\omega_{(1)}^{2}{-}\lambda)^{3}(\omega_{(2)}^{2}{-}\lambda)^{5}\bigl[(\bar{\omega}_{(1)}^{2}{-}\lambda)-8\dot{\psi}^{2}(\omega_{(1)}^{2}{-}\lambda)^{-1}\bigr]^{3}

(47)

\displaystyle=\ (\omega_{(0)}^{2}{-}\lambda)(\omega_{(2)}^{2}{-}\lambda)^{5}\bigl\{(\bar{\omega}_{(\bar{1})}^{2}{-}\lambda)(\omega_{(1)}^{2}{-}\lambda)-8\dot{\psi}^{2}\bigr\}^{3}\ .

We see that the frequencies
\Omega_{(0)}^{2}{=}\omega_{(0)}^{2}
and
\Omega_{(2)}^{2}{=}\omega_{(2)}^{2}

are unchanged and given by (
44
), while the gauge mode
\bar{\omega}_{(\bar{1})}^{2}
gets
entangled with the (unphysical)
v{=}1
mode to produce the pair

\displaystyle\Omega^{2}_{(1,\pm)}

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\bar{\omega}_{(\bar{1})}^{2}{+}\omega_{(1)}^{2})\,\pm\sqrt{{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}(\bar{\omega}_{(\bar{1})}^{2}{+}\omega_{(1)}^{2})^{2}-\bar{\omega}_{(\bar{1})}^{2}\omega_{(1)}^{2}+8\dot{\psi}^{2}}

(48)

\displaystyle=\ 3\psi^{2}{+}3\psi{+}2\,\pm\sqrt{\psi^{2}(\psi{-}1)^{2}+8\dot{\psi}^{2}}

with a triple degeneracy. There are avoided crossings at
\psi{=}0
and
\psi{=}1
.
Removing the unphysical and gauge modes in pairs, we remain with the singlet mode

\Omega_{(0,0)}^{2}
and the fivefold-degenerate
\Omega_{(0,2)}^{2}
.
For all higher spins
j{>}0
, analytic expressions for the natural frequencies
\Omega_{(j,v,\beta)}

now require merely solving a few polynomial equations of order four at worst.
We have done so up to
j{=}2
and list them in the Appendix
but refrain from giving further explicit examples here.

Figure 4
:
Plots of
\Omega^{2}_{(0,v,\beta)}(\tau)
over one period, for different values of
k^{2}
:

0.51
(top left),
0.99
(top right),
1.01
(bottom left) and
5
(bottom right).

Figure 5
:
Plots of
\Omega^{2}_{(2,v,\beta)}(\tau)
over one period, for different values of
k^{2}
:

0.505
(top left),
0.550
(top right),
0.999
(bottom left) and
1.001
(bottom right).

From the cases of
j{=}0
and
j{=}2
displayed below one can see that some of the normal modes
dip into the negative regime, i.e. their frequency-squares become negative, for a certain fraction of the time
\tau
.
Because of this and, quite generally, due to the
\tau
variability of the natural frequencies,
it is not easy to predict the long-term evolution of the fluctuation modes.
Clearly, the stability of the zero solution
\Phi{\equiv}0
,
equivalent to the linear stability of the background Yang–Mills configuration,
is not simply decided by the sign of the
\tau
-average of the corresponding frequency-square.

3
Stability analysis: stroboscopic map and Floquet theory

The diagonalized linear fluctuation equation (
34
) represents
a bunch of Hill’s equations, where the frequency-squared is a root of a polynomial of order up to four
with coefficients given by a polynomial of twice that order in Jacobi elliptic functions.
A unique solution requires fixing two initial conditions, and so
for each fluctuation
\Phi_{(j,v,\beta)}
there is a two-dimensional solution space.
It is well known that Hill’s equation, e.g. in the limit of Mathieu’s equation, displays parametric resonance phenomena,
which can stabilize otherwise unstable systems or destabilize otherwise stable ones.

For oscillating dynamical systems with periodically varying frequency,
there exist some general tools to analyze linear stability.
Switching to a Hamiltonian picture and to phase space,
it is convenient to transform the second-order differential equation into
a system of two coupled first-order equations (suppressing all quantum numbers),

\bigl[\partial^{2}_{\tau}+\Omega^{2}(\tau)\bigr]\Phi(\tau)\ =\ 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\partial_{\tau}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\[4.0pt]
-\Omega^{2}&0\ \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}\ =:\ \mathrm{i}\,\widehat{\Omega}(\tau)\,\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(1)

where the frequency
\Omega(\tau)
is
T
-periodic (sometimes
\frac{T}{2}
-periodic) in
\tau
.
The solution to this first-order system is formally given by

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}(\tau)\ =\ {\cal T}\exp\,\Bigl\{\int_{0}^{\tau}\!\mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime}\ \mathrm{i}\,\widehat{\Omega}(\tau^{\prime})\Bigr\}\,\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}(0)\ ,

(2)

where
{\cal T}
denotes time ordering.
Because of the time dependence of
\Omega
, the time evolution operator above
is not homogeneous thus does not constitute a one-parameter group, except when
the propagation interval is an integer multiple of the period
T
. For
\tau{=}T
,
one speaks of the stroboscopic map
[
35
]

M\ :=\ {\cal T}\exp\,\Bigl\{\int_{0}^{T}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\ \mathrm{i}\,\widehat{\Omega}(\tau)\Bigr\}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}(nT)\ =\ M^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}\end{pmatrix}(0)\ .

(3)

The linear map
M
is a functional of the chosen background solution
\psi

and hence depends on its parameter
E
or
k
.
This background is Lyapunov stable if the trivial solution
\Phi{\equiv}0
is,
which is decided by the two eigenvalues
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
of
M
.
Since the system is Hamiltonian,
\det M{=}1
, we have three cases:

\displaystyle|\mathrm{tr}\,M|>2

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mu_{i}\in\mathds{R}

\displaystyle\quad\;\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\textrm{hyperbolic/boost}

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\textrm{strongly unstable}\ ,

(4)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{tr}\,M|=2

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mu_{i}=\pm 1

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\textrm{parabolic/translation}

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\textrm{marginally stable}\ ,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{tr}\,M|<2

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mu_{i}\in\textrm{U}(1)

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\textrm{elliptic/rotation}

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\textrm{strongly stable}\ .

Clearly,
|\mathrm{tr}\,M|
determines the linear stability of our classical solution.

Let us thus try to evaluate the trace of the stroboscopic map
M
,
making use of the special form of the matrix
\widehat{\Omega}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,M

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{i}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\ \ldots\int_{0}^{\tau_{n-1}}\!\!\!\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{n}\ \mathrm{tr}\,\bigl[\widehat{\Omega}(\tau_{1})\,\widehat{\Omega}(\tau_{2})\cdots\widehat{\Omega}(\tau_{n})\bigr]

(5)

\displaystyle=\ 2+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\ \ldots\int_{0}^{\tau_{n-1}}\!\!\!\!\mathrm{d}\tau_{n}\ H_{n}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{n})\Omega^{2}(\tau_{1})\,\Omega^{2}(\tau_{2})\,\cdots\Omega^{2}(\tau_{n})

\displaystyle\textrm{with}

\displaystyle H_{n}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{n})\ =\ (\tau_{1}{-}\tau_{2})(\tau_{2}{-}\tau_{3})\cdots(\tau_{n-1}{-}\tau_{n})(\tau_{n}{-}\tau_{1}{+}1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad H_{1}(\tau_{1})=1\ .

It is convenient to scale the time variable such as to normalize the period to unity,

\tau=T\,x\qquad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\qquad\Omega^{2}(Tx)=:\omega^{2}(x)\ ,\quad H(\{Tx\})=:h(\{x\})\ ,

(6)

hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,M

\displaystyle=\ 2+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigl(-T^{2}\bigr)^{n}\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\ldots\int_{0}^{x_{n-1}}\!\!\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{n}\,h_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\omega^{2}(x_{1})\omega^{2}(x_{2})\cdots\omega^{2}(x_{n})

(7)

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2}{(2n)!}\,M_{n}\,\bigl(-T^{2}\bigr)^{n}\ =:\ 2-M_{1}T^{2}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{12}}M_{2}T^{4}-{\textstyle\frac{1}{360}}M_{3}T^{6}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{20160}}M_{4}T^{8}-\ldots\ .

It is impossible to evaluate the integrals
M_{n}
without explicit knowledge of
\omega^{2}(x)
.
As a crude guess, we replace the weight function by its (constant) average value

\langle h_{n}\rangle\ :=\ \frac{1}{n!}\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\ \ldots\int_{0}^{x_{n-1}}\!\!\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{n}\ h_{n}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\ =\ \frac{2\,n!}{(2n)!}

(8)

and obtain

M_{n}\ =\ \frac{(2n)!}{2}\,\langle h_{n}\rangle\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{0}^{x_{1}}\!\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{2}\ \ldots\int_{0}^{x_{n-1}}\!\!\!\!\mathrm{d}x_{n}\ \prod_{i=1}^{n}\omega^{2}(x_{i})\ =\ \Bigl(\int_{0}^{1}\!\!\mathrm{d}x\ \omega^{2}(x)\Bigr)^{n}\ =:\ \langle\,\omega^{2}\rangle^{n}\ ,

(9)

which yields

\mathrm{tr}\,M\ =\ 2\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n)!}\,\langle\,\omega^{2}\rangle^{n}\,T^{2n}\ =\ 2\,\cos\bigl(\sqrt{\langle\,\omega^{2}\rangle}\,T\bigr)\ .

(10)

This expression indicates stability as long as
\langle\omega^{2}\rangle>0
.
However, the result for the
j{=}0
singlet mode
\omega^{2}=\Omega^{2}_{(0,0)}
in (
11
)
already showed that the averaged frequency-squared may turn negative in certain domains
thus changing the cos into a cosh there.

To do better, let us look at the individual terms
M_{n}
in (
7
) for the simplest case
of the SO(4) singlet fluctuation, i.e.
\Omega^{2}_{(0,0)}=6\psi^{2}{-}2
in (
44
).
Its average frequency-square is easily computed to be

\langle\,\Omega_{(0,0)}^{2}\rangle\ =\ \frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\Bigl(6\frac{E(k)}{K(k)}+4k^{2}-5\Bigr)\ ,

(11)

where
E(k)
and
K(k)
denote the second and first complete elliptic integrals, respectively.
Plotting this expression as a function of the modulus
k
, we see that it
becomes negative only in a very narrow range around
k{=}1
, namely for
|k{-}1|\lesssim 0.00005
.

Figure 6
:
Plot of
\langle\Omega^{2}_{(0,0)}\rangle
as a function of
k
, with detail on the right.

We have only been able to analytically evaluate (with
k{<}1
for simplicity)

M_{1}\ =\ \langle\,\Omega_{(0,0)}^{2}\rangle\quad\textrm{and}\quad M_{2}\ =\ \langle\,\Omega_{(0,0)}^{2}\rangle^{2}\ -\ \frac{1}{\epsilon^{4}}\Bigl(9\frac{2{-}k^{2}}{K(k)^{2}}-27\frac{E(k)}{K(k)^{3}}+\frac{9\,\pi^{2}}{4\,K(k)^{4}}\Bigr)\ ,

(12)

which does not suffice to rule out instability.
Indeed, numerical studies show that
M_{n}
as a function of
k
looses its positivity in a range
around
k{=}1
which increases with
n
, where the series (
7
) ceases to be alternating.
Moreover, even in the limit of a very large background amplitude,
k^{2}\to\frac{1}{2}
, we find that

\langle\,\Omega_{(0,0)}^{2}\rangle\ \to\ \frac{24\,\pi^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}\,\Gamma(\frac{1}{4})^{4}}\ \approx\ \frac{1.37}{\epsilon^{2}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\sqrt{\langle\,\Omega^{2}\rangle}\,T\ \to\ \sqrt{24\,\pi}\ \approx\ 8.68\ ,

(13)

implying that we must push the series in (
7
) at least to
O(M_{10}T^{20})
,
even though it turns out that
M_{n}<\langle\,\Omega^{2}_{(0,0)}\rangle^{n}
at
k^{2}=\frac{1}{2}
for
n>1
.

For a more complete analysis of linear stability in an oscillating system with time-dependent frequency
we can take recourse to Floquet theory.
It tells us that a general fundamental matrix solution

\widehat{\Phi}(\tau)\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{1}&\Phi_{2}\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}_{1}&\dot{\Phi}_{2}\end{pmatrix}(\tau)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\partial_{\tau}\widehat{\Phi}(\tau)\ =\ \mathrm{i}\,\widehat{\Omega}(\tau)\,\widehat{\Phi}(\tau)

(14)

of our system (
1
) with some initial condition
\widehat{\Phi}(0)=\widehat{\Phi}_{0}

can be expressed in so-called Floquet normal form as

\widehat{\Phi}(\tau)\ =\ Q(\tau)\;\mathrm{e}^{\tau R}\qquad\textrm{with}\qquad Q(\tau{+}2T)\ =\ Q(\tau)\ ,

(15)

where
Q(\tau)
and
R
are real
2{\times}2
matrices,
so that the time dependence of the frequency can be transformed away by a change of coordinates,

\Psi(\tau)\ :=\ Q(\tau)^{-1}\widehat{\Phi}(\tau)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\partial_{\tau}\Psi(\tau)\ =\ R\,\Psi(\tau)\ .

(16)

Due to the identity

\widehat{\Phi}(\tau{+}T)\ =\ \widehat{\Phi}(\tau)\,\widehat{\Phi}(0)^{-1}\,\widehat{\Phi}(T)\ =\ \widehat{\Phi}(T)\,\widehat{\Phi}(0)^{-1}\,\widehat{\Phi}(\tau)\ =\ M\,\widehat{\Phi}(\tau)

(17)

we see that our stroboscopic map
M
is nothing but the monodromy, and

M^{2}\ =\ \widehat{\Phi}(2T)\,\widehat{\Phi}(0)^{-1}\ =\ Q(0)\,\widehat{\Phi}(0)^{-1}\,\widehat{\Phi}(2T)\,Q(0)^{-1}\ =\ Q(0)\,\mathrm{e}^{2RT}\,Q(0)^{-1}\ ,

(18)

so that its eigenvalues (or characteristic multipliers)

\mu_{i}=\mathrm{e}^{\rho_{i}T}\qquad\textrm{for}\quad i=1,2

(19)

define a pair of (complex) Floquet exponents
\rho_{i}

whose real parts are the Lyapunov exponents.
Since
\mu_{1}\mu_{2}=1
implies that
\rho_{1}{+}\rho_{2}=0
,
our system is linearly stable if and only if both eigenvalues
\rho_{i}
of
R

are purely imaginary (or zero).

Generally it is impossible to find analytically the monodromy pertaining to
a normal mode
\Phi_{(j,v,\beta)}
.
6
6

6

An exception is the SO(4) singlet perturbation
\Phi_{(0,0)}
, to be treated in the following section.

However, we can get a qualitative understanding by looking numerically at some examples.
Before numerically integrating Hill’s equation, however, let us estimate at which energies
E

or, rather, moduli
k
, possible resonance frequencies might occur.
To this end, we determine the period-average of the natural frequency
\Omega_{(j,v,\beta)}

and compare it to its modulation frequency
{\textstyle\frac{2\pi}{T}}
. If we model

\Omega^{2}(\tau)\ =\ \langle\,\Omega^{2}\rangle\,\bigl(1+h(\tau)\bigr)\quad\textrm{with}\quad\langle\,\Omega^{2}\rangle={\textstyle\frac{1}{T}}\smallint_{0}^{T}\!\mathrm{d}\tau\ \Omega^{2}(\tau)\quad\textrm{and}\quad h(\tau)\ \propto\ \cos(2\pi\tau/T)\ ,

(20)

where
T=4\,\epsilon\,K(k)
, then the resonance condition is met for

\sqrt{\langle\Omega^{2}\rangle}\ =\ \ell\,\frac{\pi}{T}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad k=k_{\ell}(j,v,\beta)\qquad\textrm{for}\quad\ell=1,2,3,\ldots\ .

(21)

Since this model reproduces only the rough features of
\Omega^{2}(\tau)
, we expect potential instability
due to parametric resonance effects in a band around or near the values
k_{\ell}
.

Below we display, together with the would-be resonant values
k_{\ell}
,
the function
\mathrm{tr}M(k)
for the sample cases of
(j,v)=(2,0)
and
(2,2)
.

Figure 7
:
Plot of
\mathrm{tr}\,M(k)
for
(j,v)=(2,0)
, with detail on the right. Would-be resonances marked in red.

Figure 8
:
Plots of
\mathrm{tr}\,M(k)
for
(j,v)=(2,2)
and
\beta=1,2,3,4
. Would-be resonances marked in red.

One sees that, on both sides of the critical value of
E{=}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}
(or
k{=}1
),
corresponding to the double-well local maximum, the
k_{\ell}
values accumulate at the critical point.
But while for
k{>}1
(energy below the critical point)
\mathrm{tr}M(k)
oscillates between values close to
2

in magnitude and thus exponential growth is rare and mild, for
k{<}1
(energy above the critical point)
the oscillatory behavior of
\mathrm{tr}M(k)
comes with an amplitude exceeding 2 and growing with energy.
Hence, in this latter regime stable and unstable bands alternate.
This is supported by long-term numerical integration, as we demonstrate by plotting

\Phi(\tau)
for
(j,v,\beta)=(2,2,1)
with initial values
\Phi(0){=}1
and
\dot{\Phi}(0){=}0

on both sides very close to the end of the first instability
(at the highest value of
E
or the lowest value of
k
).

Figure 9
:
Plot of
\Phi(\tau)
for
(j,v,\beta)=(2,2,1)
and
k{=}0.73198
(left) and
k{=}0.73199
(right).

Most relevant for the cosmological application is the regime of very large energies,
E\to\infty
(or
k\to 1/\sqrt{2}
).
In this limit, we observe the following universal behavior.
Because the period
T
collapses with
\epsilon{=}\sqrt{k^{2}{-}1/2}
, we rescale

{\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}}=z\in[0,4K({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})]\ ,\quad\epsilon\,\psi=\tilde{\psi}\ ,\quad\epsilon^{2}\dot{\psi}=\partial_{z}\tilde{\psi}\ ,\quad\epsilon^{2}\Omega^{2}=\tilde{\Omega}^{2}\ ,\quad\epsilon^{2}\lambda=\tilde{\lambda}

(22)

so that the tilded quantities remain finite in the limit,
and find, with
\bar{\omega}^{2}_{(\bar{v})}\to 2\psi^{2}
,
7
7

7

For the cases
(j,v)=(0,1)
and
(1,0)
, the factor
\tilde{\lambda}
is missing;
for
(j,v)=(0,0)
, one only has
R=Q\sim(\tilde{\lambda}{-}6\tilde{\psi}^{2})
.

\displaystyle Q_{(v\pm 2)}\ \sim\ \tilde{\lambda}\ ,

(23)

\displaystyle Q_{(v\pm 1)}\ \sim\ \tilde{\lambda}\,(\tilde{\lambda}-4\tilde{\psi}^{2})\ ,\qquad\qquad R_{(v\pm 1)}\ \sim\ \tilde{\lambda}\,\bigl[(\tilde{\lambda}-2\tilde{\psi}^{2})(\tilde{\lambda}-4\tilde{\psi}^{2})-8(\mbox{$\partial$}_{z}\tilde{\psi})^{2}\bigr]\ ,

\displaystyle Q_{(v)}\ \sim\ \tilde{\lambda}\,(\tilde{\lambda}-4\tilde{\psi}^{2})(\tilde{\lambda}-6\tilde{\psi}^{2})\ ,\quad R_{(v)}\ \sim\ \tilde{\lambda}\,\bigl[(\tilde{\lambda}-2\tilde{\psi}^{2})(\tilde{\lambda}-4\tilde{\psi}^{2})-8(\mbox{$\partial$}_{z}\tilde{\psi})^{2}\bigr](\tilde{\lambda}-6\tilde{\psi}^{2})\ ,

because all
j
-dependent terms in the polynomials are subleading and drop out in the limit.
Factorizing the
R
polynomials, we find the four universal natural frequency-squares

\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{1}=0\ ,\quad\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{2}=3\,\tilde{\psi}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{\psi}^{4}+8(\mbox{$\partial$}_{z}\tilde{\psi})^{2}}\ ,\quad\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{3}=3\,\tilde{\psi}^{2}+\sqrt{\tilde{\psi}^{4}+8(\mbox{$\partial$}_{z}\tilde{\psi})^{2}}\ ,\quad\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{4}=6\,\tilde{\psi}^{2}\ .

(24)

One must pay attention, however, to the fact that the avoided crossings disappear in the
\epsilon\to 0
limit.
Therefore, the correct limiting frequencies to input into

\bigl[\mbox{$\partial$}_{z}^{2}+\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{(j,v,\beta)}\bigr]\,\tilde{\Phi}_{(j,v,\beta)}\ =\ 0

(25)

are

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{(j,j\pm 2)}\ \ =0\ ,

(26)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{(j,j\pm 1,\beta)}\in\bigl\{\textrm{min}(\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{1},\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{2}),\ \textrm{max}(\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{1},\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{2}),\ \tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{3}\bigr\}\ ,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{(j,j,\beta)}\ \ \ \in\bigl\{\textrm{min}(\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{1},\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{2}),\ \textrm{max}(\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{1},\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{2}),\ \textrm{min}(\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{3},\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{4}),\ \textrm{max}(\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{3},\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{4})\bigr\}\ ,

of which we show below the last list as a function of
z
.

Figure 10
:
Plot of the universal limiting natural frequency-squares

\tilde{\Omega}^{2}_{(j,v,\beta)}
for
v{=}j
and
\beta=1,2,3,4
.

The monodromies are easily computed numerically,
8
8

8

For the cases
(j,v)=(0,1)
and
(1,0)
one gets
\{56.769,\ -1.659\}
;
for
(j,v)=(0,0)
we have
\mathrm{tr}\,M=2
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,M_{(j,j\pm 2)}(E{\to}\infty)\ \ \ =2\ ,

(27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,M_{(j,j\pm 1,\beta)}(E{\to}\infty)\ \in\bigl\{306.704,\ -1.842,\ -1.659\bigr\}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,M_{(j,j,\beta)}(E{\to}\infty)\ \ \ \ \in\bigl\{306.704,\ -1.842,\ 2.462,\ -1.067\bigr\}\ ,

in agreement with the figures above. In particular, the extremal
v
-values become marginally stable,
while part of the non-extremal cases are unstable for high energies.

Of course, for each non-extremal value of
v
we still have to project out unphysical modes
by imposing the gauge condition (
37
). However, in the
12(2j{+}1)
-dimensional fluctuation space
the gauge condition has rank
3(2j{+}1)
while we see that (for
j{\geq}2
) in total
4(2j{+}1)
normal modes
are unstable at high energy. Therefore, the projection to physical modes cannot remove all instabilities.
We must conclude that, for sufficiently high energy
E
, some fluctuations grow exponentially,
implying that the solution
\Phi{\equiv}0
is linearly unstable, and thus is the Yang–Mills background.

4
Singlet perturbation: exact treatment

Even though the Floquet representation helped to reduce the long-time behavior of the perturbations
to the analysis of a single period
T
, it normally does not give us an exact solution to Hill’s equation.
However, for the SO(4) singlet fluctuation around
\psi(\tau)
, we can employ the fact that

\dot{\psi}
trivially solves the fluctuation equation,

(\dot{\psi})^{\cdot\cdot}=(\ddot{\psi})^{\cdot}=-\bigl(V^{\prime}(\psi)\bigr)^{\cdot}=-V^{\prime\prime}(\psi)\,\dot{\psi}\ =\ -(6\psi^{2}{-}2)\,\dot{\psi}\ =\ -\Omega^{2}_{(0,0)}(\tau)\,\dot{\psi}\ ,

(1)

with a frequency function which is
\frac{T}{2}
-periodic.
This implies that all fluctuation modes are
T
-periodic.
With the knowledge of an explicit solution to the fluctuation equation
we can reduce the latter to a first-order equation and solve that one to find a second solution.
The normalizations are arbitrary, so we choose

\Phi_{1}(\tau)\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{k}}\,\dot{\psi}(\tau)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\Phi_{2}(\tau)\ =\ \Phi_{1}(\tau)\,\int^{\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{\Phi_{1}(\sigma)^{2}}\ =\ -{\textstyle\frac{k}{\epsilon^{3}}}\,\dot{\psi}(\tau)\,\int^{\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{\dot{\psi}^{2}(\sigma)}\ ,

(2)

which are linearly independent since

W(\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})\ \equiv\ \Phi_{1}\dot{\Phi}_{2}-\Phi_{2}\dot{\Phi}_{1}\ =\ 1\ .

(3)

For simplicity, we restrict ourselves to the energy range

{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{<}E{<}\infty
, i.e.
1{>}k^{2}{>}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}
.
Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\!\!\!\Phi_{1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\ \epsilon\,{\mathrm{sn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k\bigr)}\,{\mathrm{dn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k\bigr)}\ ,

(4)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\Phi_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\ {\textstyle\frac{1}{1-k^{2}}}\,{\mathrm{cn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k\bigr)}\bigl[(2k^{2}{-}1)\,\textrm{dn}^{2}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k\bigr)-k^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle+{\mathrm{sn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k\bigr)}\,{\mathrm{dn}\bigl({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k\bigr)}\bigl[{\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}}+{\textstyle\frac{2k^{2}{-}1}{1{-}k^{2}}}\,E\bigl(\textrm{am}({\textstyle\frac{\tau}{\epsilon}},k),k\bigr)\bigr]\;,

where
\textrm{am}(z,k)
denotes the Jacobi amplitude and
E(z,k)
is the elliptic integral of the second kind.

Figure 11
:
Plot of the SO(4) singlet fluctuation modes
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
over eight periods for
k^{2}{=}0.81
.

As can be checked, the initial conditions are

\Phi_{1}(0)=0\ ,\quad\dot{\Phi}_{1}(0)=1\qquad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\qquad\Phi_{2}(0)=-1\ ,\quad\dot{\Phi}_{2}(0)=0\ ,

(5)

which fixes the ambiguity of adding to
\Phi_{2}
a piece proportional to
\Phi_{1}
. Hence,

\widehat{\Phi}(0)\ =\ \Bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\!{-}1\\[4.0pt]
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad M\ =\ \widehat{\Phi}(T)\,\Bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\[4.0pt]
{-}1&0\end{smallmatrix}\Bigr)\ .

(6)

We know that
\Phi_{1}\sim\dot{\psi}
is
T
-periodic, and so is
\dot{\Phi}_{1}
,
but not the second solution,

\Phi_{2}(\tau{+}T)\ =\ \Phi_{2}(\tau)+\gamma\,T\,\Phi_{1}(\tau)\quad\textrm{with}\quad\gamma\ =\ \frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\mathrm{d}\sigma}{\Phi_{1}(\sigma)^{2}}\bigg|_{\textrm{reg}}\ =:\ {\textstyle\frac{k^{2}}{\epsilon^{6}}}\,\bigl\langle\dot{\psi}^{-2}\bigr\rangle_{\textrm{reg}}\ ,

(7)

where the integral diverges at the turning points and must be regularized by subtracting
the Weierstraß
\wp
function with the appropriate half-periods.
Since
\Phi_{1}
has periodic zeros,
\Phi_{2}
does return to
{-}1
at integer multiples of
T
.
It follows that the
\Phi_{2}
oscillation linearly grows in amplitude with a rate (per period) of

\gamma\ =\ \frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\,\Bigl[\,1+\frac{2k^{2}{-}1}{1{-}k^{2}}\,\frac{E(k)}{K(k)}\,\Bigr]\ ,

(8)

which is always larger than 7.629, attained at
k\approx 0.882
.

Figure 12
:
Plot of the linear growth rate
\gamma
as a function of
k
.

In essence, we have managed to compute the monodromy

M\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}{-}\Phi_{2}(T)&\Phi_{1}(T)\\[4.0pt]
{-}\dot{\Phi}_{2}(T)&\dot{\Phi}_{1}(T)\ \end{pmatrix}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}\ 1&0\\[4.0pt]
\ \!\!{-}\gamma\,T&1\ \end{pmatrix}\ =\ \exp\Bigl\{{-}\gamma\,T\,\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\[4.0pt]
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\Bigr\}

(9)

and thus easily obtain the Floquet representation,

R\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\[4.0pt]
0&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathrm{e}^{\tau R}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}1&\gamma\,\tau\\[4.0pt]
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\textrm{and}\quad Q(\tau)\ =\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{1}&\Phi_{2}{-}\Phi_{1}\gamma\,\tau\\[4.0pt]
\dot{\Phi}_{1}&\dot{\Phi}_{2}{-}\dot{\Phi}_{1}\gamma\,\tau\end{pmatrix}\ .

(10)

Obviously, we have encountered a marginally stable situation, since
M
is of parabolic type.
There is no exponential growth, and
\Phi_{1}
is periodic thus bounded, but
\Phi_{2}

grows without bound as long as one stays in the linear regime.
Note that we never made use of the form of our Newtonian potential.
In fact, this behavior is typical for a conservative mechanical system with oscillatory motion.

What to make of this linear growth? It can be (and actually is) easily overturned by nonlinear effects.
Going beyond the linear regime, though, requires expanding the Yang–Mills equation to higher orders
about our classical Yang–Mills solution (
10
).
While this is a formidable task in general, it can actually be done to all orders for the singlet perturbation!
The reason is that a singlet perturbation leaves us in the SO(4)-symmetric subsector,
thus connecting only to a neighboring “cosmic background”,
\psi\to\tilde{\psi}
.
Since (
4
) gives us analytic control over all solutions
\psi(\tau)
,
the full effect of such a shift can be computed exactly.
Splitting an exact solution
\tilde{\psi}
into a background part and its (full) deviation,

\tilde{\psi}(\tau)\ =\ \psi(\tau)\ +\ \eta(\tau)\ ,

(11)

and inserting
\tilde{\psi}
into the equation of motion (
2
), we obtain

0\ =\ \ddot{\eta}+V^{\prime\prime}(\psi)\,\eta+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}V^{\prime\prime\prime}(\psi)\,\eta^{2}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{6}}V^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(\psi)\,\eta^{3}\ =\ \ddot{\eta}+(6\psi^{2}{-}2)\,\eta+6\psi\,\eta^{2}+2\,\eta^{3}\ ,

(12)

extending the linear equation (
1
) by two nonlinear contributions.
Perturbation theory introduces a small parameter
\epsilon
and formally expands

\eta\ =\ \epsilon\eta_{(1)}+\epsilon^{2}\eta_{(2)}+\epsilon^{3}\eta_{(3)}+\ldots\ ,

(13)

which yields the infinite coupled system

\displaystyle\bigl[\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}^{2}+(6\psi^{2}{-}2)\bigr]\,\eta_{(1)}\ =\ 0\ ,

(14)

\displaystyle\bigl[\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}^{2}+(6\psi^{2}{-}2)\bigr]\,\eta_{(2)}\ =\ -6\psi\,\eta_{(1)}^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\bigl[\mbox{$\partial$}_{\tau}^{2}+(6\psi^{2}{-}2)\bigr]\,\eta_{(3)}\ =\ -12\psi\,\eta_{(1)}\eta_{(2)}-2\,\eta_{(1)}^{3}\ ,

\displaystyle\ldots\ ,

which could be iterated with a seed solution
\eta_{(1)}
of the linear system.

However, we know that the exact solutions to the full nonlinear equation (
12
)
is simply given by the difference

\eta(\tau)\ =\ \tilde{\psi}(\tau)-\psi(\tau)

(15)

of two analytically known backgrounds. The SO(4)-singlet background moduli space is parametrized by
two coordinates, e.g. the energy
E
(or elliptic modulus
k
) and the choice of an initial condition
which fixes the origin
\tau{=}0
of the time variable. In (
4
), we selected
\dot{\psi}(0)=0
,
but relaxing this we can reintroduce this collective coordinate by allowing shifts in
\tau
.
We may then parametrize the SO(4)-invariant Yang–Mills solutions as

\psi_{k,\ell}(\tau)\ =\ \psi(\tau{-}\ell)\qquad\textrm{with}\qquad 2E=1/(2k^{2}{-}1)^{2}\quad\quad\textrm{and}\quad\quad\ell\in\mathds{R}

(16)

where
\psi
is taken from (
4
).
Note that
\dot{\psi}_{k,\ell}
solves the background equation (
1
) with a frequency-squared

\omega_{k,\ell}^{2}=6\psi_{k,\ell}^{2}{-}2
.
Without loss of generality we assign
\psi=\psi_{k,0}
and
\tilde{\psi}=\psi_{k{+}\delta k,\delta\ell}
,
hence

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)

\displaystyle=\ \delta k\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{k}\psi(\tau)-\delta\ell\,\dot{\psi}(\tau)+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\delta k)^{2}\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{k}^{2}\psi(\tau)-\delta k\delta\ell\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{k}\dot{\psi}(\tau)+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\delta\ell)^{2}\,\ddot{\psi}(\tau)+\ldots

(17)

\displaystyle=\ \delta k\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{k}\psi(\tau{-}\delta\ell)+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\delta k)^{2}\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{k}^{2}\psi(\tau{-}\delta\ell)+{\textstyle\frac{1}{6}}(\delta k)^{3}\,\mbox{$\partial$}_{k}^{3}\psi(\tau{-}\delta\ell)+\ldots\ ,

because
\mbox{$\partial$}_{\ell}\psi=-\dot{\psi}
.
Clearly, a shift in
\ell
only shifts the time dependence of the frequency and does not alter the energy
E
,
which is not very interesting. Its linear part corresponds to the mode
\Phi_{1}\sim\dot{\psi}
of the previous section.
A change in
k
, in contract, will lead to a solution with an altered frequency and energy.
Its linear part is given by
\Phi_{2}
, which grows linearly in time.
However, due to the boundedness of the full motion, the nonlinear corrections have to limit this growth and
ultimately must bring the fluctuation back close to zero. This is the familiar wave beat phenomenon:
the difference of two oscillating functions,
\tilde{\psi}
and
\psi
, with slightly different frequencies,
will display an amplitude oscillation with a beat frequency given by the difference.
This is borne out in the following plots.

Figure 13
:
Plots of the full perturbation
\eta
at
k{=}0.95
for
\eta(0){=}0.02
,
\dot{\eta}(0){=}0
, giving a beat ratio of
{\sim}19
.

As a result, we can assert a long-term stability of the cosmic Yang–Mills fields
against the SO(4) singlet perturbation, even though on shorter time scales an excursion
to a nearby solution is not met with a linear backreaction.

Chapter 6
Conclusion & Outlook

We have studied stability behaviour of some well known solutions of
SU(2)
Yang–Mills fields in
4
-dimensional de Sitter space
\mathrm{d}{S}_{4}
under generic gauge perturbation. These solutions could be of relevance to early time cosmology (before the electro-weak symmetry breaks down) in a scenario recently presented by Friedan
[
10
]
. An
SO(4)
symmetric sector is analytically solvable and reduces to three coupled anharmonic oscillators (for the metric, an
SU(2)
Yang–Mills field and the Higgs field, the latter being frozen to its vacuum state). We have presented a complete analysis of the linear gauge-field perturbations of the time-dependent Yang–Mills solution, by diagonalizing the fluctuation operator and studying the long-time behavior of the ensuing Hill’s equations using the stroboscopic map and Floquet theory. For parametrically large gauge-field energy (as is required in Friedan’s setup) the natural frequencies and monodromies become universal, and some unstable perturbation modes survive even in this limit. This provides strong evidence that such oscillating cosmic Yang–Mills fields are unstable against small perturbations, although we have not yet included metric fluctuations here. Their influence will be analyzed in
follow-up work.

We have also analyzed a family of electromagenetic knot configurations recently developed by making use of a conformal equivalence between
\mathrm{d}{S}_{4}
, Minkwoski space
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
and a finite Lorentzian
S^{3}
-cylinder. These solutions are constructed on the cylinder in an
SO(4)
covariant way and are then pulled back to the Minkowski space using the conformal map (that leaves the Maxwell’s theory invariant). These “basis-knot" solutions of Maxwell’s equation are labelled with
S^{3}
harmonics
Y_{j,m,n}
and give rise to field configurations of knotted field lines when pulled back to the Minkowski space. We have analyzed the symmetry feature of these basis knotted electromagnetic field configurations with the isometry group
SO(1,4)
of the de Sitter space. We have further studied, numerically, the effect of these basis configurations on the trajectories of multiple identical charged particles with different initial conditions. Various behaviors were obtained, including a separation of trajectories into different “solid angle regions” that converge asymptotically into a beam of charged particles along a few particular regions of space, an ultrarelativistic acceleration of particles and coherent twists/turns of the trajectories before they go off asymptotically. The results contribute to an effort to better understand the interactions between electromagnetic knots and charged particles
[
36
]
. This becomes increasingly relevant as laboratory generation of knotted
fields progresses
[
5
]
. Further work in this direction could be to analyze a single Fourier mode of these
solutions to understand its experimental realization via monochromatic laser beams.

Furthermore, we have considered complex linear combinations of these basis-knot field configurations (that can model any finite-energy field configuration) and characterized the corresponding moduli space of null fields. We have also computed Noether charges of such a linearly combined configuration with fixed
j
for the conformal group
SO(2,4)
, which is the largest symmetry group for the Maxwell theory. Here again the “de Sitter" method proved to be advantageous in that the expressions of these charge densities simplifies immensely on the cylinder and, thus, can be computed with ease. We found that many of these charges vanish owing to the orthogonality of the harmonics and that the energy and momentum are the only independent charged in many cases. A nice geometric structure of
1
-forms facilitated the computation of spherical components of vector charges as well. We verified our results against the results for some modified Hopf–Ranãda field configurations of
[
14
]
. We would like to further check the validity of our results by comparing them with other solutions presented in
[
14
]
.

Chapter 7
Carter-Penrose transformation

The metric on the Minkowski space
\mathds{R}^{1,3}
in polar coordinates is given by

\mathrm{d}s_{Mink}^{2}\ =\ -\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\ ;\qquad\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\ =\ \mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\phi^{2},

(1)

where
-\infty<t<\infty,\ 0\leq r<\infty,\ 0\leq\theta\leq\pi
, and
0\leq\phi\leq 2\pi
. We first employ the light-cone coordinates
(u,v)
to transform the metric in the following way

\displaystyle u\ :=\ t-r\ ,\quad v\ :=\ t+r\ ;\quad-\infty<u\leq v<\infty\

(2)

\displaystyle\implies\mathrm{d}s_{Mink}^{2}\ =\ -\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}u+{\textstyle\frac{1}{4}}(v-u)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\ .

In the second step we compactify the spacetime with the help of the coordinate (U,V) as follows:

\displaystyle U\ :=\ \arctan u\ ,\quad V\ :=\ \arctan v\ ;\quad-{\textstyle\frac{\pi}{2}}<U\leq V<{\textstyle\frac{\pi}{2}}\

(3)

\displaystyle\implies\mathrm{d}s_{Mink}^{2}\ =\ \frac{1}{4\cos^{2}V\cos^{2}U}\left[-4\,\mathrm{d}V\,\mathrm{d}U+\sin^{2}(V-U)\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\right]\ .

Finally, we rotate the coordinate system back using
(\tau,\chi)
to obtain the desired form of the metric:

\displaystyle\tau\ :=\ V+U\ ,\quad\chi\ :=\ \pi+U-V\ ;\quad 0<\chi\leq\pi\ ,|\tau|<\chi\

(4)

\displaystyle\implies\mathrm{d}s_{Mink}^{2}\ =\ \gamma^{-2}\left[-\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}+\mathrm{d}\chi^{2}+\sin^{2}\chi\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}\right]\ ;\quad\gamma\ =\ \cos\tau-\cos\chi\ .

We realize that the Minkowski metric is conformally equivalent to a Lorentzian cylinder
\mathcal{I}\times S^{3}\ ;\ \mathcal{I}=(-\pi,\pi)
with a conformal factor that can be recasted in terms of Minkowski coordinates using the above transformations (
2
-
4
):

\mathrm{d}s_{cyl}^{2}\ :=\ -\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}+\mathrm{d}\Omega_{3}^{2}\ =\ \gamma^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s_{Mink}^{2}\ ;\quad\gamma=\frac{2\ell^{2}}{\sqrt{4\,t^{2}\,\ell^{2}+(r^{2}-t^{2}+\ell^{2})^{2}}}\ ,

(5)

where we have made
\gamma
dimensionless using the de Sitter radius
\ell
. The lightcone structure of the spacetime as presented in the Penrose diagram (see Figure
2
) is a direct consequence of (
4
).

Chapter 8
Rotation of indices

{\cal A}
is
SO(4)
invariant, which means it is also invariant under the action of

generators
\mathcal{D}_{a}
(

). For a complex-valued

expanded using (

) (for type I) and (

this means
